    decomposition of the gap rather than a case. Nothing outside the
    worker’s own problem is keeping him off the corner: part (iii)
    records that nothing in Assumptions 1–5 forces
    $\tau^\ast\le\bar\tau$, and at the reported cells the ceiling is
    slack, so what stops him is the price his own condition puts on the
    capital his tax reduces. That price is not a foreign object either —
    by (i) it is the public wedge read on the other stock, $\Omega$ with
    $\delta$ in place of $\delta_G$ — so the two forces in the last
    paragraph are one formula evaluated at the two depreciation rates.
    And the pair recovers the corner’s own policy: at $\theta=1$ — a
    limit taken on the expression, $\theta=1$ being outside Proposition
    3’s range — the bracket in (6) is $-\tau\alpha/\kappa_W<0$ at every
    split, so the condition drives $\phi$ to zero, predicting the pure
    transfer rather than assuming it. Proposition 2 established the
    nesting for the steady state; the same restriction now also recovers
    the corner’s policy. The reinvestment rate $i$ is an input to
    (7)–(9), which are therefore the worker’s pair of stationary
    conditions and not yet the equilibrium. Closing it needs the
    capitalist’s envelope, and under (O) that envelope runs over the
    worker’s own three states.

A convention, once, because two classes could in principle discount at
two rates. **From here to the end of Section 8 the two curvatures are
taken equal**, $\sigma_W=\sigma_C=\sigma$, so that
$\tilde\beta=\beta g^{1-\sigma}$ is one number and the wedges
$$\Omega=\frac{\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta_G)}{g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)},\qquad
  D_x=g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta)-\tilde\beta\alpha(g-1+\delta),\qquad
  J=\frac{\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)}{D_x}$$ are one family rather than
two. This is a restriction for readability and not a requirement of the
model: Proposition A.1 proves the state reduction *per player*, at each
class own exponent, so every result below holds verbatim with
$\tilde\beta_j=\beta g^{1-\sigma_j}$ carried through and each object
read at the exponent of the class whose condition it appears in. Nothing
would change but the subscripts, and Appendix B keeps them. The one
place the distinction does real work is Assumption 5, whose two clauses
differ off a rest point whatever the curvatures, because the capitalist
payoff object grows at the worker factor while his own reproduction rate
does not enter it at all.

Under (O) the capitalist’s value function is $V_C(K,G,N_W)$ — the
worker’s own state list, with $N_C$ gone. That is the first consequence
of the change of objective and it removes a state from the game: $N_C$
enters neither output, nor either stock law, nor the worker’s
reproduction (Corollary A.1), and now it enters nothing the capitalist
values either. Two further differences follow from (O) and both are
visible in one place. His flow payoff carries $K$ and $N_W$, where the
dynastic one carried neither, so the capital and workforce envelopes
each acquire a flow term while the stock envelope does not — $G$ is
absent from his felicity, which is the class asymmetry again and is why
(B.3) is the worker’s verbatim. The elasticities of that payoff are
$$\frac{\partial \ln P}{\partial \ln K} \;=\; \zeta \;\equiv\; \alpha+\omega(1-\alpha)
\;\in\;[\alpha,1),
\qquad
\frac{\partial \ln P}{\partial \ln N_W} \;=\; (1-\omega)(1-\alpha),
\qquad
\frac{\partial \ln P}{\partial \ln G} \;=\; 0,$$ which sum to one —
Euler on a degree-one aggregate — and leave the public stock outside his
felicity, so the paper’s class asymmetry survives the change intact. The
symbol $\zeta$ is new and is used below only in the capitalist’s
envelopes; it is distinct from the stock poles $\mu$ and $\mu_K$ of
Section 8, which are functions of the depreciation rates and carry no
preference parameter.

Write $\varphi \equiv P^{1-\sigma}>0$ for the scale of his flow marginal
value. The rate reaches $K'$ by $(1-\tau)\alpha Y$ and his own
consumption by $-1/(1-i)$, and reaches nothing else, so the condition
again has two terms:
$$\underbrace{\tilde\beta\,\frac{a_K\,(g-1+\delta)}{g\,i}}_{\text{benefit}}
\;=\;\underbrace{\frac{(1-\omega)\,\varphi}{1-i}}_{\text{cost}} . \tag{10}$$
Compare the dynastic condition it replaces,
$a_K/a_{N_C}=i\gamma g/[(1-i)(g-1+\delta)]$: the benefit is unchanged
and the cost has changed hands, from the dilution of his dynasty to the
loss of his own consumption. The factor $1-\omega$ in that cost is the
whole of what the weight does, and it is why $\omega=1$ is excluded.

Two of the three envelopes are worth reading. The stock envelope is the
worker’s verbatim — the same two terms for either class, each read at
its own exponent, so that $\Omega_C$ stands where $\Omega$ does under
class-indexing — because public infrastructure still reaches the
capitalist only through the workforce. The capital envelope is the
worker’s plus a single term:
$$a_K D_x \;=\; \tilde\beta\alpha\gamma g\,a_{N_W}\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)
   \;+\; g\,\zeta\,\varphi, \tag{11}$$ the bare $g\zeta\varphi$ being
capital’s direct claim on his felicity: capital feeds the workforce that
produces *and* enters his payoff on its own account, where for the
worker it did only the first. Delete that term and the worker’s envelope
returns exactly. This is the same slot the own-dynasty term $a_{N_C}$
occupied under the dynastic objective, and it is occupied now by a
different tenant — which is why the results built on (11) change their
reading without changing their form.

The remaining envelope is linear in $(a_K,a_{N_W})$ given $\varphi$, so
write $$\rho \;\equiv\; \frac{a_{N_W}}{\varphi}$$ for the capitalist’s
marginal valuation of the workforce, measured against the scale of his
own flow payoff. Under (O) he values owned capital and not worker
numbers: $N_W$ is absent from his felicity. It is not absent from his
output. Labour enters production at the elasticity $1-\alpha$ and is
paid its marginal product (Assumption 1), and his flow payoff reaches
$N_W$ through that channel alone, with elasticity
$(1-\omega)(1-\alpha)$. His $\ln N_W$ envelope row is therefore
inhomogeneous where the worker’s was closed against an own-dynasty term,
and what it returns is not zero.

**Proposition 4$'$ (the owner prices the workforce, and the price is
strictly positive).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$
and $\omega\in[0,1)$, and let the profile be admissible at a
constant-share rest point, so that Assumption 5’s capitalist clause
reads $\tilde\beta_C<1$. Then the capitalist’s marginal values of the
workforce, of the public stock and of capital are all strictly
positive,* $$a_{N_W}>0, \qquad a_G>0, \qquad a_K>0,$$ *hence $\rho>0$
and $\Lambda>0$, and Proposition 5’s zero $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$
is interior with no further hypothesis. The valuation is closed form:
$\rho$ solves the scalar envelope equation (B.7), whose right-hand side
is $(1-\alpha)\big[(1-\omega)+J\zeta\big]$ and whose left-hand
coefficient is strictly positive under properness.*

*Proof.* Lemma B.2, with (B.7)–(B.7$''$) supplying the closed form and
the sign of the coefficient. $\blacksquare$

The right-hand side is worth reading term by term, because it is the
whole of the economics. It is the labour elasticity $1-\alpha$
multiplied by the two routes along which an additional worker reaches
the owner’s payoff: the consumption flow that worker’s product finances,
weighted $1-\omega$, and the capital that same product helps accumulate,
weighted $J\zeta$. Neither route is a preference over people. Both are
the production function and the competitive wage. An owner who is
indifferent to how many workers exist, and who would not pay a penny to
have more, therefore carries a strictly positive shadow price on the
workforce that produces his output.

In words, that price raises the rate at which he ploughs profit back. It
is not what makes him plough anything back at all: setting the workforce
term in (12) to zero with the remaining terms held fixed leaves
$\Lambda=J\zeta/(1-\omega)>0$, so capital’s direct claim on his felicity
would sustain an interior rate on its own. What the workforce valuation
adds is the second channel, the one running through the labour that
produces the output — and it adds it with a strictly positive weight at
every admissible constant-share rest point.

**So what.** The persistence of the working class is not a constraint
imposed on the owning class from outside the model, and it is not a
taste written into its objective. It is a shadow price that the owner’s
own optimisation returns, strictly positive under properness, and it
enters his accumulation rule directly through (12). The two classes
contest the tax, the split and the rate; underneath that contest, the
owner’s problem prices the reproduction of the class he is contesting
with, and labour’s share of output is the factor that scales that price.
Nothing in the objective put it there. The technology did.

With $\rho$ in hand the rate is explicit:
$$i^\ast \;=\; \frac{\Lambda}{1+\Lambda}, \qquad
  \Lambda \;=\; \frac{J}{1-\omega}\Big[\,\zeta
    \;+\; \tilde\beta\,\alpha\gamma\,\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\,\rho\,\Big]. \tag{12}$$
At $\omega=0$, where $\zeta=\alpha$, this is
$\Lambda=J\alpha\big[\,r(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega)+1\,\big]$ with
$r=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$ — the dynastic paper’s own expression, with
the workforce valuation normalised by a flow scale instead of by an
own-dynasty marginal value. The rate condition keeps its algebraic shape
across the change of objective; what changes is what stands in the
denominator of the capitalist’s valuation of the workers.

**Proposition 5 (the reinvestment margin exists exactly for $\omega<1$,
single-crosses, and its zero is interior).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold
and fix $a_K>0$, $\varphi>0$, $g$ and $\delta$ with $g-1+\delta>0$
(Assumption 4). Define on $i\in(0,1)$ the marginal benefit and cost of
(10),*
$$\mathrm{Ben}(i) \;=\; \frac{\tilde\beta\,a_K\,(g-1+\delta)}{g\,i}, \qquad
  \mathrm{Cost}(i) \;=\; \frac{(1-\omega)\,\varphi}{1-i}.$$

*(i) **The upper endpoint of Assumption 3 is a degeneracy, not a
convention.** At $\omega=1$ the cost vanishes identically and
$\partial V_C/\partial i>0$ at every admissible $i$, so the capitalist’s
problem has no first-order condition and $i^\ast=1$ unconditionally. The
same conclusion follows without any envelope: at $\omega=1$ his payoff
object is $K$ alone, the transition in $(K,G,N_W)$ is order-preserving
in $\iota=i(1-\tau)$ — the step Proposition 14’s proof reads off the
transition laws, which needs only that every partial be nonnegative and
that $m=\gamma[\theta\alpha+(1-\theta)]<1$, both from Assumptions 1–2,
so it survives at $\theta=1$ where $m=\gamma\alpha$ and Proposition 14’s
own statement does not reach, its wedge $\Omega^{\rm fix}$ dividing by
$1-\theta$ — and raising the rate raises every date’s capital stock
componentwise with nothing to weigh against it. Both routes give the
corner, and every result below that has $i^\ast$ as its subject is
vacuous there.*

*(ii) **For $\omega<1$ the margin exists and crosses once, from above.**
$\mathrm{Ben}-\mathrm{Cost}$ is strictly decreasing on $(0,1)$, diverges
to $+\infty$ as $i\to0^{+}$ and to $-\infty$ as $i\to1^{-}$. Hence it
has exactly one zero, it crosses from above, and the zero is interior:
the one-shot marginal value is signed away from both extremes. At a
constant-share rest point with $D_x\neq0$, so that $J$ and hence
$\Lambda$ of (12) are defined, that zero is
$i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$, which lies in $(0,1)$ for every
$\Lambda>0$.*

*(iii) **The exclusion of the upper corner is uniform on closed
subintervals.** The coefficient of the divergence in (ii) is $1-\omega$
itself, so on $\omega\le1-\varepsilon$ it is bounded below by
$\varepsilon>0$: the degeneracy of (i) is a boundary phenomenon and not
a knife edge inside the admissible range.*

*Proof.* **(i)** With $\omega=1$, $\mathrm{Cost}\equiv0$ while
$\mathrm{Ben}>0$ under the stated hypotheses, so the difference is
strictly positive on $(0,1)$ and has no zero. For the envelope-free
route, $\zeta=1$ at $\omega=1$ makes $P=K$; Proposition 14’s proof shows
the transition map in $(K,G,N_W)$ is order-preserving and that $\iota$
enters it monotonically upward, so $K_t$ is non-decreasing in $i$ at
every $t$ and strictly increasing for $t\ge1$. Under (O) with $\omega=1$
nothing enters his payoff that $i$ lowers, so $V_C$ is strictly
increasing in $i$. The monotone step reaches the capitalist here
precisely because it is his own consumption that it failed to reach
before.

**(ii)** $\mathrm{Ben}'(i)=-\tilde\beta a_K(g-1+\delta)/(g\,i^{2})<0$
because $a_K>0$, $g>0$ and $g-1+\delta>0$ by Assumption 4;
$\mathrm{Cost}'(i)=(1-\omega)\varphi/(1-i)^{2}>0$ because $\varphi>0$
and $\omega<1$. Their difference is therefore strictly decreasing, so it
has at most one zero and can only cross from above. As $i\to0^{+}$,
$\mathrm{Ben}\to+\infty$ while $\mathrm{Cost}\to(1-\omega)\varphi$ is
bounded, so the difference tends to $+\infty$; as $i\to1^{-}$,
$\mathrm{Cost}\to+\infty$ while
$\mathrm{Ben}\to\tilde\beta a_K(g-1+\delta)/g$ is bounded, so the
difference tends to $-\infty$. By the intermediate value theorem a zero
exists, and by strict monotonicity it is unique and interior. Setting
$\mathrm{Ben}=\mathrm{Cost}$ is exactly (10); writing its solution as
$i/(1-i)=\Lambda$ and solving gives $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$, which
lies strictly between $0$ and $1$ whenever $\Lambda>0$ because then
$0<\Lambda<1+\Lambda$. **(iii)** is immediate from the displayed cost,
whose coefficient is $1-\omega$. $\blacksquare$

Part (i) is worth separating from the rest, because it disposes of the
objective a reader arrives with. Replacing the capitalist’s dynastic
payoff by discounted capital or after-tax wealth is the
economical-looking repair — it removes the implausible symmetry and
leaves the state reduction, the funding structure and the envelope
method standing — and it is exactly $\omega=1$. What it also removes is
the trade-off: with nothing in his payoff that reinvestment lowers,
Proposition 14’s order-preserving argument reaches him, and reaches him
with a corner rather than a criterion. Everything in this paper whose
subject is $i^\ast$ — this proposition, Proposition 10, Proposition 12’s
rate column, Theorem 6 — is then vacuous rather than rebuildable. The
opposite repair fails symmetrically: an objective in the profit rate
$\alpha Y/K$ is decreasing in the stock it accumulates, so it drives
$i^\ast$ to zero, and it is homogeneous of degree zero, which destroys
the reduction outright. The admissible replacements are the ones in
which reinvesting still costs him something he values, and (O) is the
one-parameter family of them that keeps both the homogeneity and the
log-affineness the rest of the paper uses.

Proposition 5 is a statement about the one-shot marginal value of (10)
at a fixed balanced-growth factor, not about the dynamic best response:
$a_K$, $\varphi$ and $g$ are held fixed and all three move with $i$.
That the sign of this marginal value gives the direction of travel of
the constant-share best response is Theorem 4, proved at a start equal
to the induced rest point; what this proposition delivers on its own is
the crossing, and the bridge from it to the payoff is Section 8.4’s. The
proposition takes $a_K>0$ and $\varphi>0$ as hypotheses, and its part
(ii) adds $\Lambda>0$; $\varphi>0$ is immediate from $\sigma>1$, and
Lemma B.2 discharges the other two from Assumptions 1–4 and properness:
at a constant-share rest point the capitalist’s three marginal values
are all strictly positive, which signs $\rho$ and hence $\Lambda$, so
the interiority is a consequence and not a parameter restriction. The
crossing is established exactly as $\Psi(\phi)$’s is in Proposition 3,
and again without any curvature hypothesis: no concavity of $V_C$ in $i$
is used or claimed. What the two proofs share is that each margin is a
difference of a decreasing and an increasing function, which is a
structural fact about where the instrument enters rather than a
regularity assumption.

The optimum is interior, and that is the contrast with the split. The
benefit diverges as $i\to0$ and the cost as $i\to1$, so neither extreme
can zero the one-shot margin; and at the reported cells the marginal
value is positive at the low edge of the admissible range and negative
at the high edge, so the rate settles away from the admissibility
boundary as well. Where $\phi$ sits at its corner in nine of the ten
decidable cells, $i$ is interior in all ten. The two facts carry unequal
weight: the admissible range reaches $i=0$ or $i=1$ at only one cell, so
at the rest it is admissibility rather than the divergence that keeps
the rate away from the extremes.

The two sides share the wedges but not the structure. The transition
wedge and the capital wedge govern both conditions — the same two
objects that price the worker’s transition price the capitalist’s, at
his own discount factor. But (12) carries a third, $\rho$, for which the
worker’s condition has no counterpart: the worker’s own dynasty *is* the
numerator of his payoff, so he never has to price the other class,
whereas the capitalist must price the workforce that produces for him.
The capital envelope differs from the worker’s by exactly the one term
in (11); the condition differs by that term and by $\rho$.

That the capitalist must price the workforce is not an artefact of the
old objective, and it is worth saying why, since $N_C$ has left his
problem and one might expect the cross-class term to leave with it. It
does not, because it never came from his dynasty. It comes from
production: the workforce is the complementary factor in
$Y=AK^{\alpha}N_W^{1-\alpha}$, so more workers raise his output, his
consumption and the stock he accumulates alike. Under the dynastic
objective that channel was overlaid by a second one — more workers meant
more output meant more of his own numbers — and the two were hard to
separate. Under (O) only the production channel survives, and $\rho$
measures it cleanly. The class-asymmetric term in the capitalist’s
condition is therefore a *technological* fact about who produces, not a
demographic one about who reproduces.

The asymmetry is testable, because it breaks the $\gamma$-inertia of the
tax. The tax is inert for the reason Proposition 4(ii) gives; $\rho$, by
contrast, carries $\gamma$ explicitly, so $i^\ast$ should not be inert,
and at the reported cells it is not: sweeping $\gamma$ across
$[0.30,0.70]$ moves $\tau^\ast$ by three tenths of a grid atom and
$i^\ast$ by five, while $\rho$ falls from $6.22$ to $3.26$ — a span of
two thirds of its own mean — against $\Omega$ moving $0.0036$ and $J$
moving $0.0124$. The two wedges are, in relative terms, all but inert in
$\gamma$ — under two per cent of their own level against $\rho$’s
sixty-two. The movement in the rate runs through the capitalist’s
pricing of the class that produces for him, an object with no
worker-side twin.

It does not run through $\rho$ in the direction the reader of (12) will
supply unaided, and the next two results are worth reading together for
that reason: $\rho$ *falls* across the sweep while $i^\ast$ rises. What
(12) contains is not $\rho$ but the product $\gamma\rho$ — equivalently
the $r=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$ of the $\omega=0$ reading, which rises
from $1.78$ to $2.17$ — and $\gamma$ stands in that product in its own
right as well as inside $\rho$.

That reading is a sweep, and neither direction it reports needs one.

**Proposition 5$'$ (the rate is strictly increasing in the valuation of
the workforce).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\omega<1$, at a
constant-share rest point at which $J$ and $\Omega$ are defined, with
$\Omega>0$ by Proposition 3(i) and $J>0$ under the same properness
hypothesis. Then in (12), holding $(J,\Omega,\zeta,\omega)$ fixed,*
$$\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\rho}
  \;=\; \frac{J\,\tilde\beta\alpha\gamma\,\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]}
             {(1-\omega)\,\big(1+\Lambda\big)^{2}} \;>\; 0 .$$

*Proof.* By (12) $\Lambda$ is affine in $\rho$ with slope
$J\tilde\beta\alpha\gamma[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]/(1-\omega)$. Every
factor is strictly positive: $\tilde\beta,\alpha,\gamma>0$ by
Assumptions 1–2, $\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega>0$ because $\theta\in(0,1)$
and $\Omega>0$, and $1-\omega>0$ by hypothesis. Proposition 5(ii) gives
$i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$, whose derivative in $\Lambda$ is
$(1+\Lambda)^{-2}>0$. The chain rule gives the display. $\blacksquare$

The economic content is the one the envelope already carried: capital’s
own accumulation rate rises with the price it puts on the workforce it
must reproduce. The derivative is a partial, taken with the two
transition wedges and the weight held fixed, and it must not be read as
a comparative static in a primitive: $\rho$, $J$ and $\Omega$ are each
closed forms in the primitives, so a parameter change that raises $\rho$
moves the others too. For $\gamma$ that total is settled by the next
proposition, and it is settled against the chain a reader would
otherwise complete.

**Proposition 5$''$ (in the reproduction elasticity the valuation falls
and the rate rises).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\omega<1$ at an
**admissible** constant-share rest point — admissible in Assumption 5’s
sense, so that $\tilde\beta<1$, which is what signs $\mathcal{W}$ and
$\eta$ below — and hold the balanced-growth factor $g$ fixed — which by
Proposition 4(ii) is the only channel by which $\gamma$ reaches $J$ or
$\Omega$. By $(\mathrm{B}.7'')$ write
$\mathcal{M}=(1-\tilde\beta)+\gamma\mathcal{W}$ with*
$$\mathcal{W}=\tilde\beta\Big[(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+\alpha\eta\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\Big]>0,
\qquad \eta=\frac{g(1-\tilde\beta)}{D_x}=1-(1-\alpha)J\in(0,1) \ \text{ by } (\mathrm{B}.7').$$
*Then, writing $\mathcal{Q}\equiv(1-\omega)+J\zeta>0$ for the
$\gamma$-free bracket that $(\mathrm{B}.7)$ leaves on the right,*
$$\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial\gamma}
   \;=\;-\,\frac{(1-\alpha)\,\mathcal{Q}\,\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{M}^{2}}\;<\;0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial(\gamma\rho)}{\partial\gamma}
   \;=\;\frac{(1-\alpha)\,\mathcal{Q}\,(1-\tilde\beta)}{\mathcal{M}^{2}}\;>\;0,
\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad
\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\gamma}\;>\;0 .$$

*Proof.* $(\mathrm{B}.7)$ reads $\mathcal{M}\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}$,
and neither $\mathcal{Q}$ nor $1-\alpha$ contains $\gamma$.
$(\mathrm{B}.7'')$ writes $\mathcal{M}$ as
$(1-\tilde\beta)+\gamma\mathcal{W}$, and $\mathcal{W}>0$ because
$\Omega\in(0,1)$ by Proposition 3(i), $\eta\in(0,1)$ by
$(\mathrm{B}.7')$ under properness, and $\theta\in(0,1)$.
Differentiating $\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{M}$ gives the first
display. For the second,
$\gamma\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}\,\gamma/\big[(1-\tilde\beta)+\gamma\mathcal{W}\big]$,
whose derivative is
$(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}(1-\tilde\beta)/\mathcal{M}^{2}$: the two
$\gamma\mathcal{W}$ terms cancel and what is left standing in the
numerator is properness itself. In (12) $\gamma$ and $\rho$ occur only
through the product $\gamma\rho$, so $\Lambda$ is affine and strictly
increasing in it, and Proposition 5(ii)’s $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$
is a strictly increasing transform of $\Lambda$. $\blacksquare$

Three readings, and the first is why the signs oppose. $\gamma$ reaches
$\rho$ only through $\mathcal{M}$, the net own-feedback of the $\ln N_W$
row: a workforce that reproduces more elastically is cheaper to hold at
the margin, so its shadow price falls — while the elasticity with which
that price reaches the capital envelope rises faster than the price
falls. Marginal value down, total claim up, and it is the total claim
that (12) contains. Second, properness is not hygiene here either:
$1-\tilde\beta$ is the entire numerator of the second display, and at
$\tilde\beta>1$ the rate would fall in $\gamma$ instead. And the
intercept that numerator comes from is not an artifact of Assumption 2 —
Appendix F.1 obtains the same affine structure about the same
$\gamma$-free intercept $1-\tilde\beta$ for an arbitrary composite; what
is established here under Assumption 2 is the sign of the slope. Third,
the scope, which is the honest one: $g$ is held fixed, and $g$ is itself
a fixed point that $\gamma$ moves. That indirect channel is the one
Appendix B.9 declares open. It is a product of two factors, and the next
proposition signs the one that belongs to (12) — the rate falls in the
growth factor — leaving $\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}\gamma$ itself, which is
a property of the outer fixed point and not of the rate condition, and
which Proposition 6$'$ signs there once the share loop is closed.
Freezing $r$ at its midpoint across the sweep and letting only the two
wedges move leaves $0.80$ of the $5.07$ grid atoms standing, so the part
of the indirect channel that travels through $J$ and $\Omega$ is about a
sixth of the rate’s movement at those cells; the part that travels
through $\tilde\beta$ inside $\rho$ is not separated by that
decomposition, and the sixth is a floor on the whole indirect channel
rather than a measure of it.

**Proposition 5$'''$ (the reinvestment bracket collapses, and the rate
falls in the growth factor).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold at an
**admissible** constant-share rest point with $\omega\in[0,1)$ —
admissible in Assumption 5’s sense, so that $\tilde\beta<1$. Properness
is load-bearing three times below and is named here rather than left to
the proof: it is what puts $(1-\alpha)J=1-\eta$ in $(0,1)$ by
$(\mathrm{B}.7')$, what makes $\mathcal{M}>1-\tilde\beta>0$ by
$(\mathrm{B}.7'')$, and what signs $(\sigma-1)+(1-\tilde\beta)>0$ in
(ii). Read every object of (12) as a function of $g$ with the primitives
$(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\sigma,\delta,\delta_G,\theta,\omega)$ held fixed
— a well-defined reading, because neither $\tau$ nor $\varphi$ enters
(12) except through $g$. Write*
$$\mathcal{K}\;=\;(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)\;+\;\alpha\eta\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big),
\qquad
\mathcal{S}\;=\;\frac{\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)\,\tilde\beta\,J\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)}
                     {\mathcal{M}}\;>\;0,$$ *so that $(\mathrm{B}.7'')$
reads $\mathcal{M}=1-\tilde\beta\big(1-\gamma\mathcal{K}\big)$. Then:*

*(i)*
$1-\mathcal{K}=\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)\in(0,1)$,
*and at $\omega=0$ the reinvestment bracket of (12) collapses to a ratio
of two objects of that same form:*
$$r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)+1
   \;=\;\frac{1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)}{1-\tilde\beta\big(1-\gamma\mathcal{K}\big)},
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
\Lambda\;=\;\frac{\alpha J\big[\,1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)\,\big]}{\mathcal{M}} .$$

*(ii) $\partial J/\partial g<0$ and $\partial\Omega/\partial g<0$ hold
if and only if $(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)>(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$ and
$(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta_G)>(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta)$ respectively, and
both follow from the single condition at
$\delta_\wedge=\min\{\delta,\delta_G\}$,*
$$(\sigma-1)\big(g-1+\delta_\wedge\big)\;>\;(1-\delta_\wedge)\big(1-\tilde\beta\big),\tag{$\ddagger$}$$
*under which $\partial\mathcal{K}/\partial g>0$ and
$\partial\mathcal{M}/\partial g>0$. Neither display carries $\omega$.*

*(iii) For every $\omega\in[0,1)$, (12) decomposes as*
$$\Lambda \;=\; \mathcal{S} \;+\; \frac{J\zeta\,\big(1+\mathcal{S}\big)}{1-\omega},
\qquad\text{whence}\qquad
\frac{\partial\Lambda}{\partial g}
  \;=\;\frac{\partial\mathcal{S}}{\partial g}\Big[1+\frac{J\zeta}{1-\omega}\Big]
  \;+\;\frac{\zeta\big(1+\mathcal{S}\big)}{1-\omega}\,\frac{\partial J}{\partial g},$$
*and under $(\ddagger)$ both terms are strictly negative, so*
$$\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial g}\;<\;0
\qquad\text{at every }\omega\in[0,1).$$

*Proof.* (i) $\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ and $(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)$ sum to
one, so
$1-\mathcal{K}=\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\big(1-\alpha\eta\big)$;
and $(\mathrm{B}.7')$ gives $\eta=1-(1-\alpha)J$, whence
$1-\alpha\eta=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$. That factor lies in $(0,1)$
because $(1-\alpha)J=1-\eta\in(0,1)$ is $(\mathrm{B}.7')$ itself; and
$\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\in(0,1]$ because $\Omega\in(0,1)$ by
Proposition 3(i) and $\theta\in(0,1]$ by Assumption 2, the upper end
attained exactly at $\theta=1$, where the product is nonetheless
strictly below one because its other factor is. So
$\mathcal{K}\in(0,1)$, and it is the second factor and not the weight
that keeps it there. At $\omega=0$ the bracket $(\mathrm{B}.7)$ leaves
on the right is $(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$, so with
$r=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$ and $\mathcal{M}\rho=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$,
$$r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)
   \;=\;\frac{\tilde\beta\gamma\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)}{\mathcal{M}}
   \;=\;\frac{\tilde\beta\gamma\big(1-\mathcal{K}\big)}{\mathcal{M}} .$$
Adding one and using
$\mathcal{M}=1-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\gamma\mathcal{K}$ leaves
$\mathcal{M}+\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\mathcal{K})=1-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\gamma$,
which is the first display; the second is
$\Lambda=\alpha J\big[r(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega)+1\big]$.

2)  Proposition 4(iii) writes
    $D_x=(g-1+\delta)(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$,
    so $$\frac1J\;=\;\Big(\frac1{\tilde\beta}-\alpha\Big)
       \;+\;\frac{(1-\delta)\big(\tilde\beta^{-1}-1\big)}{g-1+\delta},$$
    and (6) gives the same identity for $1/\Omega$ with $\alpha$ deleted
    and $\delta_G$ in place of $\delta$. Differentiating and clearing
    the strictly positive factor $\tilde\beta^{-1}(g-1+\delta)^{-2}$,
    $$\tilde\beta\,(g-1+\delta)^{2}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial g}\frac1J
       \;=\;(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)-(1-\delta)\big(1-\tilde\beta\big),$$
    and likewise for $\Omega$: since $J,\Omega>0$ these are the stated
    equivalences. The map
    $d\mapsto(\sigma-1)(g-1+d)-(1-d)(1-\tilde\beta)$ has derivative
    $(\sigma-1)+(1-\tilde\beta)>0$, so ($\ddagger$) at $\delta_\wedge$
    delivers both. Using $1-\alpha\eta=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$ once
    more, $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}}{\partial g}
       \;=\;(1-\alpha)\Big[-(1-\theta)(1+\alpha J)\frac{\partial\Omega}{\partial g}
     \;-\;\alpha\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\frac{\partial J}{\partial g}\Big]\;>\;0 ,$$
    and with
    $\partial\tilde\beta/\partial g=(1-\sigma)\beta g^{-\sigma}<0$ under
    Assumption 3, $\partial\mathcal{M}/\partial g
    =-\big(\partial\tilde\beta/\partial g\big)\big(1-\gamma\mathcal{K}\big)
    +\tilde\beta\gamma\,\partial\mathcal{K}/\partial g>0$, both terms
    positive because $\gamma\mathcal{K}<1$. Neither display mentions
    $\omega$, because neither $J$ nor $\Omega$ does.

3)  By $(\mathrm{B}.7)$, $\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{M}$ with
    $\mathcal{Q}=(1-\omega)+J\zeta$, so the second entry of (12)’s
    bracket is
    $\tilde\beta\alpha\gamma\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\rho=\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}/J$
    and
    $$\Lambda=\frac{J}{1-\omega}\Big[\zeta+\frac{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}}{J}\Big]
      =\frac{J\zeta+\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Q}}{1-\omega}
      =\mathcal{S}+\frac{J\zeta(1+\mathcal{S})}{1-\omega},$$ the last
    step substituting $\mathcal{Q}=(1-\omega)+J\zeta$. Differentiating
    gives the second display. Now
    $\mathcal{S}=\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)\cdot\tilde\beta J\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)
    /\mathcal{M}$: under $(\ddagger)$ the three factors of its numerator
    all fall in $g$ — $\tilde\beta$ by Assumption 3, $J$ by (ii), and
    $\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ because $\partial\Omega/\partial g<0$ —
    while $\mathcal{M}$ rises by (ii). So
    $\partial\mathcal{S}/\partial g<0$ **term by term, with no
    cancellation**. Since $\zeta,J,\mathcal{S}>0$ and $\omega<1$, the
    bracket $1+J\zeta/(1-\omega)$ is positive and
    $\partial J/\partial g<0$, so both terms of the second display are
    strictly negative. Finally $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$ is a
    strictly increasing transform of $\Lambda$.

At $\omega=0$ the same conclusion also follows from (i) alone, by a
cancellation worth recording: differentiating that ratio, the
$\tilde\beta$ cross terms vanish identically and what remains is signed
termwise,
$$\frac{\partial}{\partial g}\,\frac{1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)}{\mathcal{M}}
   \;=\;\frac{\gamma}{\mathcal{M}^{2}}
   \Big[\frac{\partial\tilde\beta}{\partial g}\big(1-\mathcal{K}\big)
   -\big(1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)\big)\,\tilde\beta\,\frac{\partial\mathcal{K}}{\partial g}\Big]
   \;<\;0 ,$$ so there $\Lambda$ is a product of two strictly positive,
strictly decreasing factors. $\blacksquare$

Three readings, and then the scope. It is worth saying first why (i)
survives at all, since (iii) proves more with less: (iii) needs only
that four objects move monotonically, and gets the whole weight range
$\omega\in[0,1)$ for it, while (i) buys a *closed form* and buys it at
the reported weight alone. Generality and sharpness are not the same
purchase, and each display is kept for the one it makes. The sign, where
they overlap, is the same sign.

First, what (i) removes. The bracket wears $\theta$, $\Omega$, $\rho$
and $r$ on its face and none of them survives: they enter only through
$\mathcal{K}$, and $1-\mathcal{K}$ is the workforce weight
$\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ times the share of the $\ln N_W$ row that
capital’s own feedback leaves unclaimed. Numerator and denominator are
the same object $1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma x)$ at $x=1$ and at
$x=\mathcal{K}<1$, so the premium the bracket carries over one is
exactly the room between them, and it closes as $\mathcal{K}\to1$ —
which is where the workforce weight vanishes. The same form returns
Proposition 5$''$ by a route that never mentions $\rho$: at fixed $g$
neither $J$ nor $\Omega$ nor $\mathcal{K}$ carries $\gamma$, by
Proposition 4(ii), and
$\mathcal{M}-\mathcal{K}\big[1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)\big]=(1-\tilde\beta)(1-\mathcal{K})$,
so the $\gamma$-derivative of the ratio is
$\tilde\beta(1-\tilde\beta)(1-\mathcal{K})/\mathcal{M}^{2}>0$ —
properness in the numerator again, in a second derivation that shares no
step with the first.

Second, the condition. Both wedges are resolvents of stock laws, and
(ii) says exactly when a faster growth factor shrinks them: the growth
term $(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta)$ must beat the impatience term
$(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$. At $\sigma=2$ that is a quadratic in $g$
with discriminant $(1-\delta_\wedge)\big[(1-\delta_\wedge)-\beta\big]$,
so ($\ddagger$) holds at *every* admissible $g$ exactly when
$\beta\ge1-\delta_\wedge$ — at $\beta=1-\delta_\wedge$ the double root
sits exactly at the admissibility floor, which admissibility excludes,
so the margin stays strictly positive there too; the reported cell has
$\beta=0.96$ against $1-\delta_G=0.95$, and it is the infrastructure
rate, not capital’s, that binds. Neither the condition nor its
conclusion is decorative. At a depreciation floor the margin is exactly
$-(1-\delta_\wedge)(1-\tilde\beta)$, so ($\ddagger$) fails — and there
§8.3’s boundary analysis has $\Lambda\to0$, which with $\Lambda\to0$ at
$g\to\infty$ forbids monotonicity outright; at the properness pole
$\tilde\beta=1$ annihilates the right-hand side, so ($\ddagger$) holds —
and there $\Lambda$ diverges. Which of the three floors binds and
whether ($\ddagger$) can hold are the same dichotomy. At $\beta=0.90$
with the reported cell’s other primitives both limbs fail and
$\partial i^\ast/\partial g=+0.196$; at $\beta=0.935$, where only the
$\delta_G$ limb fails, the rate still falls, so ($\ddagger$) is
sufficient and not necessary.

Third, the scope, which is what the two propositions buy together.
Proposition 5$''$ signs $\partial i^\ast/\partial\gamma$ at fixed $g$;
this one signs $\partial i^\ast/\partial g$ at fixed primitives; and the
total the sweep reports is $$\frac{\mathrm{d}i^\ast}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}
   \;=\;\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\gamma}\bigg|_{g}
   \;+\;\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial g}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}g}{\mathrm{d}\gamma},$$
whose second factor belongs to the outer fixed point in $(\tau,i)$
rather than to the rate condition and is not signed here. What the pair
establishes on its own is sharper than either alone: the two channels
**oppose** wherever $\gamma$ raises $g$, and the total carries
Proposition 5$''$’s sign outright wherever it does not. Proposition 6$'$
then says where that is — the growth factor rises in the elasticity at
every fixed point with $g\ge B$ whose share loop has gain below one — so
the opposing case is the one that obtains above the reproduction scale,
and the reinforcing case below it. At the reported cell
$\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}\gamma=+0.0077$ and the direct term $+0.0757$
beats the indirect $-0.0188$; that indirect share is a third of the
total, which is above the sixth the frozen-$r$ decomposition bounds it
by, as a floor requires.

Those magnitudes belong to one cell, but the asymmetry itself needs no
sweep and does not need the Cobb–Douglas composite. Write the worker’s
reproduction as $g_W=F\big(h(c_W,z)\big)$ for an arbitrary increasing
$F$ and an arbitrary composite $h$. The split trades two channels that
both reach $g_W$ through the single scalar $h$, so $F$’s elasticity
$\varepsilon_F$ multiplies both alike, and at every $\phi$ the marginal
value carries the sign of a bracket in which $F$ does not appear: the
crossing is invariant to the whole outer map, and $\gamma$ — which lives
in $F$ — goes with it. It cannot cancel from $r$. Writing $s_c,s_z$ for
the composite’s logarithmic shares, the worker population’s own law of
motion contributes
$$\frac{\partial\ln N_W'}{\partial\ln N_W} \;=\; 1-\varepsilon_F(s_c\alpha+s_z),$$
whose leading $1$ carries no elasticity at all, so there the
reproduction elasticity enters additively rather than multiplicatively.
That is the whole asymmetry, and a CES composite, a subsistence floor
and a saturating map leave it standing alike.

What the cancellation does need is a single index — the $x_W$ of Lemma 1
— rather than a particular form: both channels must reach reproduction
through the same scalar, and attaching $\gamma$ to consumption alone
leaves it in the split. Since a single index is also what makes *the*
elasticity of reproduction a well-defined object, that requirement is
nearer a definition than an assumption. What the Cobb–Douglas form buys
is the closed form, not the asymmetry: under it the composite’s shares
$(s_c,s_z)$ are the constants $(\theta,1-\theta)$, which is what makes
(7) explicit in $\theta$, while under CES they become state-dependent
and (7) survives in shape with a moving share in place of $\theta$.

With (12) the equilibrium is closed within the constant-share class.
These are stationary first-order conditions for share-valued strategies,
and nothing here bears on Markov feedback strategies. What the closed
form should and should not reproduce is decided in advance by Definition
1, and the computation bears it out. Equation (12) is evaluated at the
rest point a profile *induces*; the grid best response maximises the
path payoff from a *declared* start. Where the two coincide the closed
form should be exact, and where the start is far from the rest point it
should not be. Measured at the three starts, the gap
$|i^\ast_{\text{grid}}-i^\ast_{(12)}|$ is $0.0008$, $0.033$ and $0.102$
against log-distances $0.10$, $1.30$ and $3.19$ between the start and
the rest point it induces — monotone in that distance, and inside one
grid atom at the start that is nearly self-consistent. A closed form
that agreed everywhere would be evidence that something had been assumed
away.

One control makes the extra term in (11) earn its place. Replacing (11)
with the worker’s envelope — deleting exactly the direct flow term
$g\zeta\varphi$ — misses the computed rate by $0.068$ to $0.091$ across
four cells, against $0.004$ to $0.033$ for the correct envelope on the
same four. The deletion is therefore visible at every cell tried and not
merely at the ones where the wedges are extreme, and it is visible in
the right direction: without capital’s own claim on his felicity, the
capitalist is predicted to reinvest too little.

At the reported cells the equilibrium corner survives initial stocks
$z_0 \in \{0.2,\,1,\,5\}$ at $\theta = 0.60$, $0.70$ and $0.74$ alike;
only at $\theta=0.78$ does the high-stock start return an interior
$\phi^\ast=0.83$. The worker meets a larger inherited stock by taxing
less — at $\theta=0.70$, $\tau^\ast$ falls from $0.48$ to $0.35$ as
$z_0$ rises from $1$ to $5$ — and the lighter tax is what sustains the
corner. Computed as a self-consistent stationary equilibrium the corner
holds for $\theta \lesssim 0.76$ rather than $\theta \lesssim 0.79$.
Nothing here is a welfare condition gap: the composite still equalises
on equilibrium, so the equalisation boundary continues to bind. What is
established is incidence — at the reported cells capital funds the
reproduction stock at the worker’s chosen maximum, and both classes gain
at the margin from its accumulation.

# 7. Stability and Distributional Cycles

Lemma 1 turns on stationarity, so the first of its three exits is to
give up the rest point altogether: if the class ratio never settles, the
indices need not equalise period by period and the wedge is unpinned.
That makes bounded distributional cycles a substantive question rather
than a footnote about dynamics. In the class in which Section 6 closes
the equilibrium, the route by which such a cycle would be born *at a
rest point* is closed everywhere in the parameter space by a bound. That
is the local route only; a far-field orbit is a separate question, taken
up at the end of the section.

Time is discrete, so the bifurcations available to $(\star)$ are those
of a map: a Neimark–Sacker (a complex pair leaving the unit circle,
giving an invariant closed curve), a flip (an eigenvalue at $-1$, giving
a period-two orbit), and a fold (an eigenvalue at $+1$). The
continuous-time Hopf bifurcation and its first Lyapunov coefficient are
not defined for this system, and the Lancaster-lineage literature names
the continuous objects, so borrowing its vocabulary here would aim the
question at results written for flows.

**Theorem 2 (no bifurcation to a distributional cycle in the
constant-share class).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with
$\theta\in(0,1)$, and hold the share policies $(\tau,\phi,i)$ fixed,
with $i\in(0,1)$ and $\phi\tau>0$ for part (ii) — the upper bound
because $i=1$ leaves the capitalist no consumption, so $g_C=0$ and no
interior $\nu^\ast$ exists. The two bounds do different work, and
Proposition 6 and Theorem 3 use only the lower one: the root of $H$, and
with it $x^\ast$ and $z^\ast$, needs $i>0$ and $\phi\tau>0$ and nothing
more, the $(x,z)$ block containing no $\nu$. Write
$p=\alpha\theta\gamma$ and $q=(1-\theta)\gamma$ as in Proposition 1, and
let $a=(1-\delta)/g$, $c=(1-\delta_G)/g$ be the survival shares of
Assumption 4. Then:*

*(i) **The class ratio is exactly a first-order process.** At every
state,* $$\ln\nu' \;=\; (1-\gamma)\ln\nu \;+\; \Theta(x,z),$$ *with
$\Theta$ free of $\nu$. Since $1-\gamma\in(0,1)$ the $\nu$ block is a
contraction with a **positive** coefficient, so it cannot oscillate on
its own: at fixed $\Theta$, $\ln\nu$ converges monotonically at exactly
that rate. Along a transition $\Theta$ moves with $(x,z)$ and $\ln\nu$
may then be non-monotone — not by an alternation of its own making, but
by interference between the forcing mode and its own, which can occur
even where the $(x,z)$ spectrum is real and positive and the $(x,z)$
path does not turn. Whenever $(x,z)$ settles at $(x^\ast,z^\ast)$ **and
$i<1$**, $\ln\nu \to \Theta(x^\ast,z^\ast)/\gamma$, which is finite, at
rate at most $\max\{1-\gamma,\ \rho(\mathcal{J})\}$ for $\rho$ the
spectral radius, with equality for generic initial conditions. That
upper bound is the one the preamble attaches to part (ii), and it is
needed here for the same reason and no other: $i=1$ is admissible under
Assumption 1, and there $c_C=0$ sends the logarithm in $\Theta$ to
$-\infty$, so the limit is not finite and no interior $\nu^\ast$ exists.
What the *implication\* needs is that upper bound and nothing else; its
antecedent needs the two lower ones the preamble already names, since
without $i>0$ and $\phi\tau>0$ there is no $(x^\ast,z^\ast)$ to settle
at and the conditional is empty rather than false.\*

*(ii) **An interior rest point exists and is unique.** On
$g>\max(1-\delta,1-\delta_G)$ the function
$H(g) \equiv \ln g - \ln g_W\big(x(g),z(g)\big)$ satisfies*
$$H'(g) \;=\; \frac{1}{g} \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)(g-1+\delta)}
              \;+\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{g-1+\delta_G} \;>\; 0,$$ *a
sum of three strictly positive terms, with $H\to-\infty$ at the left
endpoint and $H\to+\infty$ as $g\to\infty$. Hence $H$ has exactly one
root, and the interior rest point is unique.*

*(iii) **No eigenvalue ever reaches the unit circle.** At any interior
rest point the $2\times2$ Jacobian of the $(x,z)$ block in logarithms
is*
$$\mathcal{J} \;=\; \begin{pmatrix} a+\alpha(1-a)-p & -q \\[2pt] \alpha(1-c)-p & c-q \end{pmatrix},
\qquad
D \equiv \det\mathcal{J} = a(1-\alpha)(c-q) + \alpha c(1-\gamma), \quad T \equiv \operatorname{tr}\mathcal{J},$$
*and for all $a,c\in[0,1]$ the three Jury margins satisfy*
$$1-D \;\ge\; p+q, \qquad 1+T+D \;>\; 1-q, \qquad 1-T+D \;\ge\; 0, \tag{J}$$
*the first and third with equality **only** at $a=c=1$, that is only at
$g=1-\delta=1-\delta_G$, a common depreciation floor at which neither
stock receives any gross investment, which (A) excludes. Consequently no
Neimark–Sacker, flip or fold bifurcation occurs at any interior rest
point anywhere in the admissible parameter space, and every interior
rest point is locally asymptotically stable.*

*Proof.* **(i)** By $(\star)$, $\ln\nu' = \ln\nu + \ln g_C - \ln g_W$.
Assumption 2 gives $\ln g_C = \ln B + \gamma\ln c_C$ with
$c_C = (1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha A x^{\alpha}/\nu$, so
$\ln g_C = \ln B + \gamma\ln\!\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha A x^{\alpha}\big] - \gamma\ln\nu$.
The worker’s factor $\ln g_W$ is a function of $c_W$ and $z$ only, and
neither contains $\nu$. Collecting,
$$\ln\nu' = (1-\gamma)\ln\nu + \underbrace{\ln B + \gamma\ln\!\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha A x^{\alpha}\big]
            - \ln g_W(x,z)}_{\textstyle \Theta(x,z)},$$ which is the
stated identity, and it is exact rather than a linearisation. Because
$\gamma\in(0,1)$ we have $1-\gamma\in(0,1)$: the recursion is a
contraction with a *positive* coefficient, so at **fixed** $\Theta$ it
converges monotonically, and the $\nu$ block generates no oscillation of
its own — that would require a negative coefficient. Monotonicity does
not survive a moving $\Theta$. Unrolling,
$$\ln\nu_t-\ln\nu^\ast \;=\; (1-\gamma)^t\big(\ln\nu_0-\ln\nu^\ast\big)
  \;+\; \sum_{s<t}(1-\gamma)^{\,t-1-s}\big[\Theta(x_s,z_s)-\Theta^\ast\big],$$
so a forcing term still in transit can turn the path around — trivially
so when $\nu_0=\nu^\ast$ while $(x_0,z_0)\neq(x^\ast,z^\ast)$, since
$\ln\nu$ must then leave its own limit and return — and the rate of
convergence is at most $\max\{1-\gamma,\rho(\mathcal{J})\}$ rather than
$1-\gamma$, with equality generically. Two qualifications are needed and
neither is cosmetic. First, the bound is not attained when the dominant
mode’s coefficient vanishes: if $(x_0,z_0)=(x^\ast,z^\ast)$ the forcing
is identically $\Theta^\ast$ and the rate is exactly $1-\gamma$; on the
strong stable manifold of $\mathcal{J}$ it is the subdominant
$|\lambda_2|$; and when $\rho(\mathcal{J})<1-\gamma$ there is, for each
$(x_0,z_0)$, one $\nu_0$ at which the $(1-\gamma)^t$ term cancels and
the rate drops to $\rho(\mathcal{J})$. Second, $1-\gamma$ is itself an
eigenvalue of $\mathcal{J}$ exactly when
$\big(a-(1-\gamma)\big)\big(c-(1-\gamma\theta)\big)=0$; on the second
branch the error carries an extra factor $t$, so it is
$\Theta\big(t(1-\gamma)^t\big)$ rather than $O\big((1-\gamma)^t\big)$,
while on the first branch the resonant eigenvector is $(1,\alpha)$,
which the gradient of $\Theta$ annihilates, and no such factor appears.
Nothing downstream turns on the monotonicity: what (i) supplies to (iii)
is the third eigenvalue $1-\gamma$ and the finiteness of the limit, and
both are untouched. If $(x,z)\to(x^\ast,z^\ast)$ then
$\Theta\to\Theta^\ast$, and the fixed point of
$\ell = (1-\gamma)\ell + \Theta^\ast$ is $\ell = \Theta^\ast/\gamma$,
finite because $\gamma>0$. This also closes the *unbounded-ratio*
reading of Section 5’s first exit.

**(ii)** At a rest point each stock law inverts. From
$gx = (1-\delta)x + i(1-\tau)\alpha Ax^{\alpha}$,
$x(g-1+\delta) = i(1-\tau)\alpha Ax^{\alpha}$, so
$x(g) = \big[i(1-\tau)\alpha A/(g-1+\delta)\big]^{1/(1-\alpha)}$, which
is well defined and positive because $\alpha<1$, $i>0$ and
$g-1+\delta>0$. Likewise
$z(g) = \phi\tau\alpha Ax(g)^{\alpha}/(g-1+\delta_G)$ is positive
because $\phi\tau>0$. Both positivity requirements are hypotheses of the
theorem rather than consequences of Assumptions 1–4, and they cannot be
dropped: at $i=0$ the capital law gives $g=1-\delta$ at any rest point,
and at $\phi\tau=0$ the public-stock law gives $g=1-\delta_G$, each
contradicting Assumption 4. On those faces of the instrument box no
interior rest point exists at all. Substituting into $\ln g = \ln g_W$
and using
$\ln g_W = \ln B + \gamma\theta\ln(\kappa_W A x^{\alpha}) + \gamma(1-\theta)\ln z$,
the two $\ln x$ terms collect with coefficient
$\gamma\theta\alpha + \gamma(1-\theta)\alpha = \gamma\alpha$, and
$\ln x(g) = \big[\text{const} - \ln(g-1+\delta)\big]/(1-\alpha)$, so up
to a constant free of $g$,
$$H(g) \;=\; \ln g \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(g-1+\delta)
        \;+\; \gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G) \;+\; \text{const}.$$
Differentiating gives the displayed $H'$, whose three terms are strictly
positive on $g>\max(1-\delta,1-\delta_G)$ because $\alpha\in(0,1)$,
$\gamma>0$ and $\theta<1$. As $g$ decreases to that left endpoint at
least one logarithm diverges to $-\infty$, no term diverges to
$+\infty$, and every logarithm carries a strictly positive coefficient,
so $H\to-\infty$. (How many diverge depends on the parameters and does
not matter: when $\delta\neq\delta_G$ and both are below $1$ exactly one
does and the others stay finite; when $\delta=\delta_G<1$ two do; when
$\delta=\delta_G=1$ the left endpoint is $g=0$ and all three do.
Positive coefficients make the sum diverge in each case.) As
$g\to\infty$ every term diverges to $+\infty$. A continuous, strictly
increasing function with these limits has exactly one root. That root
fixes $g$, hence $x^\ast=x(g)$ and $z^\ast=z(g)$; the class ratio is
then pinned by part (i) rather than by a second fixed-point argument.
Stationarity of $\nu$ in $\ln\nu'=(1-\gamma)\ln\nu+\Theta(x,z)$ reads
$\gamma\ln\nu^\ast=\Theta(x^\ast,z^\ast)$, so
$\nu^\ast=\exp\big[\Theta(x^\ast,z^\ast)/\gamma\big]$, unique because
$\gamma>0$ and lying in $(0,\infty)$ because $\Theta(x^\ast,z^\ast)$ is
finite — which is exactly what $i<1$ buys, since $\Theta$ carries
$\gamma\ln\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha Ax^{\alpha}\big]$ and $i=1$ sends it
to $-\infty$ and $\nu^\ast$ to zero. The triple
$(x^\ast,\nu^\ast,z^\ast)$ is therefore interior, and unique.

**(iii)** *The Jacobian.* Write $\mathcal{N}_x,\mathcal{N}_z$ for the
numerators of $x',z'$ in $(\star)$. Since the inflow into each is
proportional to $y = Ax^{\alpha}$,
$$\frac{\partial\ln \mathcal{N}_x}{\partial\ln x} = s_x + \alpha(1-s_x), \qquad
  \frac{\partial\ln \mathcal{N}_z}{\partial\ln x} = \alpha(1-s_z), \qquad
  \frac{\partial\ln \mathcal{N}_z}{\partial\ln z} = s_z,$$ where
$s_x = (1-\delta)x/\mathcal{N}_x$ and
$s_z = (1-\delta_G)z/\mathcal{N}_z$ are the shares of each next-period
stock inherited from the surviving old stock. At a rest point
$\mathcal{N}_x = gx$ and $\mathcal{N}_z = gz$, so
$s_x = (1-\delta)/g = a$ and $s_z = (1-\delta_G)/g = c$. Since
$x' = \mathcal{N}_x/g_W$ and $z' = \mathcal{N}_z/g_W$, subtracting the
elasticities of $g_W$ from Proposition 1 gives the four entries of
$\mathcal{J}$. Expanding the determinant and substituting
$p=\alpha\theta\gamma$, $q=(1-\theta)\gamma$ — under which
$\alpha-p-q\alpha = \alpha(1-\gamma)$ — yields
$D = a(1-\alpha)(c-q) + \alpha c(1-\gamma)$.

*The margins.* $D$ is bilinear in $(a,c)$ and $T$ is affine in each, so
each of
$$M_{\mathrm{NS}} = 1-D-(p+q), \qquad M_{\mathrm{flip}} = (1+T+D)-(1-q), \qquad
  M_{\mathrm{fold}} = 1-T+D$$ is **multilinear** in $(a,c)$: affine in
$a$ for fixed $c$ and conversely. A multilinear function on the box
$[0,1]^2$ attains its minimum at a vertex, so it suffices to evaluate
the four vertices:

| vertex $(a,c)$ | $M_{\mathrm{NS}}$ | $M_{\mathrm{flip}}$ | $M_{\mathrm{fold}}$ |
|----|----|----|----|
| $(0,0)$ | $1-p-q$ | $\alpha(1-\theta\gamma)$ | $(1-\alpha)+\gamma\big[1-\theta(1-\alpha)\big]$ |
| $(0,1)$ | $(1-\alpha)(1-q)$ | $1+\alpha(2-\gamma\theta-\gamma)$ | $\gamma(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)$ |
| $(1,0)$ | $1-\alpha\gamma$ | $1-\gamma(1-\theta)+\alpha\gamma(1-2\theta)$ | $\alpha\gamma$ |
| $(1,1)$ | $0$ | $3-\gamma(1-\theta)-2\alpha\gamma\theta$ | $0$ |

Every entry is non-negative on the declared domain, and each is strictly
positive except the two zeros. For $M_{\mathrm{NS}}$:
$p+q = \gamma\big[1-\theta(1-\alpha)\big] < \gamma < 1$ gives the
$(0,0)$ entry; $q=\gamma(1-\theta)<1$ and $\alpha<1$ give $(0,1)$;
$\alpha\gamma<1$ gives $(1,0)$. For $M_{\mathrm{fold}}$: the $(0,1)$
entry is strictly positive precisely because $\theta<1$, and the $(1,0)$
entry because $\alpha,\gamma>0$. For $M_{\mathrm{flip}}$:
$\theta\gamma<1$ gives $(0,0)$; $\gamma\theta+\gamma<2$ gives $(0,1)$;
at $(1,0)$, $\gamma<1$ and $\alpha<1$ give
$1-\gamma(1-\theta)+\alpha\gamma(1-2\theta) > 1-(1-\theta)-(2\theta-1)=1-\theta>0$
when $\theta>\tfrac12$ and $>1-\gamma>0$ otherwise; and at $(1,1)$,
$\gamma\big[(1-\theta)+2\alpha\theta\big] < (1-\theta)+2\theta = 1+\theta \le 2$
leaves the entry above $1$. Hence $M_{\mathrm{NS}}\ge0$ and
$M_{\mathrm{fold}}\ge0$ on the box, and $M_{\mathrm{flip}}>0$
throughout.

*Where the equalities are.* Minimality at a vertex pins the minimum
*value*; it does not locate the zero *set*, and a multilinear function
can be non-negative, minimised at a vertex, and still vanish on a whole
edge. So the “only at $a=c=1$” of (J) is earned by the signs of the
partials rather than by the table. On the box,
$$\frac{\partial M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\partial c} = -\big[a(1-\alpha)+\alpha(1-\gamma)\big],
\qquad \frac{\partial M_{\mathrm{NS}}}{\partial a}\bigg|_{c=1} = -(1-\alpha)(1-q),$$
$$\frac{\partial M_{\mathrm{fold}}}{\partial a} = -(1-\alpha)\big[(1-c)+q\big],
\qquad \frac{\partial M_{\mathrm{fold}}}{\partial c}
   = -\big[\alpha\gamma+(1-a)(1-\alpha)\big],$$ the last two after the
identities $(1-\alpha)(c-q-1)=-(1-\alpha)[(1-c)+q]$ and
$1-a(1-\alpha)-\alpha(1-\gamma)=\alpha\gamma+(1-a)(1-\alpha)$, and all
four are strictly negative on the declared domain — the first because
$\alpha(1-\gamma)>0$, the second because $q=\gamma(1-\theta)<1$, the
third because $q>0$, the fourth because $\alpha\gamma>0$. Hence
$M_{\mathrm{fold}}$ falls strictly in both arguments and exceeds
$M_{\mathrm{fold}}(1,1)=0$ at every other point of the box; and
$M_{\mathrm{NS}}(a,c)>M_{\mathrm{NS}}(a,1)$ for $c<1$, with
$M_{\mathrm{NS}}(a,1)>M_{\mathrm{NS}}(1,1)=0$ for $a<1$. The order of
the two directions is not interchangeable:
$\partial M_{\mathrm{NS}}/\partial a = -(1-\alpha)(c-q)$ changes sign at
$c=q$, so $M_{\mathrm{NS}}$ is not monotone in $a$ on the whole box and
the argument has to run through $c$ first. This is (J), with equality in
the first and third only at $a=c=1$.

*Conclusion.* The three inequalities in (J) are the Jury conditions
$D<1$, $1+T+D>0$ and $1-T+D>0$ for the characteristic polynomial
$\lambda^2 - T\lambda + D$, strengthened by the explicit margins $p+q$,
$1-q$ and $0$ respectively. The test for a quadratic is usually written
with $|D|<1$; the lower half is redundant, since adding $1+T+D>0$ to
$1-T+D>0$ gives $2(1+D)>0$. On the interior $a,c\in[0,1)$ — which is
where Assumption 4 puts an interior rest point — all three hold
strictly, so both eigenvalues of $\mathcal{J}$ lie strictly inside the
unit disc and neither can reach its boundary as parameters vary. The
third eigenvalue of the full system is $1-\gamma$, by part (i) and
because $x'$ and $z'$ contain no $\nu$, which makes the $3\times3$
Jacobian block-triangular with spectrum
$\operatorname{spec}(\mathcal{J})\cup\{1-\gamma\}$; and
$1-\gamma\in(0,1)$. The whole spectrum is therefore inside the unit
disc, the rest point is locally asymptotically stable, and no local
bifurcation of any of the three types available to a map can occur.
$\blacksquare$

Interiority is a conclusion of part (ii) and not a hypothesis of it,
which is what keeps the interiority clause every later result carries
from being empty. Part (ii) constructs
$\nu^\ast=\exp\big[\Theta(x^\ast,z^\ast)/\gamma\big]$ and places it in
$(0,\infty)$ because $\Theta(x^\ast,z^\ast)$ is finite. The instrument
box has faces on which interiority fails, and they fail in two different
ways. At $i=0$ or $\phi\tau=0$ a stock has no source term, the growth
factor is driven to that stock’s own depreciation floor, and no interior
rest point exists at all. At $i=1$ the pair $(x^\ast,z^\ast)$ survives
and the ratio does not: the capitalist consumes nothing, so $g_C=0$, his
dynasty stops in one period and $\nu^\ast$ is zero. There is no third
case, because $\Theta^\ast$ is finite wherever $x^\ast$ and $z^\ast$ are
positive and $(1-i)(1-\tau)>0$, and $\tau\le\bar\tau<1$ by Assumption 1.
The two blocks also settle on different terms, and not in the direction
the state list suggests: part (i) is exact rather than a linearisation
and its coefficient $1-\gamma$ lies in $(0,1)$ at every state, so
$\ln\nu_t$ returns to $\ln\nu^\ast$ from any initial composition once
the forcing settles, while the $(x,z)$ block carries no such guarantee
and part (iii) is a statement at a rest point. Class composition is the
coordinate that cannot be dislodged; the stocks through which
accumulation runs are the ones whose return is proved only locally.

The $i=1$ corner has a counterpart at the other class’s instrument, and
the two are not symmetric. Each class can stop its own dynasty in a
single period, and only the capitalist can do it with his instrument
alone: the worker’s split at zero does it only where the public stock
fully depreciates, which is Proposition 2(iii)’s first case, while
reinvestment at one does it at every parameter value. Neither class can
stop the other’s in a single period, since $\tau\le\bar\tau<1$ leaves
the capitalist’s consumption strictly positive and the worker’s
$\kappa_W A x^{\alpha}$ carries no $i$. What differs is how the two
corners are closed. The worker’s is closed by his own admissibility:
under Assumptions 3 and 5 the date-$2$ payoff there is $-\infty$, so it
is inadmissible rather than merely poor. The capitalist’s is not closed
by his, because (A5) is a condition on $g_W$ and on $g_P$ and does not
contain $g_C$ at all; nothing in properness stops him from reinvesting
his own class out of existence. What stops him is that he values
consumption, which is a property of his objective and not of the
structure.

The two equalities in (J) are not the same event. At $a=c=1$ — that is
$g=1-\delta=1-\delta_G$, the common depreciation floor Assumption 4
excludes — the polynomial factors exactly:
$$\lambda^2-T\lambda+D = \lambda^2-(2-p-q)\lambda+(1-p-q) = (\lambda-1)\big(\lambda-(1-p-q)\big).$$
What vanishes there is the fold margin, an eigenvalue sitting at $+1$;
the Neimark–Sacker condition still holds strictly, since $D=1-p-q<1$,
and the second eigenvalue $1-p-q$ is inside the disc. That corner is a
monotone unit root and not an oscillation, and it is the same object as
the far-field limit, where the Jacobian tends to
$\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}1-p & -q\\ -p & 1-q\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]$
with spectrum $\{1,\,1-p-q\}$. The depreciation-floor limit and the
far-field limit are one degeneracy seen twice.

The argument is exhaustive rather than a search. The margins depend on
$(a,c,\alpha,\theta,\gamma)$ and on nothing else — the scale parameters,
the depreciation rates and the three instruments entering only through
the two survival shares and the growth factor inside them, never
separately — and they are multilinear in those two shares, so the square
the shares occupy reduces to its four corners. That square is the right
object rather than a convenient enclosure: with the two depreciation
rates free in Assumption 1 and the reproduction scale free in Assumption
2, every interior point of it is the survival pair of some admissible
economy, so nothing in the box argument is slack. Each corner leaves an
inequality in $\alpha$, $\theta$ and $\gamma$; those three are not
corner-evaluated in turn, their box being open, and it is on the
interior that the inequalities have to hold. Five numbers in the unit
cube is a small enough object to check numerically as well as to settle
by algebra, and both were done — the algebra is what settles it, the
draws being a sample and not an enumeration. Two of the three margins
are interpretable rather than residual: the distance to a Neimark–Sacker
is the total elasticity of worker reproduction with respect to the
state, and the distance to a period-two orbit is one minus its
elasticity with respect to the public stock alone. What carries both is
$\gamma<1$ — the same outer elasticity the fiscal asymmetry of Section 6
turns on — and it is not a knife edge: at the reported shares a flip
would require $\gamma \approx 2.2$. The third margin, the fold, does
approach zero, but only at the corner $a=c=1$ of the survival square —
reached in the far field, where both inherited shares tend to one and no
growth factor is involved, and among interior rest points only on the
diagonal $\delta=\delta_G$, where $i\to0$ drives $g$ to the common floor
$1-\delta=1-\delta_G$ (Theorem 2$'$(iii)) — which is the familiar unit
root of a stock receiving no gross investment and is monotone rather
than oscillatory. When the two stocks depreciate at the same rate one
eigenvalue is available exactly: at $\delta=\delta_G$ we have $a=c$, the
rows of $\mathcal{J}-a\mathrm{I}$ coincide identically, so
$a = (1-\delta)/g$ is an eigenvalue, and interiority *is* the stability
condition for that mode rather than merely sufficient for it. It need
not be the leading one — the other root is $a(1-\alpha)+\alpha-p-q$,
which exceeds $a$ whenever $\alpha(1-a)>p+q$ — so the exact value
settles that mode and not the spectral radius, which is what Theorem
2(iii) bounds.

Theorem 2 is a statement about rest points. No equilibrium of this
chassis can lose stability, so the bifurcation route to a cycle is shut;
it does not prove that no periodic orbit exists anywhere. Pointwise
spectral radius below one is not a contraction — products of
individually contracting matrices can expand — and a period-two orbit
can in principle be born far from the rest point in a saddle-node of the
second iterate, with no local bifurcation at all. Two facts bound that
claim rather than extend it: the eigenvalue bound does hold at every
state and not only at rest points, and on any region where the two
stocks stay bounded away from pure durability the map is a genuine
contraction in a common elliptic metric — but the bound is not uniform,
because the far-field Jacobian carries a unit root, and that is exactly
what blocks a global argument. A trajectory sweep finds no orbit other
than convergence to the rest point, and it is numerical evidence, not a
proof.

Theorem 2 pins the class ratio implicitly, through $\Theta$. It can be
made explicit, and the explicit form says which instrument reaches
relative class size and through what.

**Theorem 2$'$ (the stationary class ratio in closed form, and how the
instruments move it).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with the share
policies $(\tau,\phi,i)$ fixed, $i\in(0,1)$, $\phi\tau>0$ and
$\theta\in(0,1]$, and let $(x^\ast,\nu^\ast,z^\ast)$ be the interior
rest point. Write $\kappa_W=(1-\alpha)+(1-\phi)\tau\alpha$ as in Section
3. Then:*

*(i) **The stationary class ratio is a closed form in the instruments
and the growth factor alone, and the capital stock has cancelled out of
it:*** $$\nu^\ast \;=\; \frac{(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha}{\kappa_W^{\theta}}
  \left(\frac{g-1+\delta_G}{\phi\tau\alpha}\right)^{1-\theta}. \tag{N$'''$}$$
*At $\theta=1$ the exponent $1-\theta$ annihilates the second factor and
$\nu^\ast=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha/\kappa_W$, free of $g$ and of every stock,
at every split $\phi\in(0,1]$. That extends Proposition 2(ii)’s (N$'$),
which is the same expression on the unfunded face $\phi=0$ — a face
(N$'''$) does not reach, its hypothesis being $\phi\tau>0$ — and the two
agree as $\phi\to0$. The closed form is therefore not a property of the
unfunded corner: once $\theta=1$ it holds at every split, with $\phi$
entering it only through $\kappa_W$ — so the ratio’s **form** is general
there while its **level** still moves with the split.*

*(ii) **Beyond the direct retention term, reinvestment reaches relative
class size through one channel only, and that channel carries the weight
$1-\theta$:*** $$\frac{\partial\ln\nu^\ast}{\partial i} \;=\;
  \underbrace{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\cdot
              \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{i\,(g-1+\delta_G)\,H'(g)}}_{\textstyle S}
  \;-\; \frac{1}{1-i},$$ *with $\tau$, $\phi$ and the primitives held
fixed and $H'$ as in Theorem 2(ii). Every factor of $S$ is strictly
positive on the admissible box except $1-\theta$, so $S>0$ if and only
if $\theta<1$.*

*(iii) **At the no-social-wage corner the response is signed; with
public provision it is not.** At $\theta=1$,
$\partial\ln\nu^\ast/\partial i=-1/(1-i)<0$ at every $i\in(0,1)$:
reinvestment unambiguously shrinks the capitalist class relative to the
worker class. At $\theta<1$ it has no uniform sign, and the witnesses
below settle the sharper of the two readings that claim admits: they
hold every primitive fixed and vary only $i$, so the sign changes
**within one admissible cell** rather than merely between two of them.*

*Whether **every** $\theta<1$ cell contains such a change is a strictly
stronger statement, and it is **false**: at
$(\alpha,\gamma,\theta,\delta,\delta_G,\tau,\phi)=(0.33,\,0.5,\,0.95,\,0.02,\,0.15,\,0.4,\,1)$
the derivative is negative at every $i$ sampled across $(10^{-8},0.99)$,
least negative $-0.962$ as $i\to0$, and the cell is interior throughout
with $g$ rising through one, at $A=B=1$. **That refutation does not rest
on the sample, and it cannot**: what has to be exhibited is a cell in
which no sign change occurs at any $i$, which is a universal a grid
cannot deliver. An upper bound on $S$ in closed form rises as $i$ falls,
so it is approached only in the limit and its value there is a supremum
over $(0,1)$ rather than a maximum over a grid — at this cell, below
$0.0383$, against $1/(1-i)\ge1$ everywhere. The difference cannot change
sign.*

*On this face that supremum has a closed form in the primitives, derived
in the proof, and with it the boundary at which it reaches one. What
separates the two cells is which depreciation rate is the smaller — a
comparison with three outcomes, not two — and the mechanism is visible
in $\mathcal{H}$.*

*As $i\to0$ the growth factor is driven to $1-\min(\delta,\delta_G)$. If
$\delta_G<\delta$ then $g-1+\delta_G\to0$ while $g-1+\delta$ stays away
from zero, so $(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)\to\gamma(1-\theta)$,
$\mathcal{C}\to1$, and $S\sim\alpha/[(1-\alpha)i]$ diverges: the sign
change is forced, and this is the case the witnesses below occupy. **The
knife-edge $\delta=\delta_G$ falls on that same side, and it is worth
separating because it reaches the same conclusion through a different
limit.** There the two collapses are simultaneous, their ratio is one
throughout, and the middle term survives:
$(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)\to\gamma\big[1-\theta+\alpha\theta\big]/(1-\alpha)$,
which is finite and strictly positive, so
$\mathcal{C}\to(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)/(1-\theta+\alpha\theta)$ — strictly
below one, since one minus it is $\alpha/(1-\theta+\alpha\theta)>0$ —
while $S\sim\alpha(1-\theta)/\big[(1-\theta+\alpha\theta)\,i\big]$ still
diverges. **The forced sign change therefore holds on the whole of
$\delta_G\le\delta$**, at both ends and not only at the near one: the
derivative is positive for small $i$ by the divergence just given, and
the bound used in the proof below keeps $S$ finite away from $i=0$,
where $-1/(1-i)$ is not, so it is negative for $i$ near one. The cap of
part (v) is not merely inherited in that limit but strict. If
$\delta<\delta_G$ the collapse falls on $g-1+\delta$ instead,
$\mathcal{H}'$ diverges through its middle term, $\mathcal{C}$ vanishes
at the rate of $i$ itself, and the $1/i$ in $S$ is cancelled — $S$
approaches a finite limit and may never reach one.*

*Two levers move that limit and both were exercised: on the cell above
the change survives at $\theta=0.1$, where the derivative is $+0.41$
near zero, and is gone by $\theta=0.3$, where it is $-0.073$; and
holding $\theta$ at $0.95$ while closing $\delta_G-\delta$ to $10^{-10}$
returns $S$ to $4.5\times10^{4}$ and restores the change.*

***Where in the primitive box that boundary lies is not a further
question: the same identity the paragraph above already uses settles
it.** On $\delta<\delta_G$ the middle term of $\mathcal{H}'$ is the only
one that diverges as $g\to1-\delta$, so the $\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ carried
by $S$ cancels against it and*
$$\lim_{i\to0}S \;=\; \frac{(1-\theta)\,L}{\delta_G-\delta},
\qquad
L \;=\; \exp\!\left\{\frac{1-\alpha}{\gamma\alpha}
   \Big[\mathcal{P}_{-i}-\ln(1-\delta)-\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(\delta_G-\delta)\Big]\right\},$$
*where $\mathcal{P}_{-i}$ is the $i$-free part of Proposition 6’s share
side — this is the displayed identity for $\ln[(g-1+\delta)/i]$
evaluated at the endpoint, and nothing else. **The locus is therefore
not merely a level set but a graph.** $\mathcal{P}_{-i}$ carries $\ln B$
additively, so $\ln\lim_{i\to0}S$ is **affine in $\ln B$** with slope
$(1-\alpha)/(\gamma\alpha)>0$: the limit is strictly increasing in the
reproduction scale, so $\lim_{i\to0}S=1$ has a **unique root**, and
solving for it turns the boundary into a formula:*
$$B^\ast \;=\; \frac{1-\delta}{\big(\kappa_W A\big)^{\gamma\theta}}
  \left(\frac{\delta_G-\delta}{\phi\tau\alpha A}\right)^{\gamma(1-\theta)}
  \left(\frac{\delta_G-\delta}{(1-\theta)(1-\tau)\alpha A}\right)^{\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}} .$$

***That single comparison decides the sign on the whole of $(0,1)$ and
not merely near zero.** The bound of the paragraph above falls in $i$
and is tight at the endpoint, so the supremum of $S$ over $(0,1)$ is the
limit just displayed and is attained nowhere inside. Hence
$\partial\ln\nu^\ast/\partial i$ changes sign at some $i\in(0,1)$ **if
and only if $B>B^\ast$**; where $B\le B^\ast$ it is strictly negative at
every rate, the threshold itself falling on the no-change side because a
supremum approached and not attained cannot be reached. Where the change
does occur the derivative is positive near $i=0$ and diverges to
$-\infty$ as $i\to1$.*

*On this face it is the reproduction scale that separates two regimes.
Above $B^\ast$ reinvestment enlarges the capitalist class relative to
the worker class at low rates and shrinks it at high ones; at or below
$B^\ast$ it shrinks it at every rate, which is the sign that holds
throughout the $\theta=1$ corner. At the cell just quoted $B^\ast=2.234$
against the $B=1$ it is evaluated at, which is why the sign never turns
there. The formula does not reach the knife-edge: as
$\delta_G\downarrow\delta$ its exponent diverges, which is the diverging
branch above meeting this one. Witnesses at
$(\alpha,\gamma,\theta,\delta,\delta_G,\tau,\phi,B)=(0.33,\,0.5,\,0.6,\,0.08,\,0.05,\,0.4,\,1,\,1)$:
$\partial\ln\nu^\ast/\partial i=+18.90$ at $i=0.02$ and $-0.86$ at
$i=0.35$, with the sign changing at $i=0.2059$. The positive region is
not an artefact of a contracting economy, though at those primitives it
sits in one. That $g<1$ at small $i$ is forced rather than incidental —
the only $i$-dependence of $H$ is the additive
$-\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln i$, which diverges to $+\infty$ as
$i\to0$, while $B$ enters $H$ only as a finite additive constant; since
$H$ is strictly increasing in $g$ and tends to $-\infty$ at the left
endpoint, its root is driven to $1-\min(\delta,\delta_G)<1$ at every
reproduction scale — by that argument and not by a sweep. That is not
what produces the positive sign: at $B=1.20$ with everything else
unchanged the sign changes at $i=0.1892$, and just inside the positive
region, at $i=0.1836$, $\partial\ln\nu^\ast/\partial i=+0.053$ while
$g=1.0081$. The positive region and the growing region therefore
intersect.*

*Taken with the depreciation split, that settles the question on the
whole of the $\theta<1$ region: **$\partial\ln\nu^\ast/\partial i$
changes sign on $(0,1)$ if and only if $\delta_G\le\delta$ or
$B>B^\ast$.** Where the public stock depreciates faster than capital the
reproduction scale decides which way reinvestment moves relative class
size; where it does not, no scale prevents the reversal.*

*(iv) **At the no-social-wage corner relative class size is a function
of the instruments and the capital share alone — no feature of the
reproduction technology survives into it — and public provision is
exactly what breaks that.** This part does not use Assumption 2’s
**outer map**: the reproduction function may be any common strictly
increasing $F$, as in Lemma 1. Assumption 2 enters only to name the
corner — $\theta=1$ is where the worker’s reproduction index is his own
private consumption — and the rest is Assumption 1’s budget. At that
corner Lemma 1 delivers $c_W=c_C$, the paper’s (N), and the budget pins*
$$\nu^\ast=\frac{(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha}{\kappa_W},$$ *in which no
reproduction primitive appears at all: not the scale, not the
elasticity, not the map. Productivity and both depreciation rates are
absent too. All of them still govern **whether** an interior stationary
state exists; none of them governs the **ratio** at one. On that face
all three instruments are signed unambiguously,*
$$\frac{\partial\ln\nu^\ast}{\partial i}=-\frac{1}{1-i}<0, \qquad
  \frac{\partial\ln\nu^\ast}{\partial\tau}=-\frac{1}{1-\tau}-\frac{(1-\phi)\alpha}{\kappa_W}<0,
  \qquad
  \frac{\partial\ln\nu^\ast}{\partial\phi}=\frac{\tau\alpha}{\kappa_W}>0,$$
*so at $\theta=1$ the class ratio falls in the tax and in reinvestment
and rises in the split.*

*(v) **The tax is the one instrument public provision cannot turn.**
Write*
$$\mathcal{C} \;\equiv\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{(g-1+\delta_G)\,\mathcal{H}'(g)} ,$$
*with $\mathcal{H}$ Proposition 6’s growth side, so that this part uses
that proposition rather than re-deriving it. $\mathcal{H}'$ is the same
object as part (ii)’s $H'$ — Proposition 6(i) gives
$H=\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{P}$ and $\mathcal{P}$ carries no $g$ — and is
written here in the form that displays where the bound comes from.
Proposition 6 and Theorem 2 both stop at $\theta<1$ while $\mathcal{C}$
is used here across $\theta\in(0,1]$; the endpoint is reached and not
assumed, because there the term $\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)$ is
**absent from $\mathcal{H}$** rather than present with zero weight,
leaving $\mathcal{H}$ a strictly increasing bijection with
$\mathcal{H}'(g)=1/g+\gamma\alpha/[(1-\alpha)(g-1+\delta)]>0$. Then
$\mathcal{C}\in[0,1)$ at every admissible interior rest point, and for
every $\theta\in(0,1]$* $$\frac{\partial\ln\nu^\ast}{\partial\tau}
  \;=\; -\frac{1}{1-\tau}\Big(1+\frac{\mathcal{C}\,\alpha}{1-\alpha}\Big)
  \;-\; \Big(\theta\,\frac{(1-\phi)\alpha}{\kappa_W}+\frac{1-\theta}{\tau}\Big)\big(1-\mathcal{C}\big)
  \;<\;0 .$$ *The bound $\mathcal{C}<1$ restricts no parameter and is
not an extra hypothesis. It is a property of $\mathcal{H}$ and can be
read off Proposition 6’s display: the public input enters the growth
side only through the term $\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)$, whose
contribution to $(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)$ is exactly
$\gamma(1-\theta)$, while the two remaining terms of $\mathcal{H}$
contribute strictly positive amounts:*
$$(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)-\gamma(1-\theta)
  \;=\; \frac{g-1+\delta_G}{g}
      \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\cdot\frac{g-1+\delta_G}{g-1+\delta}
  \;>\;0$$ *on the whole admissible box. **The term that opens the
public-input channel is the same term that stiffens the growth factor
against it**, and it cannot outweigh itself plus two positives. At
$\theta=1$, $\mathcal{C}=0$ and the expression collapses to part (iv)’s
$-1/(1-\tau)-(1-\phi)\alpha/\kappa_W$.*

*Public provision removes both properties. It restores $g$ to (N$'''$),
and with it every primitive the corner had eliminated; and it reverses
signs rather than perturbing them. Part (iii) shows the reversal for
reinvestment. It happens for the worker’s own instrument too: at
$\phi=0.5$ and the primitives of (iii),
$\partial\ln\nu^\ast/\partial\phi=+0.179$ at $\theta=1$ and $-0.323$ at
$\theta=0.6$, with $\theta$ the only difference between the two
evaluations. The tax is the exception, and part (v) proves it rather
than sampling it: its sign survives public provision at every $\theta$
and everywhere on the admissible box. The asymmetry is structural, not
numerical. Reinvestment’s indirect channel carries a free $1/i$
multiplying a positive term, so it can be made to dominate the direct
one; the tax’s divergent channel is the $1/\tau$ inside the same
bracket, and that bracket enters multiplied by $-(1-\mathcal{C})$.
Because $\gamma(1-\theta)$ is one of the summands of
$(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)$, $\mathcal{C}$ lies below one by
construction, and the tax’s indirect channel is therefore capped by the
very derivative it would have to overcome. Two of the three instruments
lose their sign once public provision enters; the third cannot.*

*Proof.* **(i)** At a stationary class ratio $g_C=g_W=g$, so Assumption
2 gives $c_C=(g/B)^{1/\gamma}$, and
$c_C=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha Ax^{\ast\alpha}/\nu^\ast$ gives
$\nu^\ast=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha Ax^{\ast\alpha}(B/g)^{1/\gamma}$. Taking
logarithms and substituting the rest-point condition
$\ln g=\ln B+\gamma\theta\ln(\kappa_W Ax^{\ast\alpha})+\gamma(1-\theta)\ln z^\ast$
to eliminate $\ln B-\ln g$,
$$\ln\nu^\ast=\ln\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha\big]+\ln(Ax^{\ast\alpha})
  -\theta\ln(\kappa_W Ax^{\ast\alpha})-(1-\theta)\ln z^\ast .$$ The two
$\ln(Ax^{\ast\alpha})$ terms collect with coefficient $1-\theta$, and
$z^\ast=\phi\tau\alpha Ax^{\ast\alpha}/(g-1+\delta_G)$ from Theorem
2(ii) gives $Ax^{\ast\alpha}/z^\ast=(g-1+\delta_G)/(\phi\tau\alpha)$, in
which $x^\ast$ does not appear. Collecting the terms yields (N$'''$).
The cancellation is exact rather than approximate, and its source is the
model’s own asymmetry: output per worker enters the capitalist’s
consumption and the worker’s private consumption with the *same*
elasticity, so it survives into the ratio only through the mismatch
$1-\theta$ between the worker’s private and public arguments, and
vanishes with it. At $\theta=1$ the $(1-\theta)$ term of $H$ is absent
and $H(g)=\ln g-\ln B-\gamma\ln(\kappa_W Ax(g)^\alpha)$, still
continuous and strictly increasing on $g>1-\delta$ with the same two
limits, so it still has exactly one root. What that does *not* deliver
is interiority: Assumption 4 asks for $g>1-\delta_G$ as well, and at
$\theta=1$ the public stock has left $H$ entirely, so nothing in $H$
enforces it. Interiority at $\theta=1$ is therefore a hypothesis of this
theorem, as its statement has it, and not a consequence of the root’s
existence.

**(ii)** Differentiate the logarithm of (N$'''$) in $i$ at fixed $\tau$,
$\phi$ and primitives. The first factor contributes $-1/(1-i)$ and the
second $(1-\theta)(g-1+\delta_G)^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}i$. The
only $i$-dependence of $H$ is the additive
