$-\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln i$ inherited from $x^\ast(g)$, so
implicit differentiation of $H(g)=0$ gives
$\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}i=\frac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)\,i\,H'(g)}$,
strictly positive because $H'>0$ by Theorem 2(ii). Substituting gives
the display. Note what the derivation did *not* need: the
general-equilibrium response of $x^\ast$ cancels in (i) before any
differentiation, so no term of $H'$ other than the one carrying
$1-\theta$ survives into $S$.

**(iii)** At $\theta=1$, $S\equiv0$ and the display is $-1/(1-i)$,
negative on $i\in(0,1)$; equivalently (N$'''$) is then free of $g$, so
only the retention factor moves. At $\theta<1$ the two stated cells are
interior and admissible under Assumptions 1 and 4 and carry opposite
signs, which refutes constancy of sign; the intermediate value theorem
then places a zero between them.

The cell at which *no* change occurs needs a different instrument,
because what is refuted there — that every $\theta<1$ cell contains a
change — requires a universal in $i$, and no sample delivers one. A
cover does. Both the first and the third term of
$$(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)\;=\;\frac{g-1+\delta_G}{g}
  \;+\;\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\cdot\frac{g-1+\delta_G}{g-1+\delta}\;+\;\gamma(1-\theta)$$
are strictly positive on the admissible box, so dropping them can only
lower the denominator of $S$:
$$S\;\le\;\frac{(1-\theta)\,(g-1+\delta)}{i\,(g-1+\delta_G)}.$$ The
remaining $i$ is not free. On the share side of Proposition 6 the only
$\ln i$ carries the coefficient $\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$, and on the
growth side so does the only $\ln(g-1+\delta)$; matching them in
$\mathcal{H}(g)=\mathcal{P}$ therefore makes
$$\ln\!\Big[\frac{g-1+\delta}{i}\Big]\;=\;\frac{1-\alpha}{\gamma\alpha}
  \Big[\mathcal{P}_{\!-i}-\ln g-\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)\Big]$$
a function of $g$ alone, where $\mathcal{P}_{\!-i}$ collects
$\mathcal{P}$’s $i$-free terms. Its derivative is
$-\big[1/g+\gamma(1-\theta)/(g-1+\delta_G)\big](1-\alpha)/(\gamma\alpha)$,
strictly negative on the admissible box, so $(g-1+\delta)/i$ falls in
$g$ while $g-1+\delta_G$ rises in it. Both movements push the bound up
as $g$ falls, and by Theorem 2(ii) $g$ falls to
$1-\min(\delta,\delta_G)$ exactly as $i\to0$. **The bound is therefore
attained in the limit and nowhere inside $(0,1)$**, so its value there
is a supremum and not a sampled maximum.

None of that step is specific to a cell, and taking it at a general one
is what yields the equivalence in the statement. On $\delta<\delta_G$
the two dropped terms stay bounded as $g\to1-\delta$ while the one
retained diverges, so the ratio of $S$ to the bound tends to one: the
supremum is not merely an upper bound on $S$ but $\lim_{i\to0}S$ itself,
in the closed form displayed above. Since $1/(1-i)>1$ on $(0,1)$ while
$S$ never reaches its supremum there, $\lim_{i\to0}S\le1$ makes
$S-1/(1-i)$ strictly negative at every $i$, and $\lim_{i\to0}S>1$ makes
it positive near zero and $-\infty$ as $i\to1$, so the intermediate
value theorem places a change. Those two cases are exhaustive and
$\ln\lim_{i\to0}S$ is affine in $\ln B$ with slope
$(1-\alpha)/(\gamma\alpha)>0$, so they are exactly $B\le B^\ast$ and
$B>B^\ast$, with $B^\ast$ obtained by solving $\lim_{i\to0}S=1$ for $B$
— the display in the statement. The knife-edge $B=B^\ast$ falls on the
no-change side, since a supremum that is approached and not attained
cannot be met by $1/(1-i)>1$.

At the stated cell, which is one instance of that partition, with
$A=B=1$ — the calibration at which the quoted $-0.962$ is reproduced —
that supremum is $0.03824$ to four figures against $B^\ast=2.234$, while
$1/(1-i)\ge1$ throughout; the difference cannot vanish, and the
conclusion does not depend on where the sample points were placed.

**(iv)** Two routes reach the closed form, and the shorter one is also
the more general. Setting $\theta=1$ in (N$'''$) makes the exponent
$1-\theta$ vanish, which eliminates $g$ and leaves
$\nu^\ast=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha/\kappa_W$; since $g$ is the only symbol in
(N$'''$) through which $A$, $B$, $\gamma$, $\delta$ or $\delta_G$ could
enter, all five are gone with it. But that route carries Assumption 2’s
outer map along for no reason. Directly: at $\theta=1$ the public stock
leaves the worker’s reproduction map, so his index is $c_W$ — this much
is Assumption 2’s parametrisation of the corner and is not dispensable,
since $\theta$ is defined there; an interior stationary class ratio is
$g_C=g_W$ by $(\star)$; and Lemma 1 — whose hypothesis is only that a
common $F$ be strictly increasing, and which is proved for arbitrary $F$
— gives $c_W=c_C$, which is Proposition 2(i)’s (N). Assumption 1’s
budget writes the two sides as $c_W=\kappa_W Ax^{\alpha}$ and
$c_C=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha Ax^{\alpha}/\nu$, and $Ax^{\alpha}$ cancels
between them, leaving $\nu^\ast=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha/\kappa_W$ uniquely.
**No reproduction primitive enters that chain at any step**, which is
why the invariance is a statement about Lemma 1 and the budget rather
than about the Cobb–Douglas composite. Differentiating the logarithm of
that expression gives the three displayed derivatives, using
$\partial\kappa_W/\partial\tau=(1-\phi)\alpha$ and
$\partial\kappa_W/\partial\phi=-\tau\alpha$. Their signs follow from
$\kappa_W\ge 1-\alpha>0$, which holds because $(1-\phi)\tau\alpha\ge0$
on Assumption 1’s boxes: the first derivative is negative because $i<1$;
the second is a sum of a strictly negative term and a non-positive one;
the third is positive because $\tau>0$. The reversal for the split is
one proved value against one witness — the displayed
$\tau\alpha/\kappa_W$ is positive at $\theta=1$ by the same argument,
and the $\theta=0.6$ value is computed at the same $\phi$ and the same
primitives, so $\theta$ is the only difference.

**(v)** By (N$'''$),
$\ln\nu^\ast=\ln(1-i)+\ln(1-\tau)+\ln\alpha-\theta\ln\kappa_W
+(1-\theta)\big[\ln(g-1+\delta_G)-\ln(\phi\tau\alpha)\big]$, so $\tau$
reaches $\nu^\ast$ directly through $1-\tau$, through $\kappa_W$ and
through $\ln\tau$, and indirectly through $g$:
$$\frac{\partial\ln\nu^\ast}{\partial\tau}
 =-\frac{1}{1-\tau}-\theta\frac{(1-\phi)\alpha}{\kappa_W}-\frac{1-\theta}{\tau}
  +\frac{1-\theta}{g-1+\delta_G}\,\frac{\partial g}{\partial\tau}.$$ The
last term needs no new derivation: it is Proposition 6(ii). There
$g=\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})$ with $\mathcal{H}$ a strictly
increasing bijection, so
$\partial g/\partial\tau=(\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau)/\mathcal{H}'(g)$,
and $\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau$ is the expression already
displayed in Section 8, $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}
 =\gamma\Big[\theta\frac{(1-\phi)\alpha}{\kappa_W}+\frac{1-\theta}{\tau}
   -\frac{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}{1-\tau}\Big].$$ Its first two terms are the
same bracket that stands directly above, which is what makes the two
occurrences combine into the factor $1-\mathcal{C}$; its third comes
from $\mathcal{P}$’s
$\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha A\big)$ — capital
is accumulated out of **after-tax** income — and supplies the
$\mathcal{C}\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ term. Substituting gives the display in
(v). One caution about that derivative, since it is easy to lose:
$\kappa_W$ depends on $\tau$ as well as on $\phi$, and Proposition 6’s
shorthand $\kappa_W(\phi)$ names only the second argument; reading it as
$\tau$-free would delete the $\theta$ term above. For the bound,
differentiate Proposition 6’s growth side:
$\mathcal{H}'(g)=1/g+\gamma\alpha/\big[(1-\alpha)(g-1+\delta)\big]+\gamma(1-\theta)/(g-1+\delta_G)$.
Multiplying by $g-1+\delta_G$ returns $\gamma(1-\theta)$ from the third
term — the public input’s own term in $\mathcal{H}$ — and the two
displayed remainders from the first two, each strictly positive on the
admissible box, so $\mathcal{C}<1$. Both terms of the display are then
strictly positive, and the derivative is their negative. The bound is
attained nowhere but approached: $\mathcal{C}=0.9985$ at $\alpha=0.1$,
$\gamma=0.9$, $\theta=0.1$, $\delta=1$, $\delta_G=0.1$ and $g=0.901$ — a
growth factor a hair above the public floor $1-\delta_G$, where the
$\ln g$ term’s remainder $(g-1+\delta_G)/g$ is a tenth of a per cent —
so the cap binds tightly rather than generously. At the closed endpoint
$\theta=1$ the argument is made by evaluation and not by a limit, which
matters because that is the only place strictness could fail: there
$\gamma(1-\theta)$ vanishes, so $\mathcal{C}=0$ while
$(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)$ stays strictly positive, and the display
reduces to part (iv)’s $-1/(1-\tau)-(1-\phi)\alpha/\kappa_W$, already
proved from Lemma 1 and the budget. $\blacksquare$

At the corner the hypothesis is stronger than four of the five parts
need. Theorem 2$''$ below makes the class ratio at $\theta=1$ an
autonomous recursion whose limit is (N$'''$)’s corner form whatever the
two stocks do, so parts (i), (iii) and (iv) hold there without an
interior rest point in the stocks, and part (v), which collapses to
(iv)’s sign at $\theta=1$, holds with them. Only part (ii)’s
decomposition, written at the rest point’s own growth factor, needs that
rest point to exist — Theorem 2$''$(iii) says exactly when it does — and
at the corner it reduces to (iii) wherever it does.

The economics of (iii) is the paper’s own mechanism appearing on a
second margin. When the worker reproduces on private consumption alone,
capital deepening raises both classes’ reproduction inputs in the same
proportion, so it leaves their ratio untouched and only the capitalist’s
retained slice moves: more reinvestment, a smaller capitalist class,
always. Public provision breaks that proportionality, because the
worker’s index also carries a stock whose replacement cost moves with
growth. The residue is exactly $S$, and it is weighted by the same
$1-\theta$ that opens the private-consumption gap of Theorem 1. Set
$\theta=1$ and both effects vanish together: the wedge that lets policy
separate reproduction from private consumption is the wedge that makes
the size response ambiguous. What is not claimed is a comparative static
in the primitives — $S$ is evaluated at the rest point’s own $g$, and
$\tau$ and $\phi$ are held fixed throughout.

Theorem 2 and Proposition 6 both open at $\theta\in(0,1)$, and
Assumption 2 admits the endpoint. What happens there is not a limiting
case of either result but a structure of its own, and it is the one
place in the admissible set where the long-run state can fail to be
interior.

**Theorem 2$''$ (the no-social-wage corner is triangular: the class
ratio and the capital ratio are autonomous contractions, and the public
stock settles behind them exactly when the corner growth factor clears
its floor).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta=1$, and hold the
share policies $(\tau,\phi,i)$ fixed with $i\in(0,1)$, $\phi\tau>0$ and
$\tau<1$. Write*
$$\mathcal{H}_1(g) \;=\; \ln g + \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(g-1+\delta) \quad\text{on } g>1-\delta,
\qquad
\mathcal{P}_1 \;=\; \ln B + \gamma\ln(\kappa_W A) + \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln\!\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha A\big),$$
*the growth side and the share side of Proposition 6 at $\theta=1$, the
third term of each having vanished with the public input. Then:*

*(i) **The class ratio is an autonomous contraction with constant
forcing.** At every state,*
$$\ln\nu' \;=\; (1-\gamma)\ln\nu \;+\; \gamma\ln\frac{(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha}{\kappa_W},$$
*so from every $\nu_0>0$, and whatever the two stocks do, $\ln\nu_t$
converges monotonically at rate $1-\gamma$ to $\ln\nu^\ast$ with
$\nu^\ast=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha/\kappa_W$ — the corner form of (N$'''$),
reached here without a rest point in the stocks.*

*(ii) **The capital ratio is an autonomous contraction.** $x'$ depends
on $x$ alone, and in $\ln x$ the map has slope*
$$\frac{\partial\ln x'}{\partial\ln x} \;=\; \alpha(1-\gamma) + s_x(1-\alpha)
  \;\in\; \big[\alpha(1-\gamma),\ 1-\alpha\gamma\big)$$ *at every state,
$s_x\in[0,1)$ being the share of next period’s capital inherited from
the surviving old stock. It is therefore a contraction of modulus
$1-\alpha\gamma$ with a positive slope: from every $x_0>0$, $\ln x_t$
converges monotonically to $\ln x_1$, where*
$$x_1 \;=\; \left(\frac{i(1-\tau)\alpha A}{g_1-1+\delta}\right)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},
\qquad \mathcal{H}_1(g_1)=\mathcal{P}_1,$$ *and $g_1$ exists and is
unique at every share profile, because $\mathcal{H}_1$ is a strictly
increasing bijection of $(1-\delta,\infty)$ onto $\mathbb{R}$.*

*(iii) **The public stock is a follower, and it settles exactly when the
corner growth factor clears its floor.** An interior rest point of
$(\star)$ exists if and only if $g_1>1-\delta_G$; it is then unique,
equal to $(x_1,\nu^\ast,z_1)$ with
$z_1=\phi\tau\alpha A\,x_1^{\alpha}/(g_1-1+\delta_G)$, and every path
converges to it. Where $\delta_G\ge\delta$ the condition holds at every
profile. Where $\delta_G<\delta$ it holds if and only if
$\mathcal{P}_1>\mathcal{H}_1(1-\delta_G)$, which is a floor on net
reinvestment:*
$$i(1-\tau) \;>\; \underline{\iota} \;\equiv\; \frac{\delta-\delta_G}{\alpha A}
   \left(\frac{1-\delta_G}{B\,(\kappa_W A)^{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1-\alpha}{\gamma\alpha}} .$$
*The floor bites: $\underline{\iota}$ is free of $i$, so the condition
fails at every profile with $i<\underline{\iota}/(1-\tau)$, an open set;
at such a profile $x_t$ and $\nu_t$ still converge as in (i)–(ii), and
$z_t$ is unbounded.*

*(iv) **Where it exists, the rest point is stable with a real,
non-negative spectrum.** At $(x_1,z_1)$ the Jacobian of Theorem 2(iii)
is lower triangular,*
$$\mathcal{J} \;=\; \begin{pmatrix} a(1-\alpha)+\alpha(1-\gamma) & 0 \\[2pt] \alpha(1-c)-\alpha\gamma & c \end{pmatrix},
\qquad a=\frac{1-\delta}{g_1},\quad c=\frac{1-\delta_G}{g_1},$$ *so the
spectrum of the full system is
$\{a(1-\alpha)+\alpha(1-\gamma),\ c,\ 1-\gamma\}\subset[0,1)$: real,
non-negative and inside the unit disc. No oscillatory mode exists at the
corner, and Theorem 2(iii)’s conclusion holds there for a stronger
reason — the characteristic polynomial factors.*

*Proof.* **(i)** By $(\star)$, $\ln\nu'=\ln\nu+\ln g_C-\ln g_W$. At
$\theta=1$ Assumption 2 gives
$\ln g_W=\ln B+\gamma\ln(\kappa_W A x^{\alpha})$ and
$\ln g_C=\ln B+\gamma\ln\!\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha A x^{\alpha}\big]-\gamma\ln\nu$,
so the two $\ln x$ terms cancel and the displayed recursion follows: the
forcing term of the class-ratio recursion, which in general moves with
$(x,z)$, is here a constant. Since $1-\gamma\in(0,1)$, the map
$\ln\nu\mapsto\ln\nu'$ is an increasing contraction of $\mathbb{R}$, so
its orbits converge monotonically to its unique fixed point, which is
the displayed $\ln\nu^\ast$; that value is finite because $i<1$,
$\tau<1$ and $\kappa_W\ge1-\alpha>0$.

**(ii)** At $\theta=1$, $g_W$ depends on $x$ alone, so
$x'=\mathcal{N}_x(x)/g_W(x)$ with
$\mathcal{N}_x=(1-\delta)x+i(1-\tau)\alpha Ax^{\alpha}$. Differentiating
in logarithms,
$\partial\ln\mathcal{N}_x/\partial\ln x=s_x+\alpha(1-s_x)$ with
$s_x=(1-\delta)x/\mathcal{N}_x$, and
$\partial\ln g_W/\partial\ln x=\alpha\gamma$; subtracting gives the
displayed slope, and $s_x\in[0,1)$ — the inflow is strictly positive —
places it in $[\alpha(1-\gamma),\,1-\alpha\gamma)$. A $C^1$ self-map of
$\mathbb{R}$ whose derivative lies in $(0,L]$ with $L=1-\alpha\gamma<1$
is an increasing contraction, so it has exactly one fixed point and
every orbit converges to it monotonically. At the fixed point
$\mathcal{N}_x=g_Wx$, that is
$(1-\delta)+i(1-\tau)\alpha Ax_1^{\alpha-1}=g_1$ with
$g_1\equiv g_W(x_1)>1-\delta$, which is the displayed $x_1$;
substituting it into $\ln g_1=\ln B+\gamma\ln(\kappa_WAx_1^{\alpha})$
gives
$\ln g_1=\ln B+\gamma\ln(\kappa_WA)+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\big[\ln\!\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha A\big)-\ln(g_1-1+\delta)\big]$,
and moving every term in $g_1$ to the left gives
$\mathcal{H}_1(g_1)=\mathcal{P}_1$; the substitution is reversible, so
fixed points of the map and roots of $\mathcal{H}_1=\mathcal{P}_1$ on
$(1-\delta,\infty)$ correspond one to one.
$\mathcal{H}_1'(g)=1/g+\gamma\alpha/\big[(1-\alpha)(g-1+\delta)\big]>0$,
$\mathcal{H}_1\to-\infty$ as $g\downarrow1-\delta$ through its second
term, and $\mathcal{H}_1\to+\infty$ as $g\to\infty$, so $\mathcal{H}_1$
is a bijection onto $\mathbb{R}$ and $g_1$ is its unique root at every
profile.

**(iii)** The public stock’s law in $(\star)$ is
$z'=\big[(1-\delta_G)z+\phi\tau\alpha Ax^{\alpha}\big]/g_W(x)$. A rest
point of $(\star)$ has $x=x_1$ by (ii) and $\nu=\nu^\ast$ by (i), and
needs $z'=z$ with $z>0$ at $x_1$, that is
$z\,(g_1-1+\delta_G)=\phi\tau\alpha Ax_1^{\alpha}>0$, which has a
positive solution if and only if $g_1>1-\delta_G$, and then exactly the
displayed $z_1$; criterion (A) holds because $g_1$ then exceeds both
floors. If $\delta_G\ge\delta$ then $1-\delta_G\le1-\delta<g_1$ at every
profile. If $\delta_G<\delta$ then $1-\delta_G$ lies in the domain of
$\mathcal{H}_1$, which is increasing, so $g_1>1-\delta_G$ if and only if
$\mathcal{P}_1=\mathcal{H}_1(g_1)>\mathcal{H}_1(1-\delta_G)=\ln(1-\delta_G)+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(\delta-\delta_G)$;
multiplying by $\frac{1-\alpha}{\gamma\alpha}>0$ and exponentiating
turns this into
$i(1-\tau)\alpha A\,\big[B(\kappa_WA)^{\gamma}\big]^{\frac{1-\alpha}{\gamma\alpha}}
 >(\delta-\delta_G)(1-\delta_G)^{\frac{1-\alpha}{\gamma\alpha}}$, which
is the displayed floor. It does not contain $i$, so every
$i\in\big(0,\underline{\iota}/(1-\tau)\big)$ violates it. *Convergence.*
Write $z_{t+1}=c_tz_t+b_t$ with
$c_t=(1-\delta_G)/g_W(x_t)\to c=(1-\delta_G)/g_1$ and
$b_t=\phi\tau\alpha Ax_t^{\alpha}/g_W(x_t)\to b>0$, both limits by (ii).
Where $g_1>1-\delta_G$, $c<1$: pick $\bar c\in(c,1)$ and $T$ with
$c_t\le\bar c$ for $t\ge T$; then
$|z_{t+1}-z_1|\le\bar c\,|z_t-z_1|+|c_t-c|\,z_1+|b_t-b|$ for $t\ge T$, a
contraction with a vanishing perturbation, so $z_t\to z_1$. Where
$g_1\le1-\delta_G$, $c\ge1$: if $c>1$ then $z_{t+1}\ge\tfrac{1+c}{2}z_t$
for all large $t$ and $z_t$ grows geometrically; if $c=1$, suppose
$z_t\le M$ for every $t$ and take $T$ with $c_t\ge1-b/(4M)$ and
$b_t\ge3b/4$ for $t\ge T$; then $z_{t+1}\ge z_t+b/2$ for $t\ge T$,
contradicting the bound. So $z_t$ is unbounded, while (i)–(ii) are
untouched by $z$.

**(iv)** Write $\mathcal{N}_z=(1-\delta_G)z+\phi\tau\alpha Ax^{\alpha}$
for the numerator of $z'$ in $(\star)$, with $\mathcal{N}_x$ and $s_x$
as in (ii) and $s_z=(1-\delta_G)z/\mathcal{N}_z$ the share of next
period’s public stock inherited from the surviving old stock. Since each
inflow is proportional to $x^{\alpha}$ and $\mathcal{N}_x$ does not
contain $z$, $\partial\ln\mathcal{N}_x/\partial\ln z=0$,
$\partial\ln\mathcal{N}_z/\partial\ln x=\alpha(1-s_z)$ and
$\partial\ln\mathcal{N}_z/\partial\ln z=s_z$. At $\theta=1$,
$\partial\ln g_W/\partial\ln x=\alpha\gamma$ and
$\partial\ln g_W/\partial\ln z=0$, so subtracting the elasticities of
$g_W$ from those of the numerators gives
$\partial\ln x'/\partial\ln z=0$, $\partial\ln z'/\partial\ln z=s_z$,
$\partial\ln x'/\partial\ln x=s_x+\alpha(1-s_x)-\alpha\gamma$ and
$\partial\ln z'/\partial\ln x=\alpha(1-s_z)-\alpha\gamma$. At the rest
point $\mathcal{N}_x=g_1x_1$ and $\mathcal{N}_z=g_1z_1$, so $s_x=a$ and
$s_z=c$, which is the displayed matrix, and
$a+\alpha(1-a)-\alpha\gamma=a(1-\alpha)+\alpha(1-\gamma)$. The
eigenvalues of a triangular matrix are its diagonal:
$a(1-\alpha)+\alpha(1-\gamma)\in[\alpha(1-\gamma),\,1-\alpha\gamma)$
since $a\in[0,1)$, and $c\in[0,1)$ since $g_1>1-\delta_G$. The third
eigenvalue is $1-\gamma$ by (i). All three are real and lie in $[0,1)$,
so the rest point is locally asymptotically stable, no complex pair
exists to leave the unit circle, and no eigenvalue is negative.
$\blacksquare$

At the corner the reduced system is not merely stable but triangular.
The class ratio and the capital ratio each converge from every start, by
their own contractions, and neither sees the public stock. What can fail
is only the public stock’s own ratio, and it fails for a reason the
corner makes visible. With $\theta<1$ the public stock’s pole is one of
the three terms that drive $\mathcal{H}$ to $-\infty$ at the floor, so
the growth condition always has a root above both depreciation floors;
at $\theta=1$ that term is gone, and the only pole left is capital’s.
Where the public stock depreciates at least as fast as capital,
capital’s floor is the binding one and nothing changes. Where the public
stock is the more durable of the two, its floor sits above capital’s,
and whether the corner’s growth factor clears it is decided on the share
side alone, $\mathcal{P}_1>\mathcal{H}_1(1-\delta_G)$, and
$\mathcal{P}_1$ carries the three instruments through two coefficients:
net reinvestment $i(1-\tau)$, which raises it, and the worker’s
consumption coefficient $\kappa_W$, which the split lowers. So the split
enters the floor and nothing else, and it enters the wrong way — with
the public input reproduction-irrelevant, revenue directed to the stock
is revenue taken from the consumption that drives growth, and
$\partial\ln\underline{\iota}/\partial\phi=(1-\alpha)\tau/\kappa_W>0$: a
higher split raises the floor the stock it feeds must clear. Below
$\underline{\iota}$ the economy grows more slowly than the stock decays,
and a stock that nothing in the corner’s economy uses accumulates
without bound relative to the workforce it cannot benefit — provision
funded by the tax, sustained by the split, and inert. The threshold is a
closed form in the primitives, as $B^\ast$ of Theorem 2$'$(iii) is:
there the reproduction scale decides whether reinvestment’s sign turns,
and here it enters the floor that decides whether the corner’s public
stock settles at all.

Two consequences follow for results stated at $\theta\in(0,1]$. Theorem
2$'$ takes an interior rest point as given; at the corner, part (iii)
says exactly when one exists, and part (i) says that Theorem 2$'$’s
parts (i) and (iii) — the closed form
$\nu^\ast=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha/\kappa_W$ and the sign $-1/(1-i)$ — need
no rest point in the stocks at all, since $\nu^\ast$ is the limit of an
autonomous recursion whatever $z$ does. And the exception Section 1
states is a theorem rather than a description: inside the constant-share
class the long-run state can fail to be interior only at the corner,
only where the public stock outlasts capital, and only below the floor
on net reinvestment displayed above.

**Computation 2 (where the cycle lives, and who declines it).** *The
Jacobian of Theorem 2 holds the shares fixed. Under a Markov rule the
policy response enters the linearisation and the bound is not weakened
but false, and at the reported cells the cheap crossing is a fold — a
real root at $+1$, which duplicates or destroys rest points
monotonically. The genuine oscillation is the flip, and it is dear: an
elasticity of $-21.75$ at the illustrative cell and between $-11$ and
$-13$ at the three computed equilibria. Across the scanned range the
feedback spectrum never leaves the reals, so a Neimark–Sacker never
becomes a candidate. In the split the flip is unreachable rather than
merely expensive: the split sits at the corner $\phi^\ast=1$, where a
power rule is a share only on a band that closes as the anchor
approaches one, and along the computed paths — all of which approach
their rest points monotonically from below — a counter-cyclical rule is
never triggered. Priced over sixteen hundred rules at each of the three
equilibria, no state-contingent split beats the flat equilibrium rule.
In the tax the answer changes. The tax is interior, so a rule anchored
on it is admissible in both directions, and at the second equilibrium a
counter-cyclical tax rule settles on a period-two orbit worth one per
cent of discounted payoff against the flat rule. Such orbits exist at
all three equilibria and pay at two of them. On the orbit the two
reproduction indices stop equalising period by period — $\ln(g_W/g_C)$
alternates by $\pm0.032$ where at a rest point it vanishes to
$3\times10^{-11}$ — and the private gap runs between $1.82$ and $2.07$
around the $1.94$ of the equilibrium it orbits. But no class chooses it.
At every cell and both starts the payoff-maximising rule in the enlarged
family is stable, never the oscillation, and where a cycle pays a stable
rule in the same family is worth roughly twice as much. Nor does the
cycle survive being chosen: asked for his best reply to the announced
rule, the capitalist cuts his reinvestment rate from $0.395$ to $0.205$,
the orbit’s amplitude falls to machine precision, and the worker’s one
per cent becomes a loss of thirty. The same reversal occurs at the other
two equilibria, and against the capitalist’s own feedback family with
its level free. The capitalist’s mirror family contains no profitable
cycle at any of the three computed equilibria, over the rules priced
here. This computation was not re-run under (O) — G.1$'$ says so — and
the profiles it prices are therefore the dynastic sweep’s: two of the
three coincide with the profiles above, and the second is
$(0.60,1,0.395)$ where Computation 1 now reports $(0.61,1,0.390)$. The
rates quoted here are at that profile.*

An identity bounds what any such cycle can do. Because
$\nu' = \nu\,g_C/g_W$, a class ratio that returns to itself after $p$
periods forces $\prod_{t}(g_C/g_W) = 1$ over the period: the two
reproduction indices equalise in the cycle average exactly, whatever the
amplitude. On any path that merely keeps the ratio bounded above and
away from zero rather than periodic the same telescoping drives the
average gap to zero in the limit — bounded alone will not do, a ratio
decaying geometrically to zero leaving a gap equal to its own decay
rate. A bounded cycle can therefore break Lemma 1 period by period and
cannot break it on average — the escape is confined to within-cycle
timing. The admissibility conditions are reached by a second
telescoping, and not by that identity. The substitution behind Section
8’s Step 4 — $P=C_C^{1-\omega}K^{\omega}$ with
$C_C=(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha Y$, $K=xN_W$ and $Y=Ax^{\alpha}N_W$ — gives
$\ln P_t=(1-\omega)\ln\big[(1-i)(1-\tau_t)\alpha A\big]+\zeta\ln x_t+\ln N_{W,t}$
with $\zeta=\alpha+\omega(1-\alpha)$; on an orbit that closes in the
state, and hence under a state-contingent rule in the shares as well,
the first two terms return to their starting values, so $g_P$ and $g_W$
share a geometric mean over the cycle and the two clauses of (A5) can
differ only through their exponents. The $\nu$-identity cannot deliver
that conclusion: it constrains $g_C/g_W$, and $g_C$ is absent from the
capitalist’s clause.

The exclusion of the cycle from the constant-share class does not depend
on the functional form either: the argument uses only that the output
elasticity is below one, that $a,c\in(0,1)$, and that the reproduction
map’s elasticities sum to less than one, so it survives a CES composite
under a saturating outer map. So the bifurcation route to a
distributional cycle is shut inside the class in which this paper closes
its equilibrium, and open in the model at large. The cycle has to be
bought in the strategy space; the cycles found are period-doublings
rather than the invariant curve the question was originally posed about;
and at the reported equilibria it is bought in one of the worker’s two
instruments and not in the other, is profitable in the worker’s family
at two of three equilibria and in the capitalist’s at none, is chosen in
neither class, and at none of them would survive the other class’s reply
if it were.

Read back to Section 5, Theorem 2 closes the first of Lemma 1’s three
exits inside the constant-share class, and closes it twice over, since a
unique rest point also rules out the path dependence that multiple
interior attractors would give. Outside that class the first exit is not
closed but realised: the tax orbit breaks the period-by-period
equalisation outright, though within a band around the stationary wedge
rather than away from it, and only period by period. The other two exits
— lumpiness and prising the object of interest apart from the index —
therefore still carry most of the weight.

**So what.** Relative class size is not a free variable, and it is not a
residue of accumulation. At any stationary configuration inside this
class the ratio is pinned by the fiscal shares and the growth factor
alone, and the capital stock has cancelled out of it: how much an
economy has accumulated does not settle how its population divides
between those who live on capital income and those who live on wages and
provision. The instruments and the growth rate settle that. Which way an
instrument pushes it, however, turns on whether public provision enters
reproduction at all. Where it does not, reinvestment shrinks the
capitalist class relative to the worker class at every *interior* rate —
a clean sign, but the sign of a corner, and one that stops at the ends
of the interval, since $i=1$ is admissible under Assumption 1 and the
derivative is not defined there. Once provision does enter, the same
instrument acquires no uniform sign, and the reversal occurs within a
single cell of the parameter space rather than between distant regions
of it. **Public provision is what turns the direction of that effect
into a question**, and the weight the channel carries is $1-\theta$,
provision’s own elasticity in the reproduction index. Finally, the class
relation cannot oscillate of its own accord: the ratio is a first-order
contraction with a *positive* coefficient at every state, so it carries
no cycle of its own, and whenever the two stocks settle and the
reinvestment rate is interior, it settles with them. Inside the
constant-share class no distributional cycle can be reached by local
bifurcation either. A cycle must therefore be bought in the strategy
space rather than found in the dynamics — and at the reported equilibria
neither class buys it. What is not shown, and is not claimed, is that no
orbit exists far from the rest point.

# 8. Existence, and the One-Shot Bridge

Section 6 characterises the equilibrium and Section 7 certifies the rest
point it settles on, and neither produces one. Definition 1 asks three
things of a profile: that it be admissible, that it induce an interior
rest point, and that neither class gain by deviating to another constant
share. Sections 6 and 7 supply conditions a candidate must satisfy. What
has been missing is the candidate.

Two obstructions blocked the obvious route, and both were structural
rather than technical. The stationary conditions send a profile to a
profile, so a fixed point of that map is the natural candidate — but
Theorem 2(ii) leaves the balanced-growth factor undefined on the faces
$i=0$ and $\phi\tau=0$, so the map is not total on the instrument box,
and Proposition 4(iii) records that nothing in Assumptions 1–5 forces
$\tau^\ast\le\bar\tau$, so its image was not known to land in the box
either. Proposition 6 removes the first by making the growth factor
explicit. Proposition 7 removes the second by replacing the
unconstrained root with the constrained maximiser it was standing in
for. Theorem 3 then runs the fixed-point argument on the whole box.
Section 8.4 turns to what that fixed point is a fixed point *of*, and
shows that the fixed balanced-growth factor at which every margin in
Section 6 is evaluated costs nothing: the one-shot margins are the total
ones.

## 8.1 The growth factor is an explicit inverse

**Proposition 6 (the rest-point growth factor in closed form).** *Let
Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$ and fix constant shares with
$i>0$, $\phi\tau>0$ and $\tau<1$. Write*
$$\mathcal{H}(g) \;=\; \ln g \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(g-1+\delta)
   \;+\; \gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G),$$
$$\mathcal{P}(\tau,\phi,i) \;=\; \ln B \;+\; \gamma\theta\ln\!\big(\kappa_W(\phi)A\big)
   \;+\; \gamma(1-\theta)\ln(\phi\tau\alpha A)
   \;+\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln\!\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha A\big).$$
*Then:*

*(i) **The rest-point condition separates.** Theorem 2(ii)’s condition
is exactly $\mathcal{H}(g)=\mathcal{P}(\tau,\phi,i)$, so
$H = \mathcal{H}-\mathcal{P}$ and the constant that theorem leaves
unnamed is $-\mathcal{P}$: every $g$ is on one side, every share on the
other.*

*(ii) **The growth factor is an explicit function of one scalar.**
$\mathcal{H}$ is a strictly increasing bijection from
$\big(\max(1-\delta,1-\delta_G),\ \infty\big)$ onto $\mathbb{R}$, so*
$$g(\tau,\phi,i) \;=\; \mathcal{H}^{-1}\big(\mathcal{P}(\tau,\phi,i)\big), \tag{13}$$
*continuous and strictly increasing in $\mathcal{P}$. Moreover
$\mathcal{P}\to-\infty$ as any of $\tau$, $\phi$, $i$ falls to zero, and
$\mathcal{P}$ is bounded above on the instrument box; so $g$ runs down
to the left endpoint at exactly the faces where Theorem 2(ii) has no
interior rest point, and is bounded above everywhere else.*

*(iii) **Properness is a condition on one number.** At a rest point
Assumption 5 holds if and only if* $$g \;>\; \beta^{1/(\sigma-1)} .$$
*Hence on any set of profiles satisfying
$\mathcal{P}\ge\mathcal{H}(g_\star)$ for some
$g_\star>\beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}$, every profile is proper, with
$\tilde\beta\le\beta g_\star^{1-\sigma}<1$ uniformly.*

*(iv) **The split has a growth optimum, and where it sits is free of the
whole dynamic block.** Fix $\tau$ and $i$ and let $\varpi\equiv\phi\tau$
range over $(0,\tau]$, so that
$\kappa_W=(1-\alpha)+\alpha(\tau-\varpi)$. Then*
$$\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\varpi} \;=\;
  \gamma\Big[\frac{1-\theta}{\varpi}\;-\;\frac{\theta\alpha}{\kappa_W}\Big] ,$$
*whose bracket is strictly decreasing in $\varpi$ and unbounded above as
$\varpi\downarrow0$. It therefore vanishes exactly once, at*
$$\varpi^{\circ} \;=\; \frac{(1-\theta)\big(1-\alpha+\alpha\tau\big)}{\alpha} ,$$
*and $g$ is **strictly single-peaked** in the split: strictly increasing
on whatever part of $(0,\tau]$ lies below $\varpi^{\circ}$ and strictly
decreasing on whatever part lies above, the second being empty when the
peak is not interior. The peak is interior to $(0,\tau]$ exactly when
$\alpha\theta\tau>(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)$, and sits at the corner $\phi=1$
otherwise. Consequently $\{\varpi:g(\varpi)>\bar g\}$ is an **interval**
for every $\bar g$, so by (iii) the splits at which a profile is proper
form an interval too. None of $\gamma$, $\delta$, $\delta_G$, $\beta$,
$\sigma$, $i$, $A$ or $B$ appears in $\varpi^{\circ}$, and
$\partial\varpi^{\circ}/\partial\theta=1-\tau-1/\alpha<0$. Finally, at a
proper profile (Assumption 5), every interior zero of Corollary 2’s
split map $\Psi$ lies **strictly below** $\phi_g=\varpi^{\circ}/\tau$,
the peak read in the split rather than in the receipt: a worker who
stops inside the box stops short of the growth peak, never past it.*

*Proof.* **(i)** At a rest point Theorem 2(ii) inverts both stock laws:
$x(g) = \big[i(1-\tau)\alpha A/(g-1+\delta)\big]^{1/(1-\alpha)}$ and
$z(g) = \phi\tau\alpha A\,x(g)^{\alpha}/(g-1+\delta_G)$, both positive
under $i>0$, $\phi\tau>0$ and Assumption 4. Substituting into
$\ln g = \ln g_W$ with
$\ln g_W = \ln B + \gamma\theta\ln(\kappa_W A x^{\alpha}) + \gamma(1-\theta)\ln z$,
the two $\ln x$ terms collect with coefficient
$\gamma\theta\alpha+\gamma(1-\theta)\alpha = \gamma\alpha$, and
$\ln x(g) = \big[\ln\!\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha A\big)-\ln(g-1+\delta)\big]/(1-\alpha)$.
Moving every term in $g$ to the left and every term in the shares to the
right gives $\mathcal{H}(g)=\mathcal{P}$.

**(ii)** $\mathcal{H}' = H' > 0$ is Theorem 2(ii), and the same
theorem’s limits give $\mathcal{H}\to-\infty$ at the left endpoint and
$\mathcal{H}\to+\infty$ as $g\to\infty$. A continuous strictly
increasing function with those limits is a bijection onto $\mathbb{R}$,
so $\mathcal{H}^{-1}$ exists and is continuous and strictly increasing,
which is (13). In $\mathcal{P}$ each of $\ln\tau$, $\ln\phi$ and $\ln i$
carries a strictly positive coefficient while $\kappa_W\ge1-\alpha>0$
throughout, so no term can offset the divergence at those three faces.
For the upper bound, $\kappa_W\le(1-\alpha)+\bar\tau\alpha$,
$\phi\tau\alpha\le\bar\tau\alpha$ and $i(1-\tau)\alpha\le\alpha$ on the
box.

**(iii)** With $\sigma>1$, $\tilde\beta = \beta g^{1-\sigma}<1$ if and
only if $g^{\sigma-1}>\beta$, which is the displayed inequality; at a
rest point $g_W=g_C=g$, so the two conditions of Assumption 5 coincide
and this is Assumption 5. If $\mathcal{P}\ge\mathcal{H}(g_\star)$ then
$g\ge g_\star$ by the monotonicity in (ii), and
$g\mapsto\beta g^{1-\sigma}$ is decreasing.

**(iv)** With $\tau$ and $i$ fixed the split reaches $\mathcal{P}$
through two terms only, since $\phi\tau\alpha A=\varpi\alpha A$ and
$\kappa_W=(1-\alpha)+\alpha(\tau-\varpi)$; the first and fourth terms
carry no $\varpi$. Differentiating gives the display. ⭕ **The first
derivative is not new here:** Corollary 2’s proof already carries it in
the split, as
$\mathcal{P}_\phi=\gamma\big[(1-\theta)/\phi-\theta\tau\alpha/\kappa_W\big]$,
and $\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\varpi=\mathcal{P}_\phi/\tau$ since
$\varpi=\phi\tau$ at fixed $\tau$. What that corollary does with it is
evaluate it *at a zero of $\Psi$*; what follows here is the **second**
differentiation, which is where the peak comes from. Both entries of the
bracket fall in $\varpi$ — the first because $\theta<1$, the second
because $\kappa_W$ falls at the constant rate $\alpha$ while staying at
or above $1-\alpha>0$ — so the bracket’s own derivative is
$-(1-\theta)/\varpi^{2}-\theta\alpha^{2}/\kappa_W^{2}<0$ and the bracket
is strictly decreasing. A strictly decreasing function that tends to
$+\infty$ at the left endpoint crosses zero at most once; substituting
$\varpi^{\circ}$ shows it does cross, so $\mathcal{P}$ rises and then
falls. By (13) $g$ is a strictly increasing function of $\mathcal{P}$,
and a strictly increasing transformation moves neither the location of a
peak nor the number of crossings of a level, which gives both the
single-peakedness and the interval. At $\varpi=\tau$, where $\phi=1$ and
$\kappa_W=1-\alpha$, the bracket equals
$\big[(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)-\alpha\theta\tau\big]/\big[\tau(1-\alpha)\big]$
with a strictly positive denominator, so the peak lies strictly inside
exactly when that numerator is negative. The absences are read off the
display, and each has its own reason: $\delta$, $\delta_G$, $\beta$ and
$\sigma$ never enter $\mathcal{P}$ at all; $i$ enters only the fourth
term, which carries no $\varpi$; $A$ and $B$ enter additively under the
logarithms and so do not survive the derivative; and $\gamma$ multiplies
**both** entries of the bracket, so it scales the derivative but cannot
move the zero. Only $\tau$ survives, through $\kappa_W$ as well as
through the upper endpoint. For the last clause, Corollary 2 shows that
at a zero of $\Psi$ the same $\mathcal{P}_\phi$ collapses to
$\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)/\phi$, which is strictly positive because
$\Omega<1$ by Proposition 3(i) at a proper profile — the one hypothesis
this clause adds to the proposition’s, and one Corollary 2 carries
already; a strictly positive $\mathcal{P}_\phi$ is a strictly positive
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\varpi$, and that holds exactly below
$\varpi^{\circ}$. $\blacksquare$

Part (iii) is what the instrument box needed. A fixed-point argument has
to be run away from the faces where the map is undefined, and no
construction of that shrinkage was available. Proposition 6 supplies
one, and supplies it in the right variable: the restriction is a single
lower bound on $g$, which by (13) is a single lower bound on
$\mathcal{P}$, which is a condition on the shares that can be read off
the primitives. It also says what shape the restriction must take. The
three lower faces are excluded because $\mathcal{P}$ diverges there; the
face $i=1$ is excluded for a different reason, because Theorem 2(i)’s
limit $\ln\nu^\ast=\Theta^\ast/\gamma$ carries
$\ln\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\big]$ and the class ratio leaves the interior;
and no upper bound on $\phi$ below one is called for at all. The
asymmetric box — floors on all three instruments, a ceiling on $i$ alone
— is therefore forced by the model rather than chosen to keep the
computed corner inside it.

Part (iv) says that funding the stock is not a margin that keeps paying.
Move a unit of the receipt from cash into the stock and two things
happen at once. The public leg of the worker’s continuation gains, at a
rate $(1-\theta)/\varpi$ that is large when little has been built and
falls as more is; the private leg loses, at a rate
$\theta\alpha/\kappa_W$ that is small when the cash share is generous
and rises as it is cut. One rate falls and the other rises, so they
cross once, and the crossing is the best the split can do for growth.
Below it the receipt buys more continuation as clinics than as cash;
above it the arithmetic reverses, and a state that keeps building past
$\varpi^{\circ}$ is buying provision with reproduction it cannot
recover.

Two things the crossing does not depend on are worth as much as its
location. The reproduction elasticity $\gamma$ multiplies both rates, so
it changes how sharply growth answers the split but not which way: there
is no $\gamma$ margin to find here, and a sweep that looks for one is
searching a coordinate the mechanism does not use. The public stock’s
own durability is absent for a different and stronger reason —
$\delta_G$ never enters $\mathcal{P}$, and it reaches $g$ only through
$\mathcal{H}^{-1}$, a strictly increasing map, which can raise or lower
the whole profile of $g$ but cannot move where that profile peaks. So
where a computation reports the chosen split answering to $\delta_G$,
that response is running through the worker’s valuation of the stock —
through $\Omega$ of (6) — and not through anything the stock does to the
growth factor. The two margins that move the corner are separated by
channel, and only one of them is structural.

At the reported cells the crossing is far outside the instrument box:
$\phi_g$ is $1.99$, $1.73$ and $1.53$ at the three equilibrium taxes
$0.51$, $0.61$ and $0.72$, so growth is strictly increasing in the split
across the whole of it and the corner is not a knife-edge. ⛔ **It is
tempting to read the $\theta$ margin as the explanation of the computed
corner-flip, and the temptation should be refused.** Holding those three
taxes fixed, raising $\theta$ to $0.80$ would pull the crossing inside
the box at all of them. But the tax does not hold fixed. At the cell
where the split actually goes interior — $\theta=0.80$ at
$\delta_G=0.05$ — the equilibrium tax falls to $0.34$, and there
$\phi_g=1.39$: the crossing is still outside the box, and growth is
still rising in the split everywhere the worker may go. **The growth
peak is not what moves that split.** What moves it is the part of the
problem $\varpi^{\circ}$ excludes — the discounted wedges $\Omega$ and
$J$ — which is the conclusion (iv) already forces for $\delta_G$ by an
absence, now reached for $\theta$ as well by a computation. (iv)’s
content is the structural channel and where it stops, not the location
of any chosen split.

And the two are not merely near one another. Corollary 2’s collapse puts
every interior stationary split **strictly below** $\phi_g$: a worker
who stops inside the box stops while the growth factor is still rising.
He is not balancing growth against something else at the margin — he is
declining growth he could still have, because the cash he would give up
to buy it costs him more, discounted, than the larger dynasty it
returns. The distance between the two splits is the price of impatience,
and it is exactly the part of the decision $\varpi^{\circ}$ does not
contain. ⚠ It is also a constraint the computed cells must satisfy: the
reported interior split at $\theta=0.80$ must sit below $\phi_g$ at that
cell’s own tax, and at $\tau=0.34$ that is $0.94$ against $1.39$.

<figcaption style="font-size:0.86em;line-height:1.45;color:#333;margin-top:0.5em"><strong>The growth-maximising split, and why the worker never reaches it.</strong> Growth answers the split of the capital tax through two rates that move against each other: provision gains at <em>(1&#8722;&#x3B8;)/&#x3D6;</em>, large when little has been built and falling as more is, while the private leg loses at <em>&#x3B8;&#x3B1;/&#x3BA;<sub>W</sub></em>, small when cash is generous and rising as it is cut. They cross once, at &#x3D6;&#x25E6;, where the receipt spent on provision per worker stands to the worker&#8217;s private claim as (1&#8722;&#x3B8;) stands to &#x3B8; &#8212; the two weights in his own reproduction composite. <strong>A.</strong> At the computed equilibrium the crossing sits above the whole instrument box (&#x3D5;&#x25E6; = 1.99), so growth rises across every split available and the worker takes the corner: the entire receipt is built, none of it paid as cash. <strong>B.</strong> Where the crossing is interior, growth rises to it and falls after it, and every interior stationary split lies strictly to its left &#8212; the worker stops while growth is still rising, declining growth he could still have because the cash he would surrender costs more, discounted, than the larger dynasty it returns. Shading marks the splits admissible under Assumption 5; the excluded ones are the lowest, which is where Proposition 6(iv) puts every interior stationary split. The dashed horizontal line is Assumption 5&#8217;s properness floor &#x3B2;<tspan font-size="8" dy="-3">1/(&#x3C3;&#8722;1)</tspan><tspan dy="3"></tspan>; in A the curve crosses it close to the left edge, which is Computation 1&#8217;s observation that the splits it has to exclude are the lowest ones. Panel A is Computation 1&#8217;s own equilibrium. Panel B is the paper&#8217;s &#x3B8; = 0.80 cell read at instruments inside the box rather than at that cell&#8217;s own equilibrium, because at every equilibrium whose tax this paper reports &#8212; 0.51, 0.61, 0.72, and 0.34 at the &#x3B8; = 0.80 cell &#8212; the crossing lies outside the box.</figcaption>
</figure>

<!-- FIG:prop6iv:END -->

How much of this is Cobb–Douglas? Less than it looks, and not the part
one would guess. Write the reproduction composite in its homogeneous
form $\mathsf{X}(c,z)=c\,\mathcal{F}(z/c)$ and put $\mathsf{r}=z/c$.
Along the budget the growth margin is positive exactly when
$$\mathsf{m}(\mathsf{r})\;\equiv\;
  \frac{\mathcal{F}'}{\mathcal{F}-\mathsf{r}\,\mathcal{F}'}\;>\;\alpha ,
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\mathsf{m}'(\mathsf{r})\;=\;\frac{\mathcal{F}\,\mathcal{F}''}
  {\big[\mathcal{F}-\mathsf{r}\,\mathcal{F}'\big]^{2}} .$$ So **strict
quasiconcavity alone** — $\mathcal{F}''<0$ — makes $\mathsf{m}$ fall,
allows one crossing, and delivers the single peak. No elasticity of
substitution enters, bounded or otherwise: the elasticity sets how fast
$\mathsf{m}$ rotates, never whether its level ever meets $\alpha$. What
Cobb–Douglas supplies is the *boundary*. There
$\mathsf{m}(\mathsf{r})=(1-\theta)/(\theta\mathsf{r})$, which diverges
as $\mathsf{r}\downarrow0$, so the crossing is always reached from
above. Drop that and the peak can fail to exist outright: at
$\mathcal{F}(\mathsf{r})=1+\tfrac{1}{10}\mathsf{r}+\tfrac{1}{10}\big(1-e^{-\mathsf{r}}\big)$
— smooth, increasing and strictly quasiconcave — with $\alpha=\tfrac12$,
$\mathsf{m}(0^{+})=\tfrac15<\alpha$ and provision never pays at the
margin at any split. **Uniqueness is quasiconcavity’s, existence is
Cobb–Douglas’s, and only the closed form needs both.**

⚠ **The reach of that stops one step short, and the step is the
reduction itself.** Both statements are about $\mathcal{P}$, and
$\mathcal{P}$ exists because the rest-point condition *separates* —
every $g$ on one side, every share on the other, which is (i).
Homogeneity alone does not buy the separation: it factors the scale out
of $\mathsf{X}$ and leaves $g$ inside the second argument through
$g-1+\delta_G$. For a composite homogeneous but not log-linear, the line
along which $\mathsf{X}$ is read has a slope that moves with $g$, and
the crossing argument stops closing on its own. **What is established is
that the shape needs no Cobb–Douglas once the condition separates — not
that the condition separates.**

Three primitives are left, and
$\varpi^{\circ}=(1-\theta)\big[1/\alpha-(1-\tau)\big]$ prices each. It
falls in $\theta$, which is the margin just discussed. It rises in the
tax, at rate $1-\theta$, while the *split* it recommends falls, at
$-(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)/(\alpha\tau^{2})$: a state that taxes more should
build a smaller fraction of a larger receipt, and the two movements are
not in tension because the receipt itself is growing faster than the
fraction shrinks. The third is the one worth reading twice. The peak
falls in the capital share, at $-(1-\theta)/\alpha^{2}$, so **the more
of the product that accrues to capital, the less of the receipt a
growth-maximising state routes into the stock.** The reason is not that
provision has become less productive — $\theta$ and the reproduction
technology have not moved. It is that the cash leg provision competes
with has become scarcer: $\kappa_W$ starts from $1-\alpha$, and every
unit diverted from a thinner wage share costs more reproduction than the
same unit cost before. An economy in which labour’s share has fallen is
one in which the funded share that maximises growth is smaller, and the
model returns that in a closed form carrying no dynamic primitive at
all. What it does not return, and what no rearrangement of it will
return, is a level of provision that guarantees the workforce
reproduces: $g_W\ge1$ is a joint restriction on $c_W$ and $z$ together
with the primitives, not a threshold in either one alone.

Proposition 6 makes the growth factor an explicit function of the
shares. Propositions 4 and 5 make the shares explicit functions of the
growth factor. Composing the two closes a loop that neither result
closes on its own, and it is that loop, rather than any rate condition,
which owns the derivative $\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}\gamma$ that
Proposition 5$'''$ leaves standing.

**Proposition 6$'$ (the share loop is a scalar loop, and its gain signs
the growth channel).** *Let Assumptions 1–5 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$
and $\omega<1$, at the corner $\phi=1$ with $i$ interior, and let $g$ be
a point at which the constrained tax $\min\{\tau^\ast,\bar\tau\}$ of
Proposition 7(iii) is differentiable — which excludes only the switch
itself, the $g$ at which (9) equals $\bar\tau$. By (6), (8), (9) and
(12) each of $\Omega$, $J$, $\tau^\ast$ and $i^\ast$ is a function of
$g$ and the primitives alone, and at $\phi=1$ the worker’s own wedge is
$\kappa_W(1)=1-\alpha$, free of $\tau$. So the map carrying a share pair
to the rest point it induces and back to the shares that rest point
induces acts through the single scalar*
$$\mathcal{L}(g) \;=\; \mathcal{H}^{-1}\Big(\mathcal{P}\big(\min\{\tau^\ast(g),\bar\tau\},\,1,\,
   i^\ast(g;\gamma)\big)\Big),
\tag{13$'$}$$ *whose fixed points are exactly the share pairs the loop
can select. Write $\mathcal{P}_g$ for the derivative of the composition
inside. Then:*

*(i) **The loop gain.** At a fixed point
$\mathcal{L}'(g)=\mathcal{P}_g/\mathcal{H}'(g)$, with $\mathcal{H}'>0$
by Theorem 2(ii), and the fixed point is locally attracting for the loop
exactly when $|\mathcal{L}'(g)|<1$.*

*(ii) **Three partials, each one line from Assumption 2.** At a rest
point,*
$$\frac{\partial(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{H})}{\partial\gamma} \;=\; \frac{\ln g-\ln B}{\gamma},
\qquad
\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial i} \;=\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)\,i},
\qquad
\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}\bigg|_{\phi=1} \;=\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{\tau}
   \;-\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)(1-\tau)} .$$

*(iii) **The comparative static.** At a fixed point with
$\mathcal{L}'(g)\neq1$,* $$\frac{\mathrm{d}g}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}
 \;=\; \frac{1}{\mathcal{H}'(g)\big(1-\mathcal{L}'(g)\big)}
   \left[\;\frac{\ln g-\ln B}{\gamma}
   \;+\;\frac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)\,i^\ast}\,
      \frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\gamma}\bigg|_{g}\;\right].
\tag{13$''$}$$ *Holding the shares fixed instead deletes the second
bracket term and replaces $\mathcal{H}'(1-\mathcal{L}')$ by
$\mathcal{H}'$, so the two derivatives differ in both factors and
neither difference is a normalisation. The second term is there because
$\gamma$ reaches $i^\ast$ directly, through $r=\tilde\beta\gamma\rho$,
while it reaches $\tau^\ast$ only through $g$ — which is Proposition
4(ii).*

*(iv) **The sign.** Both bracket terms are non-negative when $g\ge B$,
the second strictly positive by Proposition 5$''$. Hence*
$$g\ \ge\ B \quad\text{and}\quad \mathcal{L}'(g)<1
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \frac{\mathrm{d}g}{\mathrm{d}\gamma}\;>\;0,$$
*and the two channels of Proposition 5$'''$’s decomposition then
**oppose**. Local attraction of the loop supplies $\mathcal{L}'(g)<1$.
Where the ceiling binds, the constrained tax is the constant $\bar\tau$
and $\mathcal{P}_g$ reduces to its $i^\ast$ term, which is negative
under $(\ddagger)$ by Proposition 5$'''$; there $\mathcal{L}'(g)<0$ and
the second hypothesis costs nothing at all. Below $B$ the first bracket
term turns negative and the display determines nothing: the sign is then
a contest between the two terms.*

*Proof.* **(i)** By Proposition 6(ii), $\mathcal{H}$ is a strictly
increasing bijection with $\mathcal{H}'=H'>0$, so $\mathcal{H}^{-1}$ is
differentiable with derivative $1/\mathcal{H}'$, and the chain rule
gives
$\mathcal{L}'(g)=\mathcal{P}_g/\mathcal{H}'\big(\mathcal{L}(g)\big)$,
which at a fixed point is $\mathcal{P}_g/\mathcal{H}'(g)$. The
attraction criterion is the standard one for a $C^{1}$ map of one
variable.

**(ii)** Assumption 2 gives
$\ln g_W=\ln B+\gamma\big[\theta\ln c_W+(1-\theta)\ln z\big]$, in which
$\gamma$ appears only as that outer factor, so
$\partial\ln g_W/\partial\gamma=(\ln g_W-\ln B)/\gamma$; at a rest point
$\ln g_W=\ln g$, and $\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{P}=H=\ln g-\ln g_W$ by
Proposition 6(i). For the other two, Proposition 6’s proof collects the
two $\ln x$ terms with coefficient $\gamma\alpha$ and carries
$\ln x(g)=\big[\ln\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha A\big)-\ln(g-1+\delta)\big]/(1-\alpha)$,
so $\mathcal{P}$ contains $i$ only in
$\tfrac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha A\big)$ and, at
$\phi=1$, contains $\tau$ only in that term and in
$\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(\tau\alpha A)$, $\kappa_W(1)=1-\alpha$ carrying
none.

**(iii)** Write
$\Phi(g;\gamma)=\mathcal{H}(g)-\mathcal{P}\big(\tau^\ast(g),1,i^\ast(g;\gamma)\big)$,
whose zeros are the fixed points of $\mathcal{L}$ and whose
$g$-derivative is
$\mathcal{H}'-\mathcal{P}_g=\mathcal{H}'\big(1-\mathcal{L}'\big)$ by
(i). Differentiating $\Phi=0$ in $\gamma$, and using Proposition 4(ii) —
$\tau^\ast$ carries no $\gamma$ of its own —
$$\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\gamma}
 \;=\; \frac{\partial(\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{P})}{\partial\gamma}
   \;-\; \frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial i}\,\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial\gamma}\bigg|_{g},$$
which by (ii) is minus the bracket of (13$''$). The implicit function
theorem, licensed by $\mathcal{H}'(1-\mathcal{L}')\neq0$, gives
(13$''$).

**(iv)** The first bracket term has the sign of $g-B$ because
$\gamma>0$. The second is strictly positive: $i^\ast\in(0,1)$ by
Proposition 5(ii), and $\partial i^\ast/\partial\gamma|_g>0$ by
Proposition 5$''$, whose hypotheses are those assumed here. With
$\mathcal{H}'>0$ and $\mathcal{L}'<1$ the quotient is then strictly
positive. For the ceiling, Propositions 4(iii) and 7(iii) make the
constrained tax the constant $\bar\tau$ wherever (9) exceeds it, so its
derivative in $g$ vanishes and
$\mathcal{P}_g=\tfrac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)i^\ast}\,\partial i^\ast/\partial g$,
which is negative under $(\ddagger)$ because
$\partial i^\ast/\partial g<0$ there by Proposition 5$'''$.
$\blacksquare$

Three readings, and the first is why the factor is a multiplier.
$1/\big(1-\mathcal{L}'(g)\big)$ is a loop gain of exactly the kind
Theorem 5 bounds in the split: there the gain is
$\varepsilon_\Omega\varepsilon_g$ and the theorem puts it below one;
here it is $\mathcal{L}'$ and the same bound is what part (iv) needs.
The two loops are different — Theorem 5’s runs from the split through
$g$ to $\Omega$ and back to the split margin, this one from the share
pair through $g$ back to the share pair — and neither implies the other.
At the reported cell $\mathcal{L}'=-0.219$, so the multiplier is $0.82$,
the bracket is $3.21$ times the fixed-share numerator, and the product
is the $2.64$ by which the two derivatives differ: $+0.0077$ against
$+0.0029$.

Second, what part (iv) does not buy. $\mathcal{L}'(g)<1$ is a hypothesis
about the loop and not a theorem about it. It is implied by local
attraction, and every fixed point reported here is attracting for the
iteration that computes it, but nothing here rules out a fixed point
where it fails. A search over $895$ fixed points found none, the
smallest $\mathcal{H}'(1-\mathcal{L}')$ seen being $2.80$. That is a
sample, and a sample cannot establish a universal — Proposition 6$''$
below replaces it at the calibration of Computation 1, and does not
replace it everywhere. The $895$ are the cells that are admissible
**and** at which $\phi=1$ is the split the worker actually chooses,
which by Proposition 3(iii) is
$\tau\le(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)\Omega/(\theta\alpha)$; a further $336$
solve the scalar equation while violating that condition, and they are
fixed points of a loop nobody runs.

Third, the dichotomy is now stated in a primitive. Proposition 5$'''$
says the two channels oppose wherever $\gamma$ raises $g$ and that the
total carries Proposition 5$''$’s sign outright wherever it does not,
without saying where that is. It is $g\ge B$: at or above the
reproduction scale of Assumption 2 the growth factor rises in the
elasticity and the channels oppose. Below it both signs occur — over the
same search, $294$ fixed points sit at $g<B$, of which $168$ carry
$\mathrm{d}g/\mathrm{d}\gamma<0$ and $126$ carry it positive, the second
bracket term winning. Every one of the $168$ has $g<B$ and none has
$g\ge B$, which is the observation part (iv) would have failed.

The hypothesis is not, however, where it has to be left. Proposition
6$'$ states it at a fixed point, which is why the evidence for it could
only be a search: one has to locate the fixed points before one can test
them, and locating them is what makes the answer a sample. The next
result moves the condition off the fixed points and onto the interval
that contains them, and in doing so removes the reproduction elasticity
from it altogether.

**Proposition 6$''$ (the loop gain is free of the reproduction
elasticity, and the hypothesis is a condition on one interval).** *Let
the hypotheses of Proposition 6$'$ hold. Interiority of the tax is not
assumed here: parts (i)–(iii) work with the unconstrained objects
throughout, and the one implication that needs an interior tax carries
the hypothesis itself in (iv). Write*
$$\tau^{\mathcal{P}} \;=\; \frac{(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)}{(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)+\alpha}\;\in\;(0,1),
\qquad
\mathcal{U}(g) \;=\; \frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\,\frac{1}{g-1+\delta}
   \;+\;\frac{1-\theta}{g-1+\delta_G}
   \;-\;\frac{1}{\gamma}\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}\,
        \frac{\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast}{\mathrm{d}g} .
\tag{13$'''$}$$ *Then:*

*(i) **The elasticity is an outer factor.**
$\mathcal{H}'(g)=1/g+\gamma\big[\tfrac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}(g-1+\delta)^{-1}+(1-\theta)(g-1+\delta_G)^{-1}\big]$,
and at $\phi=1$* $$\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}
 \;=\; \gamma\,\frac{\big[(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)+\alpha\big]\big(\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-\tau\big)}
                    {\tau(1-\tau)(1-\alpha)} .$$ *Both carry $\gamma$ as
a single outer factor, and $\tau^\ast(g)$ carries none at all by
Proposition 4(ii). Hence $\mathcal{U}$ is free of $\gamma$.*

*(ii) **$\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ is the growth-maximising tax at the
corner.** $\mathcal{P}(\cdot,1,i)$ is strictly concave in $\tau$ with
unique maximiser $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$; since
$g=\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})$ is strictly increasing,
$\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ maximises the induced growth factor at fixed $i$.
It is free of $\beta$, $\gamma$, $\delta$, $\delta_G$, $\sigma$ and $i$
— the list that (5) is free of — and by (i) the tax channel of the loop
carries the sign of $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-\tau^\ast$.*

*(iii) **The tax responds to growth through the two wedge derivatives
alone.*** $$\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast}{\mathrm{d}g}
 \;=\; \frac{\alpha(1-\theta)\Big[\theta J\,\Omega'
        \;-\;\Omega\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)J'\Big]}
      {\Big[(1-\theta)\Omega+\alpha J\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\Big]^{2}},$$
*with $\Omega'$ and $J'$ the derivatives Proposition 5$'''$(ii) signs.*

*(iv) **The reduction.** Under $(\ddagger)$,*
$$\mathcal{H}'(g)-\mathcal{P}_g \;>\; \frac1g \;+\; \gamma\,\mathcal{U}(g),$$
*so that* $$\mathcal{U}(g)\;\ge\;0 \ \text{ on the admissible interval}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\mathcal{L}'(g)<1 \ \text{ at every fixed point with the tax interior, for every admissible }
\gamma .$$ *The left-hand condition names no fixed point, does not
invert $\mathcal{H}$, and does not contain $\gamma$. At a fixed point
where the ceiling binds instead, the conclusion is already Proposition
6$'$(iv)’s corollary, which gives $\mathcal{L}'<0$ under $(\ddagger)$
and needs nothing further; so between them the two cover every fixed
point.*

*(v) **The same condition delivers uniqueness.** If $(\ddagger)$ and
$\mathcal{U}\ge0$ hold throughout the admissible interval, then
$\Phi=\mathcal{H}-\mathcal{P}$ is strictly increasing there, so it
vanishes at most once: the outer fixed point is **unique**, again for
every admissible $\gamma$. With the sign change that brackets it, it is
unique and it exists — both sides of the switch being covered by (iv),
the ceiling one through Proposition 6$'$(iv)’s corollary.*

*Proof.* **(i)** Differentiating $\mathcal{H}$ gives the display, every
$\gamma$ of which sits in the bracket. At $\phi=1$,
$\kappa_W(1)=1-\alpha$ carries no $\tau$, so $\mathcal{P}$’s $\tau$-part
is
$\gamma\big[(1-\theta)\ln\tau+\tfrac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(1-\tau)\big]$,
whose derivative is
$\gamma\big[(1-\theta)/\tau-\alpha/((1-\alpha)(1-\tau))\big]$; placing
that over $\tau(1-\tau)(1-\alpha)$ and collecting gives the stated form,
the numerator being
$\big[(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)+\alpha\big]\tau^{\mathcal{P}}-\big[(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)+\alpha\big]\tau$
by the definition of $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$. **(ii)** The second
derivative is
$-\gamma\big[(1-\theta)/\tau^{2}+\alpha/((1-\alpha)(1-\tau)^{2})\big]<0$,
so the stationary point is the unique maximiser, and Proposition 6(ii)
makes $g$ strictly increasing in $\mathcal{P}$. The freedom list is read
off the formula. **(iii)** Write $W=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ and
$d=(1-\theta)\Omega+\alpha JW$, so that (9) is
$\tau^\ast=(1-\theta)\Omega/d$. Then
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g=\big[(1-\theta)\Omega' d-(1-\theta)\Omega\,d'\big]/d^{2}$
with $d'=(1-\theta)\Omega'+\alpha\big(J'W+J(1-\theta)\Omega'\big)$; the
$(1-\theta)^2\Omega'\Omega$ terms cancel and what survives is
$\alpha(1-\theta)\big[\Omega'JW-\Omega J'W-\Omega J(1-\theta)\Omega'\big]$,
which is $\alpha(1-\theta)\big[\theta J\Omega'-\Omega WJ'\big]$ because
$W-(1-\theta)\Omega=\theta$. **(iv)** By the chain rule
$\mathcal{P}_g=\tfrac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast}{\mathrm{d}g}
+\tfrac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial i}\tfrac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial g}$,
and Proposition 6$'$(ii) gives
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial i=\gamma\alpha/\big((1-\alpha)i^\ast\big)>0$.
Subtracting from (i),
$$\mathcal{H}'-\mathcal{P}_g \;=\; \frac1g+\gamma\,\mathcal{U}(g)
   \;-\;\frac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)i^\ast}\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial g},$$
and under $(\ddagger)$ Proposition 5$'''$(iii) gives
$\partial i^\ast/\partial g<0$, so the last term is strictly positive
and may be dropped. With $\mathcal{H}'>0$, $\mathcal{U}\ge0$ then forces
$\mathcal{H}'-\mathcal{P}_g>1/g>0$, which is
$\mathcal{L}'=\mathcal{P}_g/\mathcal{H}'<1$. **(v)**
$\Phi'=\mathcal{H}'-\mathcal{P}_g$, which (iv) has just put above
$1/g>0$ wherever the tax is interior; where the ceiling binds
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g=0$ and
$\Phi'=\mathcal{H}'-\tfrac{\gamma\alpha}{(1-\alpha)i^\ast}\partial i^\ast/\partial g>\mathcal{H}'>0$
under $(\ddagger)$ without further hypothesis. $\Phi$ is continuous
across the switch, so it is strictly increasing on the whole interval
and has at most one zero; its zeros are the fixed points of
$\mathcal{L}$ by (13$'$). $\blacksquare$

Two things follow, and the first is the economics. Part (ii) supplies
the tax member of a pair the paper has so far had only one half of: (5)
is the split that maximises balanced growth, and $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ is
the tax that does, at the corner where the split sits. They are free of
the same primitives, for the same reason — each is the interior
stationary point of a strictly concave $\ln$-combination — and neither
is the worker’s choice. At the reported cell the worker’s stationary tax
is $0.4993$ against $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}=0.4482$: he taxes **above** the
growth-maximising rate, because (8) charges him for the public stock’s
contribution to his own dynasty and not for the growth of the economy
that contains it. That gap is not a curiosity. By (i) it is exactly what
signs the tax channel of the loop, and at the reported cell it signs it
the stabilising way — $\tau^\ast$ above $\tau^{\mathcal{P}}$ with
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g>0$ makes the product negative, so the
tax channel *subtracts* from the loop gain rather than adding to it.

The second is what part (iv) buys that the search could not.
$\mathcal{U}$ is an explicit elementary function of $g$ and the
primitives other than $\gamma$, so it can be enclosed on subintervals
rather than evaluated at points, and a subdivision that closes every
subinterval settles a continuum. At the calibration of Computation 1
that subdivision closes, and so does the one for $(\ddagger)$ on the
same interval, leaving no hypothesis outstanding: at those primitives
$\mathcal{L}'(g)<1$ holds at every fixed point — interior by part (iv),
ceiling-bound by Proposition 6$'$(iv)’s corollary — and for **every**
admissible $\gamma$ — Assumption 2’s whole range, not one value of it.
The interval itself has to be free of $\gamma$ for that last word to be
earned, and it is: the lower endpoint is the admissibility floor
$\max\{1-\delta,\ 1-\delta_G,\ \beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}\}$, which carries no
$\gamma$, and an upper endpoint $\bar g$ is licensed by
$\mathcal{H}(\bar g)>\sup\mathcal{P}$, a difference affine in $\gamma$
with coefficients $\ln\bar g-\ln B$ and a bracket increasing in
$\bar g$. Any $\bar g>\max\{B,\ 2\}$ therefore makes both strictly
positive at these primitives, and the bound then holds at every $\gamma$
at once rather than at the one it was evaluated at. That is a statement
no search over fixed points can reach, because each fixed point it
visits belongs to one $\gamma$. Part (v) is the same fact read once
more: a strictly increasing $\Phi$ cannot cross zero twice, so at those
primitives the outer fixed point is not merely well behaved but **the
only one**. The second hypothesis is not needed everywhere, and
Proposition 6$''''$ below says where: on a band of primitives — a joint
restriction on the capital share, the public-input weight and the
curvature, and on nothing else — $(\ddagger)$ delivers both conclusions
by itself, and no subdivision is run. That changes how the computed
profiles should be read. The constant-share concept is start-dependent
by construction, and three starts give three profiles; part (v) says the
start-dependence that survives is not a multiplicity of outer rest
points. At these primitives the shares may be reached from different
places, but the growth factor they compose to is one number. What the
subdivision does not do is settle all primitives. It closes exactly
where properness binds the admissible floor strictly above the
interiority floor, $\beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}>\max\{1-\delta,1-\delta_G\}$;
where it does not, the interval abuts the point
$g=1-\min\{\delta,\delta_G\}$ at which both terms of $\mathcal{U}$
diverge, and there the enclosure cannot resolve a sign. That is a
boundary the method meets, not a region where the loop gain is known to
misbehave: over $216$ parameter cells no subinterval anywhere returned
$\mathcal{U}<0$, and the largest $\mathcal{L}'$ found at any fixed point
in the cells the subdivision failed to close was $0.81$.

It is worth saying whose failure that is, because it is not
$\mathcal{U}$’s. Both terms of $\mathcal{U}$ carry a simple pole at
$g=1-\min\{\delta,\delta_G\}$ — the first through $\mathcal{H}'$, the
second because $\tau^\ast$ runs to a corner there and
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau$ diverges with it — and **the two
poles cancel**. The residues are equal, so $\mathcal{U}$ extends
continuously to the closed interval, with a finite limit that is
strictly positive at every cell examined. An enclosure cannot see that:
near the endpoint it differences two quantities of order $10^{17}$ and
returns a box straddling zero, which is a fact about interval arithmetic
and not about the margin. The remedy is algebraic — cancel, then
enclose. What genuinely restricts the result at those primitives is not
$\mathcal{U}$ but $(\ddagger)$, which **fails** at that endpoint, $J'>0$
as $g-1+\delta_\wedge\to0$ where $(\ddagger)$ needs $J'<0$. It is
restored at a single crossing, the margin
$(\sigma-1)(g-1+\delta_\wedge)-(1-\delta_\wedge)(1-\tilde\beta)$ having
derivative $(\sigma-1)\big[1-(1-\delta_\wedge)\tilde\beta/g\big]>0$
under $\tilde\beta<1$ and $g>1-\delta_\wedge$. Below that crossing the
$i$-channel term of (13$'''$) can no longer be dropped — and it cannot
be bounded either, because it carries a pole of its own whose residue is
the one $\mathcal{H}'$ already has. That much is in the paper already,
one section further on and one derivative down: Proposition 8(ii)
records that where the floor is $1-\delta$ the divergence of
$\mathcal{H}$’s capital term is cancelled exactly by $i_\star$, which is
proportional to $\Lambda$ and so to $J$ and so to $g-1+\delta$, both
carrying the coefficient $\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$. What follows here is
that sentence differentiated. Take $\delta<\delta_G$, so that the
endpoint is $g=1-\delta$. Proposition 4(iii)’s
$D_x=(g-1+\delta)(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$ tends
there to $(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$, which properness holds away from
zero, so $J=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)/D_x$ has a *simple* zero and
$\Lambda=\alpha J\big[r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)+1\big]$
inherits it, the bracket being finite and non-zero at that point. Hence
$(g-1+\delta)\Lambda'/\Lambda\to1$ and $i^\ast=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)\to0$,
so $$\lim_{g\downarrow1-\delta}\ (g-1+\delta)\,
   \frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)i^\ast}\,\frac{\partial i^\ast}{\partial g}
   \;=\;\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha},$$ which is exactly the residue
$\mathcal{H}'$ carries there. There is no bound to be had.

What settles the case is instead which branch is live at that endpoint,
and it disposes of the difficulty rather than answering it.
$\mathcal{U}$ has already spent $\mathcal{H}'$’s pole against
$\tau^{\ast\prime}$’s, so on the interior-tax branch the two divergences
*add*: $\mathcal{H}'-\mathcal{P}_g$ falls to $-\infty$ like
$-\gamma\alpha/\big((1-\alpha)(g-1+\delta)\big)$ and the interior-tax
expression for the loop gain runs to $2$ — an expression evaluated where
its own hypothesis fails, which is why it does not conflict with the
$0.81$ above. The tax is not interior there. The same vanishing of $J$
that makes the pole sends $\tau^\ast$ of (9) to one, and $\bar\tau<1$,
so the ceiling binds on a right-neighbourhood of the endpoint; on that
branch $\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g=0$, the only poles left are
$\mathcal{H}'$’s and the $i$-channel’s, and by the display they cancel,
so $\Phi'$ extends continuously to that endpoint. The other two
orderings are easier. At $\delta_G<\delta$ it is $\Omega$ and not $J$
that vanishes at the endpoint, $\tau^\ast\to0$ and the interior branch
is the live one — and there $\Lambda$ is finite and non-zero, so the
$i$-channel carries no pole at all. At $\delta=\delta_G$ the surviving
pole of $\Phi'$ is $\gamma(1-\theta)/(g-1+\delta)$, which is positive.
In each ordering, then, the margin that governs the branch actually
taken is bounded below at the endpoint, and at every one of the $117$
cells where the subdivision stalled its limit there is strictly
positive, the smallest of them $2.85$. What is left open is not the
endpoint but the interval. Write $\Lambda=JR$, so that $R=\Lambda/J$
carries no pole of its own; the factor $g-1+\delta$ in $J$ cancels
identically, $(g-1+\delta)/\Lambda=D_x/(\tilde\beta R)$, so that on the
ceiling branch
$$\Phi' \;=\; \frac1g \;+\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{g-1+\delta_G}
   \;+\;\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\left[\frac{D_x'}{D_x}+\frac{\sigma-1}{g}
      +\frac{\Lambda'}{1+\Lambda}-\frac{R'}{R}\right],$$ every term of
which is finite on the closed interval. That bracket collapses, and what
it collapses to is the result.

One remark on the workforce weight before it does, because the section
has not needed it until here. The weight reaches $\Phi'$ through the
reinvestment rate and through nothing else. Neither $\mathcal{H}$ nor
$\mathcal{P}$ carries $\omega$, by their definitions in Proposition 6;
nor does $\mathcal{U}$, whose two pole terms carry only $\alpha$,
$\theta$ and the depreciation rates and whose third is
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau$ against
$\mathrm{d}\tau^\ast/\mathrm{d}g$ — the first free of the weight by part
(i), the second because (9) builds $\tau^\ast$ from $\Omega$, $J$ and
$\theta$ alone. Inside the rate the weight sits in two objects and no
others, $\zeta=\alpha+\omega(1-\alpha)$ and
$\mathcal{Q}=(1-\omega)+J\zeta$, and those two satisfy
$$\mathcal{Q}-J\zeta \;=\; 1-\omega,
\qquad
\mathcal{Q} \;=\; 1+\alpha J-\omega\eta, \qquad \eta=1-(1-\alpha)J\in(0,1).$$
The second display is why Proposition 5$'''$(i) is stated at $\omega=0$:
above that weight the reinvestment bracket acquires the defect
$-\omega\eta$, so $r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)+1=N/\mathcal{M}$
is exact there and at no other weight — at $\alpha=0.33$, $\theta=0.6$,
$\gamma=0.5$, $\tilde\beta=0.9$, $J=0.75$ and $\Omega=0.53$ the two
sides are $2.074$ against $2.249$ at $\omega=0.35$. The *first* display
is why that costs the collapse nothing, and it is used three times
below. What replaces the closed form is already in the paper twice over:
Proposition 5$'''$(iii) writes $\Lambda$ at every weight, and §8.5
records the proportionality that follows,
$\Lambda\propto\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon$ with
$\Upsilon=r\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)$, against $1+\Upsilon$ at
the baseline weight. The same substitution carried Theorem 6 above
$\omega=0$. It carries this section too, and everything below holds at
every $\omega\in[0,1)$; only the subdivision at the end is run at one
weight, and that is a choice of cell rather than a step in the argument.
The interior branch needs nothing separate: it differs from the ceiling
one by the tax channel alone, and that channel carries no weight, so
what is proved below is the whole of $\Phi'$ on both branches.

Three identities do the collapse, none of them numerical, and each holds
at every weight. First,
$\tilde\beta\big[(1-\delta)+\alpha(g-1+\delta)\big]=g-D_x$ with
$\partial\tilde\beta/\partial g=-(\sigma-1)\tilde\beta/g$ gives
$$\frac{D_x'}{D_x}+\frac{\sigma-1}{g} \;=\; \frac{\sigma-\tilde\beta\alpha}{D_x},$$
positive on $\tilde\beta\alpha<1$ alone, carrying no derivative at all,
and free of the weight because $D_x$ is. Second, $R$ itself. Writing
$W=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ and using
$\rho=(1-\alpha)\mathcal{Q}/\mathcal{M}$ from $(\mathrm{B}.7)$, (12)
gives $R$ outright:
$$R \;=\; \frac{\mathcal{N}_\omega}{(1-\omega)\,\mathcal{M}},
\qquad
\mathcal{N}_\omega \;=\; \zeta\mathcal{M}+\alpha\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)\,W\mathcal{Q},$$
so
$R'/R=\mathcal{N}_\omega'/\mathcal{N}_\omega-\mathcal{M}'/\mathcal{M}$
and $\rho$ and $r$ leave the expression with it. At $\omega=0$ this is
Proposition 5$'''$(i) exactly: $\mathcal{N}_0=\alpha N$ with
$N=1-\tilde\beta(1-\gamma)$, and $R=\alpha N/\mathcal{M}$ again. Third,
and this is the one that does the work, Proposition 5$'''$’s own
$\mathcal{S}$ carries no weight at all, and
$$1+\mathcal{S}\;=\;\frac{\mathcal{M}_0}{\mathcal{M}},
\qquad
1+\Lambda\;=\;\big(1+\mathcal{S}\big)\,\frac{\mathcal{Q}}{1-\omega},$$
where
$\mathcal{M}_0=1-\tilde\beta+\tilde\beta\gamma\big[(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+\alpha W\big]$
is a sum of positive terms. The first of the pair is $\mathcal{S}$’s
definition together with $\eta=1-(1-\alpha)J$; the second is
$\mathcal{Q}-J\zeta=1-\omega$ once more. At the baseline weight the two
collapse into the single statement
$\mathcal{M}(1+\Lambda)/(1+\alpha J)=\mathcal{M}_0$. What the pair buys,
with the second identity, is the rate itself in closed form,
$$i^\ast \;=\; \frac{J\zeta}{\mathcal{Q}\,\big(1-v_\omega\big)},
\qquad
v_\omega\;=\;\frac{\alpha\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)\,W\,(1-\omega)}{\mathcal{N}_\omega}\;\in\;(0,1),$$
the membership being
$\mathcal{N}_\omega-\zeta\mathcal{M}_0=\alpha\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)W(1-\omega)>0$,
which is $\mathcal{Q}-J\zeta=1-\omega$ a third time. At $\omega=0$,
where $\zeta=\alpha$ and $\mathcal{Q}=1+\alpha J$, this is
$\alpha J\big/\big[(1+\alpha J)(1-v_0)\big]$ with
$v_0=\tilde\beta\gamma(1-\alpha)W/N$, the last step using
$(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+W=1$. Together the three leave
$$\frac{D_x'}{D_x}+\frac{\sigma-1}{g}+\frac{\Lambda'}{1+\Lambda}-\frac{R'}{R}
   \;=\;\frac{(1-\omega)\sigma+\omega\tilde\beta}{D_x\,\mathcal{Q}}
   \;-\;\frac{v_\omega'}{1-v_\omega},$$ two terms where there were four.
The first is positive by inspection, its numerator a convex combination
of $\sigma$ and $\tilde\beta$; at $\omega=0$ that numerator is $\sigma$
and its denominator $D_x(1+\alpha J)$, which is the baseline form. The
only derivative surviving anywhere in the bracket is $v_\omega'$.

That is what buys the sharpening, and the sharpening is that
$(\ddagger)$ is more than the ceiling branch needs. Divide
$\mathcal{N}_\omega$ by $\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)\tilde\beta W(1-\omega)$
and both $J$ and $\mathcal{M}$ leave, so that every $g$ that remains
sits in one quotient, $$\frac{1}{v_\omega}
 \;=\; \frac{\zeta}{\alpha\gamma(1-\alpha)(1-\omega)}\cdot
       \frac{\tilde\beta^{-1}-1+\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)}{\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega}
 \;+\;\frac{\zeta}{(1-\alpha)(1-\omega)}\;+\;1 .$$ That quotient’s
numerator is positive — $\tilde\beta<1$ by properness and $\Omega<1$ by
Proposition 3(i) — and rises in $g$ whenever $\Omega'\le0$, since
$\partial_g\tilde\beta<0$ under Assumption 3; its denominator is
positive and falls. Two things follow at once, and neither needs a
computation. The right-hand side exceeds one, which returns
$v_\omega\in(0,1)$ by a second route that never forms $\mathcal{M}_0$;
and $\Omega'\le0$ forces $v_\omega'<0$, which makes the second term of
the bracket positive and $\Phi'>0$ outright. **Only the $\Omega$ half of
$(\ddagger)$ is used, and it is used at every weight.** $(\ddagger)$
signs $J$ and $\Omega$ together; the ceiling branch needs the
public-stock wedge alone — $J'$ does not appear in the display at all —
and the weakening is not vacuous: at $144$ of $2{,}320$ sampled points
$(\ddagger)$ fails through its $J$ half while $\Omega'\le0$ holds, and
both conditions are free of the weight, so the count is too. Where
$\Omega'>0$ the sign is not settled by this argument and the two terms
must be compared, which is a subdivision and not a sign question.

On the reduced form that subdivision runs, and it runs over both
instruments at once. It is run at $\omega=0$, the weight the reported
cells carry; by the display above it is needed only where $\Omega'>0$,
