reduced Jacobian’s spectrum strictly inside the unit disc, both in force
under these hypotheses —
$$\frac{\partial V_j}{\partial s} = \sum_{t\ge0}\beta^t W_{j,t}^{1-\sigma}\,
   \frac{\partial \ln W_{j,t}}{\partial s} .$$ For the **worker** the
initial population is predetermined, so
$\partial\ln N_{W,t}/\partial s=\sum_{u<t}\hat\gamma_{W,u}$; on the
incumbent balanced path
$\beta^tN_{W,t}^{1-\sigma} = N_{W,0}^{1-\sigma}\tilde\beta^{t}$, so
exchanging the order of summation,
$$\frac{\partial V_W}{\partial s} \;=\; \frac{N_{W,0}^{1-\sigma}\,\tilde\beta}{1-\tilde\beta}\;S_W,
\qquad S_W \;=\; \sum_{t\ge0}\tilde\beta^{t}\hat\gamma_{W,t}, \tag{19}$$
and since $\sigma>1$ makes $N_{W,0}^{1-\sigma}>0$, the sign of the
derivative is the sign of $S_W$. For the **capitalist** $W_{C,0}=P_0$ is
*not* predetermined — his rate moves his date-zero consumption — so the
date-zero term survives rather than dropping out. That surviving term is
the cost in (10), and Step 4 carries it explicitly instead of absorbing
it into a cumulated sum.

**Step 2: three equations in three sums.** Write
$X=\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat x_t$ and $Z=\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat z_t$.
Because $\hat x_0=\hat z_0=0$,
$\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat x_{t+1}=X/\tilde\beta$ and likewise for $z$, so
the two stock laws and the growth equation read
$$X = P_x\Big[\frac{(1-a)\hat\iota}{1-\tilde\beta}-S\Big], \qquad
  Z = P_z\Big[(1-c)\Big(\frac{\hat\varsigma}{1-\tilde\beta}+\alpha X\Big)-S\Big],$$
$$S = \gamma\theta\Big(\frac{\hat\kappa}{1-\tilde\beta}+\alpha X\Big)+\gamma(1-\theta)Z.$$
Substituting the first two into the third and replacing $(1-c)P_z$ by
$\Omega$ and $(1-a)P_x$ by $J$ throughout — which is (16) — every
resolvent disappears except the two inside $\Delta$, and the system
collapses to one identity,
$$(1-\tilde\beta)\,\Delta\,S \;=\; \gamma\Big[\theta\,\hat\kappa \;+\; (1-\theta)\Omega\,\hat\varsigma
   \;+\; \alpha\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)J\,\hat\iota\Big]. \tag{20}$$

**Step 3: the worker.** The split reaches the consumption share and the
stock flow and leaves the capital flow alone, so
$(\hat\kappa,\hat\varsigma,\hat\iota) = \big(-\tau\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi),\,
1/\phi,\, 0\big)$ and the bracket in (20) is
$(1-\theta)\Omega/\phi - \theta\tau\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi)$, which is
$\Psi(\phi)$. The tax reaches all three, with
$\big((1-\phi)\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi),\, 1/\tau,\, -1/(1-\tau)\big)$, and
the bracket is $\alpha$ times
$\theta(1-\phi)/\kappa_W + (1-\theta)\Omega/(\tau\alpha) - J[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega]/(1-\tau)$,
which is $\alpha\,\Xi(\tau;\phi)$. With (19) these are (17).

**Step 4: the capitalist.** Under (O) his payoff runs on $P$, not on
$N_C$, so the class ratio does not enter and no fourth sum is needed:
Step 2’s three sums are the whole of what he requires. Writing $K=xN_W$
and substituting $Y=AK^{\alpha}N_W^{1-\alpha}$ into
$P=C_C^{1-\omega}K^{\omega}$,
$$\ln P_t \;=\; (1-\omega)\ln\big[(1-i)(1-\tau)\alpha A\big] \;+\; \zeta\,\ln x_t \;+\; \ln N_{W,t},$$
the coefficient on $\ln N_{W,t}$ being
$(1-\omega)(1-\alpha)+(1-\omega)\alpha+\omega=1$. Hence, per unit of
$i$,
$\partial\ln P_t/\partial i = -(1-\omega)/(1-i)+\zeta\hat x_t+\hat n_{W,t}$.
On the incumbent balanced path
$\beta^tP_t^{1-\sigma}=P_0^{1-\sigma}\tilde\beta^t$, and
$\sum_t\tilde\beta^t\hat n_{W,t}=\tilde\beta S_W/(1-\tilde\beta)$ by the
exchange of Step 1, so
$$\frac{\partial V_C}{\partial i} \;=\; P_0^{1-\sigma}\Big[
   -\frac{1-\omega}{(1-i)(1-\tilde\beta)} \;+\; \zeta X \;+\; \frac{\tilde\beta\,S_W}{1-\tilde\beta}\Big].$$
The rate reaches the capital flow alone, $\hat\iota=1/i$ with
$\hat\kappa=\hat\varsigma=0$, so (20) gives
$S_W = \gamma\alpha\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]J/\big[i(1-\tilde\beta)\Delta\big]$
and $X = J/\big[i(1-\tilde\beta)\big]-P_xS_W$. Both are proportional to
$1/i$; collecting them against the $1/(1-i)$ term leaves (18), with
$\Lambda$ exactly (12).

That agreement is the load-bearing check of this section. The $\Lambda$
arrived at here is a discounted sum of linearised growth responses; the
$\Lambda$ of (12) is the solution of the $3\times3$ envelope system of
Appendix B.4–B.8. Under a dynastic capitalist the two routes overlapped
— both passed through the class-ratio block, one as a sum and one as an
envelope — so their agreement was a weaker test than it looked. Under
(O) neither route contains a class ratio at all, and they share nothing
but the transition itself: one never forms a marginal value, the other
never forms a path sum. They return the same expression identically in
every primitive and at every $\omega$.

**Step 5: the factors are positive and carry no instrument.**
$\Omega\in(0,1)$ under properness by Proposition 3(i), and $P_x,P_z>0$
by Proposition 9, so $\Delta$ is a sum of three positive terms and
exceeds one. The capitalist’s prefactor is $(1-\omega)/(1-\tilde\beta)$,
strictly positive for $\omega<1$ and vanishing exactly at the degenerate
weight of Proposition 5(i) — at which (18) says
$\partial V_C/\partial i=0\cdot\infty$ and the display stops being
informative, which is the same degeneracy arriving in a third place.
Neither expression contains $\tau$, $\phi$ or $i$ anywhere: both are
built from $\tilde\beta$, $g$ and the primitives, exactly as $\Omega$
and $J$ are. $\blacksquare$

A class that reads its margin at a fixed growth factor loses nothing by
doing so. It is not that the omitted channels are small at the reported
cells, nor that they happen to reinforce the crossing; they enter only
through $\Delta$, and a factor carrying no instrument cannot move a
zero. The reason is Proposition 9. The envelope’s two wedges are the
very resolvents that summarise the transition, so an envelope evaluated
at a fixed $g$ has already priced the transition it appeared to ignore —
and the dilution, which enters both stock laws with the same coefficient
$-1$, is absorbed into those same two objects. Three channels were
named; one algebraic fact disposes of all three.

**Corollary 2 (what is left of the second step).** *Under the hypotheses
of Theorem 4 a stationary profile satisfies each deviator’s own
first-order condition on the true discounted payoff: at every instrument
interior to its box the worker’s partial in $(\tau,\phi)$ and the
capitalist’s derivative in $i$ vanish there, and at an instrument
resting on a bound the condition is the one-sided inequality that put it
there — as at the corner $\phi^\dagger=1$, where Proposition 3(iii)
gives $\Psi(1)\ge0$ rather than $\Psi(1)=0$. What separates a stationary
profile from Definition 1 is therefore curvature, together with the
start-dependence Theorem 4 carries. That question is now one about an
explicit scalar function rather than about a value function — by (17)
the worker’s payoff rises and falls with $\Psi$ evaluated at the
profile’s own growth factor, so single-peakedness in the split is
exactly single-crossing of
$\phi\mapsto\Psi\big(\phi;\Omega(g(\tau,\phi,i))\big)$. At any zero of
that map,* $$\frac{\mathrm{d}\Psi}{\mathrm{d}\phi} \;=\;
   \frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\phi}\bigg|_{\Omega}
   \;+\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)^{2}\big(1-\Omega\big)}{\phi^{2}\,\mathcal{H}'(g)}\;\Omega'(g),$$
*whose first term is strictly negative by Proposition 3(ii), and whose
second carries the sign of $\Omega'(g)$ because $1-\Omega>0$ by
Proposition 3(i) and $\mathcal{H}'>0$ by Proposition 6(ii) — both of
which hold at a split where the profile is proper, and neither of which
is claimed elsewhere. Hence $\Omega'(g)\le0$ is sufficient for every
crossing at a proper split to be downward, and — on the interval of
proper splits that Theorem 5 supplies — for there to be at most one.*

*Proof.* By Proposition 6(ii), $g=\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})$, so
$\partial g/\partial\phi = \mathcal{P}_\phi/\mathcal{H}'(g)$ with
$\mathcal{P}_\phi = \gamma\big[(1-\theta)/\phi-\theta\tau\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi)\big]$.
Differentiating $\Psi\big(\phi;\Omega(g(\phi))\big)$ and collecting
gives $\mathrm{d}\Psi/\mathrm{d}\phi = \partial_\phi\Psi|_\Omega
+ \big[(1-\theta)/\phi\big]\,\Omega'(g)\,\mathcal{P}_\phi/\mathcal{H}'(g)$.
At a zero, $\theta\tau\alpha/\kappa_W = (1-\theta)\Omega/\phi$, so
$\mathcal{P}_\phi$ collapses to $\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)/\phi$, which
is the display. $\blacksquare$

Signing $\Omega'(g)$ cannot supply the required single-crossing
condition. The sign is not a new object: writing $s=\sigma-1$, it is the
sign of $-s\,g^{s+1}+(s+1)(1-\delta_G)g^{s}-\beta(1-\delta_G)$, which at
$g=1$ and $\sigma=2$ vanishes at
$$\delta_G \;=\; \frac{1-\beta}{2-\beta},$$ the same threshold Appendix
D.3 reports when it measures the third channel cell by cell. Above it
$\Omega$ falls with the growth factor; below it, at a public stock more
durable than any cell swept here, $\Omega$ rises. Properness does not
determine the sign. It is $g^{\sigma-1}>\beta$, a lower bound on
$g^{\sigma-1}$; the sign expression factors as
$g^{\sigma-1}\big[(1-\sigma)g+\sigma(1-\delta_G)\big]
-\beta(1-\delta_G)$, so wherever the bracket is positive a lower bound
on $g^{\sigma-1}$ pushes the expression up. At $g=1$ and $\sigma=2$
properness reduces to $\beta<1$, which is Assumption 1. There are proper
profiles with $\Omega'(g)>0$ — at $\beta=0.96$, $\sigma=2$,
$\delta_G=0.02$, $g=1$ one has $\tilde\beta=0.96$ and $\Omega'>0$ — so
Corollary 2’s sufficient condition is genuinely silent on a nonempty
part of the admissible space.

It is also far more than single-crossing needs. What the crossing
requires is not that the growth channel push the same way as the margin,
but that it not push harder. That is a statement about a loop gain, and
the loop gain is bounded by construction.

**Theorem 5 (the growth-feedback loop gain is below one).** *Let
Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$, $\gamma>0$ and $\sigma>1$;
fix constant shares with $i>0$, $\tau\in(0,1)$ and $\phi\in(0,1]$, which
are Proposition 6’s hypotheses and are what make $g(\tau,\phi,i)$
defined; and let the profile be proper. Write*
$$\varepsilon_\Omega \;=\; \frac{g\,\Omega'(g)}{\Omega},\qquad
  \varepsilon_g \;=\; \frac{\phi}{g}\frac{\partial g}{\partial\phi},\qquad
  \mu \;=\; \frac{g}{g-1+\delta_G},\qquad
  \mu_K \;=\; \frac{g}{g-1+\delta}. \tag{21}$$ *Then at every zero of
$\phi\mapsto\Psi\big(\phi;\Omega(g(\tau,\phi,i))\big)$ the two
elasticities have the closed forms*
$$\varepsilon_\Omega \;=\; \mu\Big(1-\frac{\sigma\Omega}{\tilde\beta}\Big), \qquad
  \varepsilon_g \;=\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)}{g\,\mathcal{H}'(g)}, \qquad
  g\,\mathcal{H}'(g) \;=\; 1+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mu_K+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu, \tag{22}$$
*and their product satisfies*
$$1-\varepsilon_\Omega\,\varepsilon_g \;=\;
  \frac{1+\dfrac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mu_K
        +\gamma(1-\theta)\mu\,\Omega
        +\gamma(1-\theta)\mu\,\dfrac{\sigma\Omega}{\tilde\beta}(1-\Omega)}
       {1+\dfrac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mu_K+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu} \;>\;0. \tag{23}$$
*Hence $\mathrm{d}\Psi/\mathrm{d}\phi<0$ at every such zero. Moreover
the set of splits at which the profile is proper is an interval:
$\mathcal{P}$ is strictly concave in $\phi$, with*
$$\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{P}}{\partial\phi^{2}}
  \;=\; -\gamma\left[\frac{1-\theta}{\phi^{2}}
        +\frac{\theta\tau^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\kappa_W(\phi)^{2}}\right] \;<\;0,$$
*so $g$ is unimodal in $\phi$ by Proposition 6(ii), and properness is
the superlevel set $g>\beta^{1/(\sigma-1)}$ of Proposition 6(iii). On
that interval the map therefore has at most one zero, and where it has
one it crosses from above; the worker’s one-shot objective is
single-peaked in the split there. No condition on $\Omega'(g)$ is
required, and no restriction beyond properness is imposed.*

The worker’s true payoff and the scalar map peak together, and that is
what Theorem 4 buys. Theorem 5 is a statement about the map alone and
does not invoke Theorem 4; Theorem 4 supplies the translation, without
which single-peakedness of the map would be single-peakedness of a
proxy. The two results are independent and compose.

*Proof.* Clearing $g^{\sigma-1}$ from (6) gives
$\Omega=\beta(g-1+\delta_G)\big/\big(g^{\sigma}-\beta(1-\delta_G)\big)$;
differentiating and dividing by $\Omega/g$ gives the first form in (22).
For the second, Corollary 2’s proof has
$\partial g/\partial\phi=\mathcal{P}_\phi/\mathcal{H}'(g)$ with
$\mathcal{P}_\phi$ collapsing at a zero to
$\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)/\phi$; multiplying by $\phi/g$ gives
$\varepsilon_g$, and differentiating $\mathcal{H}$ term by term gives
$g\mathcal{H}'$. Forming the product and subtracting it from one yields
(23) on a common denominator.

Every summand of the numerator of (23) is strictly positive. The first
is $1$. The second is positive because $\gamma>0$, $\alpha\in(0,1)$ and
$\mu_K>0$, the last by Assumption 4’s $g>1-\delta$. The third and fourth
are positive because $\mu>0$ by the same assumption applied to
$\delta_G$, because $\tilde\beta>0$ and $\sigma>0$, and because
$\Omega\in(0,1)$ — which is Proposition 3(i), and which holds exactly
under properness. The denominator is positive for the same reasons. So
$\varepsilon_\Omega\varepsilon_g<1$.

Corollary 2’s display rearranges, using $\varepsilon_\Omega\varepsilon_g
=\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)\Omega'(g)\big/\big(\Omega\,\mathcal{H}'(g)\big)$,
to $$\phi^{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\Psi}{\mathrm{d}\phi}
  \;=\; -(1-\theta)\,\Omega\,\big(1-\varepsilon_\Omega\varepsilon_g\big)
        \;-\; \frac{\theta\tau^{2}\alpha^{2}\phi^{2}}{\kappa_W(\phi)^{2}},$$
whose two terms are both strictly negative. The composed map is $C^{1}$
**on the proper interval**, and there only: $\kappa_W$ is bounded away
from zero throughout $(0,1]$ and $g$ is continuously differentiable in
$\phi$ by Proposition 6(ii) and the implicit function theorem, but
$\Omega$ is rational in $g$ with denominator
$g^{\sigma}-\beta(1-\delta_G)$, which vanishes at
$g=\big(\beta(1-\delta_G)\big)^{1/\sigma}$. That zero is admissible
under (A) exactly when $\beta>(1-\delta_G)^{\sigma-1}$, which is exactly
the condition under which it is *improper* — the two are the same
inequality — so it never meets the proper region and Proposition 3(i)
stands. It does, however, sit inside $(0,1]$ in the split: at the
baseline cell it falls at $\phi=0.014$, in the improper tail described
below. A $C^{1}$ function whose every zero is a strict down-crossing has
at most one zero *on an interval*: two of them would force a sign change
back to positive in between, hence a third zero, at which the derivative
would be non-negative. The interval is supplied by the concavity
display: $\partial^{2}\mathcal{P}/\partial\phi^{2}<0$ follows from
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\phi=\gamma\big[(1-\theta)/\phi-\theta\tau\alpha/\kappa_W(\phi)\big]$
and $\kappa_W'=-\tau\alpha$; $\mathcal{H}^{-1}$ is strictly increasing,
so $g$ inherits unimodality, and a superlevel set of a unimodal function
on an interval is an interval. $\blacksquare$

A society that puts too little of the taxed proceeds into provision can
fall outside the range where any of this holds, and that is not a
formality. At the cells of Computation 5 the proper set runs from
$\phi=0.036$ upward at the baseline and from $\phi=0.212$ upward at
$\sigma=3$ with $\gamma=0.7$; only the durable cells are proper on the
whole of $(0,1]$. As $\phi\to0^{+}$ the scalar $\mathcal{P}$ falls
without bound, $g$ falls to the left endpoint
$\max(1-\delta,1-\delta_G)<1$, and $\tilde\beta=\beta g^{1-\sigma}$
rises through one. The improper tail is exactly where Proposition 3(i)’s
catalogue applies, and where an unmasked grid produces a spurious second
crossing. Theorem 5 says nothing there, and is not meant to.

A public stock that barely wears out does not threaten the crossing; it
only relocates where the sensitivity sits. The cancellation behind that
is exact. As the public stock becomes permanent, $\delta_G\to0$, the
elasticity $\varepsilon_\Omega$ diverges — $\mu\to\infty$ — and that
divergence is what made signing $\Omega'$ look necessary. But
$\mathcal{H}$ carries the term $\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)$, so
$g\mathcal{H}'$ diverges through the same pole and with the same
coefficient. The growth factor becomes correspondingly insensitive to
the split at precisely the rate at which the wedge becomes sensitive to
the growth factor. In (23) the divergent $\mu$ appears in both numerator
and denominator and cancels, leaving a ratio bounded by one.

The bound is tight rather than generous: Computation 5 finds loop gains
within five per cent of one, so (23) does not say the feedback is
negligible, only that it is a contraction.

The tax and the rate are not covered, and for the tax the reason is
economic rather than technical. Three limits travel with the theorem. It
is written for the split. The same construction applied to the tax
margin (14) does not close in the same way, for a reason that is
structural rather than technical: differentiating $\mathcal{P}$ in
$\tau$ and subtracting $\gamma\alpha\,\Xi$ leaves
$$\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}\bigg|_{\Xi=0}
  \;=\; \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)}{\tau}
        \;-\; \frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\tau}
              \left[\frac{1}{1-\alpha}-J\big(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big)\right]. \tag{24}$$
Both terms are signed, and they point opposite ways. The first is the
exact analogue of the split’s collapse and is positive under properness.
The second is strictly negative there, which is not apparent from the
expression: by (B.7$'$), $(1-\alpha)J = 1-\eta$ with $\eta\in(0,1)$
exactly under properness, so the bracket equals
$\big[1-(1-\eta)T\big]/(1-\alpha)$ with
$T=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\in(\theta,1)$ — one minus a product of two
numbers below one. So $\partial g/\partial\tau$ is unsigned at a zero of
$\Xi$ where $\partial g/\partial\phi$ is signed at a zero of $\Psi$, and
that is a proved fact about the tax rather than a gap in the argument:
the tax enters the capitalist’s investable base $1-\tau$ and the split
does not, and the term that entry contributes pulls the other way. The
second difference is that $\Xi$ moves with two wedges rather than one,
and
$\partial\Xi/\partial\Omega=(1-\theta)\big[1/(\tau\alpha)-J/(1-\tau)\big]$
is unsigned on the face of it. It is not unsigned on the domain, and
nothing beyond (8) is needed to see it. Carrying
$J=\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)/D_x$ into the bracket leaves it with the sign
of $(1-\tau)D_x-\tau\alpha\tilde\beta(g-1+\delta)$, affine and strictly
decreasing in $\tilde\beta$ with root
$\tilde\beta^{\ast}=cg\big/\big[(g-1+\delta)+c\big(\alpha(g-1+\delta)+1-\delta\big)\big]$
at $c=(1-\tau)/(\tau\alpha)$; and $1-\tilde\beta^{\ast}$ carries
$(g-1+\delta)$ as a factor of its numerator, which cancels, leaving
$\tilde\beta^{\ast}<1$ exactly when $\tau>1-\alpha$. The growth factor
and both depreciation rates leave the condition altogether. So the
second wedge derivative is strictly positive at every proper profile
whose capital-income tax lies at or below labour’s share, and takes both
signs only above it — unsigned in a corner, not on the domain, and the
corner is a capital-income tax above labour’s share of output. Both
quantifiers run over the expression’s own domain,
$(\alpha,\tau,g,\delta,\tilde\beta)$ subject to $g>1-\delta$ and
$\tilde\beta<1$, so what is established is that a tax above labour’s
share is *necessary* for a negative raw partial. Whether that corner is
*occupied* at an interior rest point — where
$\tilde\beta=\beta g^{1-\sigma}$ is a function of $g$ rather than a
coordinate free beside it — is the question §G.2 records the certificate
as deliberately not answering. Section 8.5 takes both differences up: at
a zero of $\Xi$ the second derivative collapses, and what it collapses
to is signed at every split, which is enough to reduce the tax to a
criterion. Finally, the worker owns two instruments, so his second-order
condition is a joint statement about $(\tau,\phi)$ rather than two
separate single-crossing conditions — the first-order content in (17) is
a gradient and does close for the pair, but the curvature left open on
his side is genuinely two-dimensional. What Theorem 5 removes is the
split’s side condition, not the two-dimensionality.

**Computation 4 (the identity against a route that shares no step).**
*At the cells of Computation 1 and at three others — $\theta=0.80$;
$\delta_G=0.50$ with $B=1.8$; $\sigma=3$ with $\gamma=0.7$ — each
derivative in (17) and (18) is compared against the same derivative
obtained by simulating $(\star)$ from the rest point under the perturbed
constant share and differencing the discounted payoff, a procedure in
which no marginal value, no wedge and no first-order condition appears.
Across four cells and three instruments the worst relative discrepancy
is $6\times10^{-8}$. Where a margin has an interior zero the two objects
vanish together: at $\theta=0.80$ the split’s zero is
$\phi^\ast = 0.6347$, at which the simulated payoff derivative is
$7\times10^{-9}$ against a scale of $48$, while the balanced-growth
benchmark $\phi_g$ is not a zero of it. Properness is what the identity
needs, and it is visible: at $\delta_G=0.50$ with $B=1.0$ the same
profile has $\tilde\beta=1.023$, the discounted sums diverge, and the
closed form returns a finite number the path does not have.*

**Computation 5 (the crossing survives where the old condition fails).**
*The growth factor is obtained by bisection on
$\mathcal{H}(g)=\mathcal{P}$ rather than from any closed form, $\Psi$ is
built from it on a $500$-point grid in $\phi$, improper points are
masked, and sign changes are counted. The mask is contiguous at every
cell, as Theorem 5’s concavity display requires, and it is a strict
subinterval at three of six: $[0.036,1]$ at the baseline, $[0.024,1]$ at
$\theta=0.80$, $[0.212,1]$ at $\sigma=3$ with $\gamma=0.7$, and the
whole of $(0,1]$ at the three durable cells. At two cells whose public
stock is durable enough that $\Omega'(g)>0$ — $\delta_G=0.01$ with
$\theta=0.80$, and $\delta_G=0.005$ with $\sigma=3$ — Corollary 2’s
condition is unavailable and the composed map still crosses exactly
once, at $\phi^\ast=0.237$ and $\phi^\ast=0.706$, with loop gains
$0.334$ and $0.095$. A central finite difference of the composed map at
each crossing agrees with the value (23) predicts to a relative error
below $10^{-9}$ — at $\theta=0.80$, $-0.3336064793$ against
$-0.3336064792$ — so the chain from (6) through (21)–(23) is checked in
magnitude and not merely in sign. A sweep of $40{,}320$ proper points,
of which $11{,}880$ have $\Omega'(g)>0$, finds no loop gain at or above
one; the largest is $0.949$, as $\delta_G\to0$ with $\sigma$ near one.
The sweep is a refutation attempt and establishes nothing: what
establishes (23) is Theorem 5.*

The corollary’s sufficient condition holds at the cells of Computation 1
— $\Omega'(g)<0$ at all four and across the proper range of $\phi$ at
each, the largest value being $-1.07$ — but after Theorem 5 nothing
rests on it. On a $199$-point grid in $\phi$ the split’s margin changes
sign twice at $\theta=0.80$ until the improper points are removed, and
the extra crossing is $\Omega$ passing through the pole of Proposition
3(i) at $\tilde\beta=1$ rather than a margin turning. That proposition’s
improper side — $\Omega$ negative above the pole, above one below it —
is doing visible work here rather than sitting as a caveat.

**So what.** A class sitting at a settled position and deciding whether
to move an instrument does not have to price what the move sets in
motion. The transition, the dilution of capital by a workforce that is
itself growing, and the change in the growth factor all reach that
decision through a single positive number carrying no instrument, and a
number carrying no instrument cannot turn a gain into a loss. So the
best responses computed for the split, the tax and the rate — each read
off a margin taken at a fixed balanced-growth factor — travel in the
direction the true dynamic problem gives them, and are not artefacts of
the shortcut that produced them. That is bounded in two places, and the
owner carries the sharper one: at the degenerate weight where he values
only the stock he owns, his prefactor vanishes and (18) stops being
informative. The second is that direction is settled and distance is not
— how far a class should move turns on curvature, closed here for the
split alone. None of this says which way an instrument moves relative
class size; that is Section 7’s question, and Theorem 2$'$ answers it
under its own hypotheses.

## 8.5 The other two instruments

Theorem 5 leaves the tax and the rate where Section 6 left them:
single-crossing at a fixed balanced-growth factor, and nothing said
about the factor moving. The two are not equally hard, and the reason is
worth stating before either is done. The rate reaches $\mathcal{P}$
through one additively separable term, so its growth channel is the same
at every point and needs no collapse at a zero; the tax reaches
$\mathcal{P}$ through three, so its channel exists only at a zero, which
is what (24) computes. What both then need is the comparison Theorem 5
makes: the wedge’s elasticity against the growth factor’s, with the pole
in the first cancelled against the pole in the second.

**Proposition 10 (the rate margin, with the growth factor endogenous).**
*Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$; fix constant shares
with $\tau\in(0,1)$, $\phi\in(0,1]$ and $i\in(0,1)$; and let the profile
be proper. Write $c=\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$,
$\varepsilon_\Lambda=g\Lambda'(g)/\Lambda$ and
$\varepsilon^{i}_g=(i/g)\,\partial g/\partial i$. Then:*

*(i) **The growth channel is instrument-free, and needs no zero.**
$\mathcal{P}$ is additively separable in $\ln i$, so
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial i = c/i$ and*
$$\varepsilon^{i}_g \;=\; \frac{c}{g\,\mathcal{H}'(g)}
   \;=\; \frac{c}{1+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mu_K+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu}
   \;\in\;\Big(0,\ \frac{1}{\mu_K}\Big), \tag{25}$$ *the upper bound
because $\mu_K\ge1$ under Assumption 1’s $\delta\le1$. Where the split’s
elasticity had to be evaluated at a zero of $\Psi$ before it collapsed,
this one is the same at every point of the box.*

*(ii) **The criterion, and the slack.** $\Lambda$ is a function of
$(\tilde\beta,g)$, the primitives and $\omega$ alone and carries no
instrument — $\omega$ is a primitive of Assumption 3, not a choice — so
at every zero of $i\mapsto\Lambda\big(g(\tau,\phi,i)\big)/i-1/(1-i)$,*
$$i^{2}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}i}\Big[\frac{\Lambda}{i}-\frac{1}{1-i}\Big]
  \;=\; -\Lambda\Big[(1+\Lambda)\;-\;\varepsilon_\Lambda\,\varepsilon^{i}_g\Big]. \tag{26}$$
*The crossing is downward exactly when
$\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g<1+\Lambda$. The threshold is
$1+\Lambda$ rather than one, and the surplus is $\Lambda$ itself.
Moreover the set of rates at which the profile is proper is an interval:
$\mathcal{P}$ is strictly increasing and strictly concave in $i$, with
$\partial^{2}\mathcal{P}/\partial i^{2}=-c/i^{2}<0$, so $g$ is strictly
increasing in $i$ by Proposition 6(ii) and properness is an up-interval.
On it, $\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g\le1$ — at every point, not
only at zeros — makes $i\mapsto\ln\Lambda-\ln\big[i/(1-i)\big]$ strictly
decreasing, so the margin has at most one zero there and crosses from
above. That last condition is sufficient and is not always available —
$\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g$ exceeds one at the proper cells
reported immediately after this proposition, so the surplus $\Lambda$ in
(26) is load-bearing rather than decorative. That last clause is a
computed witness, not a consequence of the proof below.*

*(iii) **The wedge’s pole does not arise.** $\Lambda$ carries $\Omega$
only through $T=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega$ and through $\mathcal{M}$, and
both are affine in $\Omega$, so differentiation delivers $\Omega'(g)$
against bounded coefficients and the combination that survives is*
$$\Omega\,\varepsilon_\Omega \;=\; \frac{g\tilde\beta}{D_G}\Big(1-\frac{\sigma\Omega}{\tilde\beta}\Big),
\qquad D_G \;=\; g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G) \;\ge\; g(1-\tilde\beta) \;>\;0,$$
*which is finite at every proper profile and carries no pole in
$\delta_G$: as the public stock becomes permanent, $\mu\to\infty$ while
$\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega$ converges. It is not bounded **uniformly** —
the displayed lower bound on $D_G$ degenerates as $\tilde\beta\to1$, and
$\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega$ diverges along any sequence approaching the
vanishing of the public flow and the properness boundary together. What
(26) and (27) use is the pointwise statement, at a profile fixed before
the criterion is evaluated. The only pole left in $\varepsilon_\Lambda$
is $\mu_K$, which enters through $\varepsilon_J$, and (25) divides it by
a larger $\mu_K$. In particular $\varepsilon_\Lambda\le0$ is sufficient
for the crossing, and so is $\varepsilon_\Lambda<\mu_K(1+\Lambda)$.*

*Proof.* **(i)** Of $\mathcal{P}$’s four terms only
$\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln\big(i(1-\tau)\alpha
A\big)$ carries $i$, giving $\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial i=c/i$. By
Proposition 6(ii), $g=\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{P})$, so
$\partial g/\partial i=(c/i)/\mathcal{H}'(g)$ and
$\varepsilon^{i}_g=c/\big(g\mathcal{H}'\big)$; the denominator is (22).
For the bound, $\mu_K-1=(1-\delta)/(g-1+\delta)\ge0$ by Assumptions 1
and 4, and $g\mathcal{H}'-c\mu_K=1+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu>0$, so
$\mu_K\varepsilon^{i}_g<1$.

**(ii)** $\Lambda=J\alpha\big[r(\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega)+1\big]$ by
(12), and $J$, $\Omega$ and $r$ are functions of $(\tilde\beta,g)$ and
the primitives by Proposition 4(ii), (B.3) and (B.7), so
$\mathrm{d}\Lambda/\mathrm{d}i=\Lambda'(g)\,\partial g/\partial i$ with
no direct channel. Writing $F(i)$ for the margin,
$i^{2}F'(i)=i\Lambda'(g)\big(\partial g/\partial i\big)-\Lambda-i^{2}/(1-i)^{2}$,
and the first term is $\Lambda\,\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g$.
At a zero $\Lambda=i/(1-i)$, so $i^{2}/(1-i)^{2}=\Lambda^{2}$, which is
(26). For the global statement, put
$h(i)=\ln\Lambda\big(g(i)\big)-\ln i+\ln(1-i)$; then
$h'(i)=\big[\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g-1/(1-i)\big]/i$, and
$1/(1-i)>1$ on $(0,1)$. For the interval,
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial i=c/i>0$ and
$\partial^{2}\mathcal{P}/\partial i^{2}=-c/i^{2}<0$, so $\mathcal{P}$ is
strictly increasing in $i$; $\mathcal{H}^{-1}$ is strictly increasing by
Proposition 6(ii), so $g$ is too, and a superlevel set of a strictly
increasing function is an up-interval.

**(iii)**
$\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega=\mu\Omega\big(1-\sigma\Omega/\tilde\beta\big)$
by (22), and $\mu\Omega=g\tilde\beta/D_G$ directly from (6); the bound
on $D_G$ is the one in Proposition 9’s proof. $T$ is affine in $\Omega$
by inspection and $\mathcal{M}$ is affine in $\Omega$ by (B.7$''$),
$\eta$ carrying none. For $\varepsilon_J$, differentiating
$J=\tilde\beta u/D_x$ gives $\varepsilon_J=-(\sigma-1)+\mu_K-gD_x'/D_x$,
whose only unbounded term is $\mu_K$. The two sufficient conditions
follow from $\varepsilon^{i}_g>0$ and $\varepsilon^{i}_g<1/\mu_K$.
$\blacksquare$

The contrast with the split is worth naming. There the threshold was one
exactly, and Theorem 5 had to show the loop gain fell below it. Here the
threshold is $1+\Lambda$, and $\Lambda$ is the capitalist’s own
quantity: by (12) it is $J/(1-\omega)$ times
$\zeta+\tilde\beta\alpha\gamma T\rho$, and the lone $\zeta$ in that
bracket is capital’s direct claim on his felicity. The instrument whose
condition carries a direct flow term is the instrument whose crossing
has room to spare. Nothing on the worker’s side has a counterpart to it,
which is the same asymmetry Section 6 read off (11), arriving a second
time and in a different place. Under the dynastic objective the same
slot was occupied by his own dynasty, and the surplus was there for a
different reason; that the room survives the substitution while its
source changes is what makes it a structural feature of the rate rather
than of the payoff it was first derived from.

That the surplus is needed rather than merely available is the point of
the qualification in (ii), and the margin by which it is needed is thin.
Across $61{,}440$ proper cells of an eight-primitive grid the loop gain
exceeds one at exactly **one** of them, reaching $1.060$ — at a nearly
permanent public stock ($\delta_G=0.02$) with $g\to1$ and a low
curvature. So substituting the tidier
$\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g\le1$ for (26)’s threshold would
fail, but it would fail narrowly and at one corner rather than over a
region. The sharp threshold is met at all $61{,}440$, and at
$\omega=0.25$ and $0.50$ as well — which is what Theorem 6 proves, at
every weight in $[0,1)$. The two sides move together:
$\varepsilon_\Lambda$ is large precisely where $\rho$ is, which is where
$\Lambda$ is large too, and that is why the sharp threshold survives
what the tidy one does not. The thinness is worth stating rather than
hiding, because it is the honest measure of how much Theorem 6 buys: it
converts a bound that fails at one cell in sixty thousand into one that
fails nowhere, and a single counterexample is as fatal to a universal
claim as a region of them.

**Proposition 11 (the tax margin, at every split).** *Let Assumptions
1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$, fix $\tau\in(0,1)$ and $\phi\in[0,1]$,
and let the profile be proper. Parts (iii)–(iv) differentiate the growth
factor and therefore add Proposition 6’s own hypotheses, $i>0$ and
$\phi\tau>0$, without which $g(\tau,\phi,i)$ and hence
$\varepsilon^{\tau}_g$ are not defined. Then:*

*(i) **(24)’s second bracket is strictly positive.** By (B.7$'$),*
$$\frac{1}{1-\alpha}-J\big[\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega\big]
  \;=\; \frac{1-(1-\eta)T}{1-\alpha} \;>\;0,
\qquad T=\theta+(1-\theta)\Omega,$$ *because
$1-\eta=(1-\alpha)J\in(0,1)$ and $T\in(\theta,1)$ under properness. So
(24) is a strictly positive term plus a strictly negative one. That the
sum genuinely takes both signs — rather than merely failing to be signed
by this decomposition — is a separate matter, and Computation 6 settles
it by exhibiting one of each. So $\partial g/\partial\tau$ is unsigned
at a zero of $\Xi$ as a fact about the tax rather than for want of an
argument.*

*(ii) **At a zero the second wedge derivative collapses, and is signed
at every split.** Substituting the zero condition for $J$ into
$\partial\Xi/\partial\Omega=(1-\theta)\big[1/(\tau\alpha)-J/(1-\tau)\big]$,
and using $T-(1-\theta)\Omega=\theta$,*
$$\frac{\partial\Xi}{\partial\Omega}\bigg|_{\Xi=0}
  \;=\; \frac{\theta(1-\theta)}{T}\left[\frac{1}{\tau\alpha}-\frac{1-\phi}{\kappa_W(\phi)}\right]
  \;=\; \frac{\theta(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)}{T\,\tau\alpha\,\kappa_W(\phi)} \;>\;0,$$
*the second equality because
$\kappa_W(\phi)-\tau\alpha(1-\phi)=1-\alpha$ identically, the
$(1-\phi)\tau\alpha$ terms cancelling. Every factor is strictly positive
under properness, at every $\phi\in[0,1]$ and not only at the corner;
$\Omega$ has left the expression altogether. The raw partial is signed
by (8) alone, but only at or below labour’s share, as §8.4 shows; the
collapse signs it at every tax.*

*(iii) **The criterion, at every split.** Write
$\varepsilon^{\tau}_g=(\tau/g)\,\partial g/\partial\tau$. At every zero
of the composed tax margin,* $$\frac{\mathrm{d}\Xi}{\mathrm{d}\tau}
  \;=\; -\underbrace{\left[\frac{\theta(1-\phi)^{2}\alpha}{\kappa_W^{2}}
        +\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\alpha\tau^{2}}+\frac{JT}{(1-\tau)^{2}}\right]}_{\text{restoring}}
  \;+\; \underbrace{\left[\frac{\theta(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)\,\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega}
        {T\,\tau\alpha\,\kappa_W}-\frac{T J\varepsilon_J}{1-\tau}\right]}_{\text{feedback}}
        \frac{\varepsilon^{\tau}_g}{\tau}, \tag{27}$$ *so the crossing
is downward exactly when the feedback term falls short of the restoring
one, which is strictly positive by Proposition 7(i). At $\phi=1$, where
$\kappa_W=1-\alpha$ and the first restoring term drops, (27) collapses
to* $$\frac{\mathrm{d}\Xi}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\bigg|_{\phi=1}
  \;=\; \frac{Z}{\tau}\left[\Big(\frac{\theta\,\varepsilon_\Omega}{T}-\varepsilon_J\Big)
        \varepsilon^{\tau}_g \;-\; \frac{1}{1-\tau}\right],
\qquad Z=\frac{(1-\theta)\Omega}{\tau\alpha}>0,$$ *whose threshold
$1/(1-\tau)$ exceeds one, so a loop gain at or below one suffices there.
In (27) the wedge enters the **feedback bracket** only through the
product $\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega$, which Proposition 10(iii) exhibits
as finite at every proper profile; the divergence that made signing
$\Omega'$ look necessary is therefore absent from that bracket rather
than cancelled within it. It is not absent from the criterion as a
whole: $\varepsilon^{\tau}_g$ carries $g\mathcal{H}'$ in its denominator
and $g\mathcal{H}'$ carries $\mu$. But there the pole only shrinks the
loop gain, which is the reverse of the obstruction Corollary 2 met.*

*(iv) **The proper set is an interval in the tax, at every split.**
$\mathcal{P}$ is strictly concave in $\tau$, with*
$$\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau^{2}} \;=\; -\gamma\left[
   \frac{\theta(1-\phi)^{2}\alpha^{2}}{\kappa_W(\phi)^{2}}
   \;+\; \frac{1-\theta}{\tau^{2}}
   \;+\; \frac{\alpha}{(1-\alpha)(1-\tau)^{2}}\right] \;<\;0,$$ *so $g$
is unimodal in $\tau$ by Proposition 6(ii) and properness, being a
superlevel set of $g$, is an interval. On an interval a $C^{1}$ margin
whose every zero is a strict down-crossing has at most one zero, which
is what turns (27) into single-peakedness of the worker’s objective in
the tax, at the split it is evaluated at.*

*Proof.* **(i)** (B.7$'$) is $(1-\alpha)J=1-\eta\in(0,1)$ exactly under
properness, so $J=(1-\eta)/(1-\alpha)$ and the displayed identity is
algebra. Proposition 3(i) puts $\Omega\in(0,1)$ under properness and
$\theta\in(0,1)$, so $T\in(\theta,1)$; a product of two numbers in
$(0,1)$ is below one.

**(ii)** $\Xi$ carries $J$ exactly once and linearly, so the zero
condition solves for it:
$J=\big[(1-\tau)/T\big]\big[\theta(1-\phi)/\kappa_W+(1-\theta)\Omega/(\tau\alpha)\big]$.
Substituting into
$\partial\Xi/\partial\Omega=(1-\theta)\big[1/(\tau\alpha)-J/(1-\tau)\big]$
gives
$(1-\theta)\big[\big(T-(1-\theta)\Omega\big)/(T\tau\alpha)-\theta(1-\phi)/(T\kappa_W)\big]$,
and $T-(1-\theta)\Omega=\theta$, which is the first displayed form. For
the second, $\kappa_W(\phi)=(1-\alpha)+(1-\phi)\tau\alpha$ gives
$1/(\tau\alpha)-(1-\phi)/\kappa_W=\big[\kappa_W-\tau\alpha(1-\phi)\big]/(\tau\alpha\kappa_W)
=(1-\alpha)/(\tau\alpha\kappa_W)$.

**(iii)** The restoring bracket is
$-\partial\Xi/\partial\tau|_{\Omega,J}$, which is Proposition 7(i). For
the feedback, $\Omega'=\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega/g$,
$J'=J\varepsilon_J/g$ and
$\partial g/\partial\tau=g\varepsilon^{\tau}_g/\tau$, so the growth
factor cancels and the two wedge channels contribute
$\big[(\partial\Xi/\partial\Omega)\,\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega
+(\partial\Xi/\partial J)\,J\varepsilon_J\big]\varepsilon^{\tau}_g/\tau$
with $\partial\Xi/\partial J=-T/(1-\tau)$; substituting (ii) for the
first gives (27). At $\phi=1$ the two terms of the margin both equal $Z$
at a zero, so the restoring bracket is $Z/\tau+Z/(1-\tau)$, the feedback
bracket is
$(Z/T\tau)\big(\theta\varepsilon_\Omega-T\varepsilon_J\big)\tau$ since
$J=Z(1-\tau)/T$ and $\kappa_W=1-\alpha$, and
$-1-\tau/(1-\tau)=-1/(1-\tau)$ gives the corner form.

**(iv)** Differentiating
$\partial\mathcal{P}/\partial\tau=\gamma\big[\theta(1-\phi)\alpha/\kappa_W+(1-\theta)/\tau
-(\alpha/(1-\alpha))/(1-\tau)\big]$ once more, with
$\partial\kappa_W/\partial\tau=(1-\phi)\alpha$, gives the display term
by term; the first is non-positive and vanishes only at $\phi=1$, where
the term it differentiates is absent from (14) as well, and the other
two are strictly negative. $\mathcal{H}^{-1}$ is strictly increasing, so
$g$ inherits unimodality, and a superlevel set of a unimodal function on
an interval is an interval. $\blacksquare$

The corner is not where this stops. What made $\phi=1$ look special is
that the second bracket term of (ii) vanishes there, so the derivative
could be signed by inspection; the identity
$\kappa_W-\tau\alpha(1-\phi)=1-\alpha$ signs it everywhere else too, and
it does so by removing $\Omega$ from the expression rather than by
bounding it. The corner remains the case that matters — Computation 1
reaches $\phi^\ast=1$ at every reported cell and in nine of the ten
distinct sweep cells admitting a proper solution, and Theorem 3’s fixed
point is the corner — but (27) is not confined to it. What it does is
reduce the worker’s remaining curvature question in the tax, at every
split, to one inequality between an elasticity product and a restoring
force, both explicit.

**Computation 6 (the two criteria, attacked).** *The rate criterion is a
function of the growth factor and the primitives alone, and so is the
tax criterion at the corner, where the zero of $\Xi$ pins $\tau$ from
$(\tilde\beta,g)$ by $\tau/(1-\tau)=(1-\theta)\Omega/(J\alpha T)$; away
from the corner the split survives into the tax criterion and has to be
swept, which is what the third grid below does. So the region each
ranges over can be enumerated rather than sampled from a solved
equilibrium.*

*On a $51{,}840$-point product grid, $45{,}360$ of whose cells are
proper with $D_x>0$, $\Omega\in(0,1)$ and $\mathcal{M}>0$ verified
rather than assumed, no cell has
$\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g\ge1+\Lambda$; the largest ratio to
the threshold is $0.705$, at $\alpha=0.45$, $\beta=0.90$, $\gamma=0.70$,
$\theta=0.85$, $\delta=0.02$, $\delta_G=1$, $\sigma=1.5$, $g=1$, where
$\varepsilon_\Lambda=42.0$ against $\varepsilon^{i}_g=0.019$. That
$4{,}929$ cells have $\varepsilon_\Lambda>0$ is what makes the sweep say
anything: the sign $\varepsilon_\Lambda\le0$ closes (26) with no work.
On a $69{,}120$-point grid, $60{,}480$ of them proper, the tax bracket
of Proposition 11(i) is positive at every cell with a smallest value of
$0.0174$, and the corner form of (27) reaches at most $0.804$ of its
threshold. Away from the corner, on a $46{,}656$-point grid sweeping
$\phi$ across six values of which five are interior, the zero of $\Xi$
is located by bisection rather than assumed — Proposition 7(i) makes the
crossing transversal — at all $41{,}796$ proper cells, the closed form
of Proposition 11(ii) matches the directly computed derivative at every
one of them, and the feedback term of (27) reaches at most $0.804$ of
the restoring term, its worst case falling at $\phi=0.05$ rather than at
the corner.*

*All three grids stop at a finite elasticity — $\sigma\in\{1.5,2,3,6\}$
in the first two and $\{1.5,2,4\}$ in the third — while Assumption 3
bounds $\sigma$ only from below, and outside them the criterion does
fail. At $\alpha=0.60$, $\beta=0.80$, $\gamma=\theta=0.95$, $\sigma=12$,
$\delta=0.3612$, $\delta_G=0.005$ and $g=0.9958$, a cell proper by the
same three tests, the feedback term of (27) reaches $1.0396$ of the
restoring term at $\phi=0.6$ and the crossing there is upward. It is not
one of the artefacts the pole probes below produce: the ratio clears one
by $4\times10^{-2}$ rather than by $10^{-13}$, its denominator is of
order one, and it is unchanged at three hundred digits. That cell is not
reachable by sampling the box it sits in — $229{,}757$ draws with
$\sigma\le12$ return nothing above $0.9996$, and it was found by
maximising the ratio instead — which is the sense in which none of these
grids is a bound. So $0.804$ is a statement about the swept range rather
than about the criterion. Nothing above it falls: the single-peakedness
clause of Proposition 11(iv) is conditional on every zero being a strict
down-crossing, so what it loses at that cell is its hypothesis, not the
interval property it proves outright from the concavity of $\mathcal{P}$
in the tax.*

*Each criterion is then attacked at the poles its own grid cannot reach.
For the tax the thin place is the permanent public stock: along $g=1+e$,
$\delta_G=e$ the loop gain and the threshold both approach one, the
ratio rising monotonically to $1-4\times10^{-13}$ at $e=10^{-15}$
without reaching one, with the same verdict along fifteen further
directions with $g$ and $\delta_G$ decoupled and across $9{,}216$ cells
at $\delta_G=10^{-10}$; the iterated limit is exactly one. The tax is
thin at the capital pole as well, the ratio reaching $1-2\times10^{-10}$
along $g=1+e$, $\delta=e$ at $e=10^{-12}$. For the rate that capital
pole is the only thin place: the ratio again converges to one from below
there, while the permanent-stock limit sends it to zero and the
properness boundary to $-1/2$. Across $45{,}929$ admissible points in
eight approach families, $35{,}718$ of them with
$\varepsilon_\Lambda>0$, there is no confirmed crossing; the three
apparent ones are sixty-digit rounding artifacts at
$g-1+\delta\approx2\times10^{-42}$ and dissolve at three hundred digits,
which is also the reason the probes are run at sixty rather than in
double precision.*

*Two sign witnesses are read off the same enumeration. The first grid,
crossed with the three splits $\phi\in\{1,\ 0.5,\ 0.05\}$, admits an
interior zero of $\Xi$ at $136{,}080$ of its proper cells, and at those
cells (24) is positive at $20{,}202$ and negative at $115{,}878$ — which
is what makes Proposition 11(i)’s “unsigned” a property of the tax
rather than a limit of its decomposition. Neither sweep establishes
anything: what (26) and (27) establish is the identity, and the
inequality in each is a criterion. Theorem 6 proves (26)’s; (27)’s is
tight and not proved.*

The tightness is the same phenomenon Theorem 5 reports and not a new
one. The split’s own loop gain runs to $0.949$ as $\delta_G\to0$ with
$\sigma$ near one, and the mechanism is identical: the wedge becomes
sensitive to the growth factor at exactly the rate at which the growth
factor stops responding to the instrument. What differs at the corner is
that the surviving margin is supplied by the term (24) identified as the
obstruction. The tax enters $1-\tau$, so the margin carries
$JT/(1-\tau)$, so the direct restoring force at a zero is
$Z/\tau+Z/(1-\tau)$ rather than $Z/\tau$ alone, and the threshold is
$1/(1-\tau)$ rather than one. The channel that makes the tax harder than
the split to sign is also the channel that leaves it room.

Each criterion’s tight boundary follows from the instrument’s incidence
on the stock flows in Theorem 4’s Step 3. The split reaches the public
flow and leaves the capital flow alone, the rate reaches the capital
flow alone, and the tax reaches both. That incidence, rather than the
primitive calibration, fixes where each criterion binds.

**Proposition 12 (the pole rule).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with
$\theta\in(0,1)$, let $\delta\neq\delta_G$, and let the profile be
proper and interior in its instruments — $\tau\in(0,1)$, $\phi\in(0,1]$,
$i\in(0,1)$ — which is what (23), (26) and (27) are stated under and
therefore what makes the three loop gains defined objects to take limits
of. Write $u=g-1+\delta$ and $u_G=g-1+\delta_G$ for the two gross
investment flows, both strictly positive by (A). All three loop gains
are functions of $g$ and the primitives alone, so the limits below are
limits in $g$ at fixed primitives. Since $u-u_G=\delta-\delta_G$ is a
**non-zero** constant, at most one flow can vanish. Assume in addition
that the pole in question is *proper*. Since
$\tilde\beta=\beta g^{1-\sigma}$ falls in $g$ and properness is
$\tilde\beta<1$, that is one inequality on the primitives —
$\beta<(1-\delta)^{\sigma-1}$ for the capital pole,
$\beta<(1-\delta_G)^{\sigma-1}$ for the public one — and where it fails
the whole approach path is improper and the corresponding column below
is empty. Then:*

*(i) **What a vanishing flow does.** As $u\to0^+$ — which needs
$\delta_G>\delta$, and sends $g\downarrow1-\delta$ —*
$$D_x\to(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)>0, \qquad J\to0, \qquad \mu_KJ\to\frac{\tilde\beta}{1-\tilde\beta},
  \qquad \eta\to1, \qquad \Lambda\to0,$$
$$u\,\mu_K\to1-\delta, \qquad u\,\varepsilon_J\to1-\delta, \qquad
  u\,g\mathcal{H}'\to c\,(1-\delta), \qquad c=\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha},$$
*while $\Omega$, $\varepsilon_\Omega$ and
$\varepsilon_\Lambda-\varepsilon_J$ stay finite. As $u_G\to0^+$ — which
needs $\delta>\delta_G$, and sends $g\downarrow1-\delta_G$ —*
$$D_G\to(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta)>0, \qquad \Omega\to0, \qquad
  \mu\,\Omega\to\frac{\tilde\beta}{1-\tilde\beta},$$
$$u_G\,\varepsilon_\Omega\to1-\delta_G, \qquad
  u_G\,g\mathcal{H}'\to\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\delta_G),$$ *while
$\varepsilon_J$ and $\varepsilon_\Lambda$ stay finite.*

*(ii) **The six cells.** Write each instrument’s loop gain over its own
threshold — $\varepsilon_\Omega\varepsilon_g$ over $1$ for the split by
(23),
$\big(\theta\varepsilon_\Omega/T-\varepsilon_J\big)\varepsilon^{\tau}_g(1-\tau)$
over $1$ for the tax at $\phi=1$ by (27), and
$\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g/(1+\Lambda)$ over $1$ for the rate
by (26). Then, at every admissible primitive vector at which the pole in
question is proper — the left column at those with $\delta_G>\delta$ and
$\beta<(1-\delta)^{\sigma-1}$, the right at those with $\delta>\delta_G$
and $\beta<(1-\delta_G)^{\sigma-1}$ —*

| loop gain / threshold | $u\to0^+$ (capital) | $u_G\to0^+$ (public) |
|-----------------------|---------------------|----------------------|
| split                 | $0$                 | $1$                  |
| tax, at $\phi=1$      | $1$                 | $1$                  |
| rate                  | $1$                 | $0$                  |

*(iii) **The rule.** Each criterion is tight at the pole of each stock
flow its instrument reaches and slack at the pole of the one it does
not. The four ones and the two zeros are exact.*

*Proof.* **(i)** As $u\to0$,
$D_x=u(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$ tends to
$(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)$, strictly positive because properness gives
$\tilde\beta<1$; it is the denominator of everything that follows. Hence
$J=\tilde\beta u/D_x\to0$ while
$\mu_KJ=\tilde\beta g/D_x\to\tilde\beta/(1-\tilde\beta)$, and
$\eta=g(1-\tilde\beta)/D_x\to1$, so $\mathcal{M}\ge1-\tilde\beta>0$ by
(B.7$''$) and $r$ stays finite; with $J\to0$, (12) gives $\Lambda\to0$.
Because $u_G\to\delta_G-\delta>0$, the public objects $\Omega$, $\mu$
and $\varepsilon_\Omega$ stay finite — which is where $\delta_G>\delta$
is used, and why the two poles cannot be approached together. For the
rates, $u\mu_K=g$; and
$g D_x'=g(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(\sigma-1)\tilde\beta(\alpha u+1-\delta)$
is bounded, so $\varepsilon_J=-(\sigma-1)+\mu_K-gD_x'/D_x$ obeys
$u\varepsilon_J\to1-\delta$ as well: the pole carries coefficient one on
both. Then $u\,g\mathcal{H}'=u+cg+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu u\to c(1-\delta)$
by (22). Finally the product rule on (12) gives
$\varepsilon_\Lambda=\varepsilon_J+g\,\mathrm{d}\ln(rT+1)/\mathrm{d}g$,
and the second term is finite because $r$, $T$ and their $g$-derivatives
are and $rT+1\ge1$. The public column is the mirror:
$D_G=g-\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)\to(1-\delta_G)(1-\tilde\beta)>0$, so
$\Omega=\tilde\beta u_G/D_G\to0$ while $\mu\Omega=\tilde\beta g/D_G$
stays finite; $\varepsilon_\Omega=\mu(1-\sigma\Omega/\tilde\beta)$
therefore loses its second factor and
$u_G\varepsilon_\Omega\to1-\delta_G$;
$u_G\,g\mathcal{H}'=u_G+c\mu_Ku_G+\gamma(1-\theta)g$ tends to
$\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\delta_G)$; and $u\to\delta-\delta_G>0$ keeps
$\mu_K$, hence $\varepsilon_J$, finite, while $\Lambda$ reaches $\Omega$
only through $T$ and $\mathcal{M}$, both affine in $\Omega$, so only the
finite product $\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega$ of Proposition 10(iii)
survives into $\varepsilon_\Lambda$.

**(ii)** Algebra of limits on (i), one cell at a time. The split’s gain
is
$\varepsilon_\Omega\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)\cdot u/\big(u\,g\mathcal{H}'\big)$,
whose numerator vanishes and whose denominator tends to $c(1-\delta)>0$:
zero at the capital pole. At the public pole write it as
$\big(u_G\varepsilon_\Omega\big)\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)/\big(u_G\,g\mathcal{H}'\big)$,
which tends to
$(1-\delta_G)\gamma(1-\theta)/\big[\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\delta_G)\big]=1$.
The rate’s is
$c\big(u\varepsilon_\Lambda\big)/\big[(1+\Lambda)\big(u\,g\mathcal{H}'\big)\big]\to
c(1-\delta)/\big[1\cdot c(1-\delta)\big]=1$ at the capital pole, and
$c\,\varepsilon_\Lambda u_G/\big[(1+\Lambda)\big(u_G\,g\mathcal{H}'\big)\big]\to0$
at the public one, $\varepsilon_\Lambda$ being finite there. For the
tax, the zero of $\Xi$ at $\phi=1$ gives
$\tau/(1-\tau)=(1-\theta)\Omega/(J\alpha T)$, so with
$S=(1-\theta)\Omega$ and $Q=J\alpha T$,
$$\tau\frac{\partial\mathcal{P}}{\partial\tau}\Big|_{\Xi=0}(1-\tau)
 =\frac{\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)\,Q-\gamma\alpha\,S\big[\tfrac{1}{1-\alpha}-JT\big]}{S+Q},$$
which at the capital pole has $Q\to0$ and $S\to(1-\theta)\Omega>0$,
hence tends to $-c$, and at the public pole has $S\to0$ and
$Q\to J\alpha\theta>0$, hence tends to $\gamma(1-\theta)$. The remaining
factor is
$\big(\theta\varepsilon_\Omega/T-\varepsilon_J\big)/g\mathcal{H}'$,
which multiplied and divided by the vanishing flow tends to
$-(1-\delta)/\big[c(1-\delta)\big]=-1/c$ at the capital pole and to
$(1-\delta_G)/\big[\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\delta_G)\big]=1/\big[\gamma(1-\theta)\big]$
at the public one, $T\to\theta$ there. Both products are $1$.
$\blacksquare$

**Computation 7 (the rule does not need the depreciation to vanish).**
*The proposition moves $g$ at fixed primitives, so at the capital pole
$g\to1-\delta$ from above at whatever $\delta$ is given. The six limits
reported before this were all taken along $g=1+e$ with the depreciation
in question set to $e$, which is the single path on which $g\to1$
instead. Across five approach paths per pole and four primitive bases —
$\alpha$ from $0.20$ to $0.60$, $\sigma$ from $1.02$ to $12$, $\gamma$
down to $0.05$ — of which thirty are admissible and ten improper, the
worst departure from the tabled value at $e=10^{-14}$ is
$5\times10^{-11}$. Two paths per pole hold that depreciation fixed at
$0.02$ and at $0.25$, so $g$ tends to $0.98$ and to $0.75$ rather than
to one.*

The ten improper cases are not an artefact of that computation’s bases,
and the proposition’s properness condition is the reason the count is
not zero. It binds at the paper’s own baseline: with
$(\beta,\delta,\delta_G,\sigma)=(0.96,0.08,0.05,2)$, $\tilde\beta$ rises
to $1.0435$ at the capital pole and to $1.0105$ at the public one, so
*neither* limit can be taken inside the proper region there. The rule is
a statement about durable stocks and patient dynasties jointly — a pole
is reachable only where $\beta$ is small enough relative to the survival
share $(1-\delta)^{\sigma-1}$ — and the reported cells of Computation 1
are not among them. What the six limits describe is therefore the
*shape* of the criteria near a boundary the baseline does not itself
approach. The condition does not empty the rule of content: at
$\beta=0.90$, $\sigma=2$, $\delta=0.02$ and $\delta_G=0.05$ the capital
pole is proper, $\tilde\beta$ tending to $0.918$, and condition (E)
holds there at any reproduction scale $B>1.014$ with $A=1$ — so the
columns are populated at primitives where Theorem 3 delivers a
stationary profile. The mirror cell, $\delta=0.30$ against
$\delta_G=0.02$, does the same for the public pole at $B>1.437$.

The mechanism is one line of (22). The factor
$g\mathcal{H}'=1+c\mu_K+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu$ sits in the denominator of
every $\varepsilon_g$ and carries *both* poles always, while the
instrument’s own wedge elasticity sits in the numerator and carries only
the poles of the stocks it reaches: $\varepsilon_\Omega/\mu\to1$ at the
public pole, where $\Omega\to0$ strips
$\varepsilon_\Omega=\mu\big(1-\sigma\Omega/\tilde\beta\big)$ of its
second factor, and $\varepsilon_J/\mu_K\to1$ at the capital pole. Since
$g\mathcal{H}'/\big(\gamma(1-\theta)\mu\big)\to1$ and
$g\mathcal{H}'/(c\mu_K)\to1$ at those same two limits, a reached pole
appears above and below with matching coefficients and the ratio goes to
one; an unreached pole appears only below, and the ratio goes to zero.
The tight cells are tight because both sides diverge, not because both
vanish — in part (i) each side acquires a *nonzero* limit only after
multiplication by the vanishing flow.

That the coefficients match rather than nearly match is Proposition
6(i). The separation there is performed by substituting the rest-point
levels into $\ln g_W$, and
$z(g)=\phi\tau\alpha A\,x(g)^{\alpha}/(g-1+\delta_G)$ means the single
term $\gamma(1-\theta)\ln z$ splits into
$\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(\phi\tau\alpha A)$, which lands in $\mathcal{P}$,
and $-\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)$, which lands in $\mathcal{H}$.
One term, one coefficient, two sides. The collected $\ln x$ terms do the
same with $\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$. So the number governing a stock’s
pole in $\mathcal{H}$ is *forced* to equal the number governing its flow
in $\mathcal{P}$, and the cancellation that makes each reached pole
tight is the same separation that made the growth factor explicit in the
first place.

The rate’s slackness at the public pole is the cell that discriminates.
A criterion tight everywhere would carry no information about which
stock its instrument touches, and it is Proposition 10(iii) — the
wedge’s divergence never entering the loop — that makes that cell come
out the other way.

Two things the proposition does not say. It fixes the *limit* of each
ratio and says nothing about the *direction* of approach, which is the
whole of what (26) and (27) leave open: a ratio rising to one is
consistent with the criterion holding throughout and with its failing
arbitrarily close to the pole, and only the sweeps reported in
Computation 6 speak to which, at the cells they reach. And one of the
six cells sits outside the instrument set in the limit. The tax cell is
evaluated at the zero of $\Xi$, and that zero moves with the pole:
$\tau\to1$ as $u\to0$, because $J\to0$ empties the third term of (14),
and $\tau\to0$ as $u_G\to0$, because $\Omega\to0$ empties the second.
Assumption 1 confines the tax to $[0,\bar\tau]$ with $\bar\tau<1$, so
close enough to the capital pole the zero of $\Xi$ lies above the
ceiling and the cell describes a tax the model does not admit. The limit
is still the limit of the composed ratio, and the other five cells are
unaffected; but the tax’s capital cell is the one of the six that the
instrument set eventually leaves behind, and Section 8.2’s ceiling is
the reason.

One further reading of the tightness, because it changes what a proof of
either criterion would have to look like. Tightness is a property of the
*ratio*. Write the rate’s criterion as a difference,
$N=(1+\Lambda)g\mathcal{H}'-c\,\varepsilon_\Lambda$, and the two
$c\mu_K$ terms cancel against each other:
$$N \;=\; 1+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu
   \;+\; c\left[(\sigma-1)+\frac{gD_x'}{D_x}-\varepsilon_{rT+1}\right]
   \;+\; \Lambda\,g\mathcal{H}',
\qquad \varepsilon_{rT+1}=g\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln(rT+1)}{\mathrm{d}g},$$
where
$gD_x'=g(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(\sigma-1)\tilde\beta(\alpha u+1-\delta)>0$.
Every piece has a finite limit at the capital pole by Proposition 12(i):
$\mu$ because $\delta_G>\delta$ there, the two $D_x$ terms because
$D_x\to(1-\delta)(1-\tilde\beta)>0$, and $\Lambda g\mathcal{H}'$ because
$\Lambda\mu_K$ does. So $N$ stays bounded exactly where the ratio
approaches one — the ratio does that because two divergent quantities
differ by a bounded amount, not because a margin closes. Three of the
four pieces are strictly positive, and the fourth would settle the
criterion outright if $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$ were signed. It is not.

**Computation 8 (the sign route is closed, and the crude bound is not
available).** *On a $61{,}440$-cell proper grid over eight primitives,
and at $\omega=0$, the smallest $N$ of Theorem 6’s (28) below is
$1.0484$ and $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$ is strictly positive at $5{,}419$
cells — $8.8$ per cent of them — reaching $14.40$. That closes the sign
route, and it is the only thing this sweep is used for: a proof of that
inequality running through $\varepsilon_{rT+1}\le0$ cannot exist. The
crude majorant is not available either. Replacing
$-\varepsilon_\mathcal{M}$ by $|\varepsilon_\mathcal{M}|$ and
$\varepsilon_J$ by $\mu_K$ inside $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$ drives $N$
negative at $1{,}261$ of the same cells, down to $-731$, so the sign
structure of $\mathcal{M}$ — affine in $J$ and in $\Omega$ by (B.7$''$)
with $\eta=1-(1-\alpha)J$ — is load-bearing rather than convenient. Both
findings persist at $\omega=0.25$ and $0.50$, where $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$
is positive at $9.7$ and $11.2$ per cent of cells and the crude majorant
still breaks $N$. Neither criterion is proved by any of this; what the
sweep establishes is which two routes to a proof are unavailable.*

Signing $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$ is closed, and it was never what the
criterion needed. Each divergent channel reaches $N$ *twice* — the
capital pole through $\varepsilon_J$ in $\varepsilon_r$ and again
through $\varepsilon_J$ inside $\varepsilon_\mathcal{M}$, the wedge
through $\varepsilon_T$ and again through $\varepsilon_\Omega$ inside
$\varepsilon_\mathcal{M}$ — and the weight that multiplies them collects
each pair into a single term before anything is bounded. At the baseline
weight that weight is $\upsilon=\Upsilon/(1+\Upsilon)$ with
$\Upsilon=rT$, satisfying $\upsilon(1+\Upsilon)=\Upsilon$ and
$\upsilon\big(r+1/T\big)=r$. What survives is dominated slice by slice
by $\Lambda\, g\mathcal{H}'$, and the residual is the wedge’s own
distance from the reproduction share. Both identities, and the
decomposition $\varepsilon_{rT+1}=\upsilon(\varepsilon_r+\varepsilon_T)$
they act on, were verified numerically at $400$ random proper cells to
nine decimal places; they are identities in $(r,T)$ alone and carry no
objective.

The *object* they act on does carry one, and this is worth stating
before the theorem rather than inside it. $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$ is the
weighted sum appearing in $\varepsilon_\Lambda$ only where $\Lambda$ is
proportional to $1+\Upsilon$, which is the case at $\omega=0$ and at no
other weight; in general $\Lambda\propto\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon$ and the
weight is
$\tilde\upsilon=\alpha\Upsilon/(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)\le\upsilon$. The
collecting identity generalises exactly —
$\tilde\upsilon(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)=\alpha\Upsilon$ — so the argument
below is the same argument, run on the weight the payoff actually
supplies.

**Theorem 6 (the rate criterion holds, on the whole proper region, at
every admissible weight).** *Let Assumptions 1–4 hold with
$\theta\in(0,1)$ and $\omega\in[0,1)$; fix constant shares with
$\tau\in(0,1)$, $\phi\in(0,1]$ and $i\in(0,1)$; and let the profile be
proper. Then*
$$N \;=\; (1+\Lambda)\,g\mathcal{H}' \;-\; c\,\varepsilon_\Lambda \;>\; 1, \tag{28}$$
*so $\varepsilon_\Lambda\varepsilon^{i}_g<1+\Lambda$ everywhere on the
proper region and not merely in the limit. In fact
$N-1>c(\sigma-1)(1+\tilde\upsilon)+c\,gD_x'/D_x+\Lambda$, so the margin
is not merely positive but bounded below by three named channels:
curvature, the capital denominator and the rate index. By Proposition
10(ii) the rate margin crosses zero downward at every zero; since the
proper set is an interval in $i$, it has at most one zero there, and the
capitalist’s one-shot objective is single-peaked in the reinvestment
rate.*

The weight enters through one elasticity and one explicit factor —
$\zeta=\alpha+\omega(1-\alpha)$ and $1-\omega$ — and reaches the
argument at three points, every other $\omega$-bearing quantity below
inheriting it from those two. Separating the three is most of what a
reader needs. First, the capitalist’s workforce valuation: the relation
is $r\mathcal{M}=\tilde\beta(1-\alpha)\gamma\,\mathcal{Q}$ by (B.7),
where
$$\mathcal{Q} \;=\; (1-\omega)+J\zeta \;=\; 1+\alpha J-\omega\eta \;\ge\; 1-\omega \;>\;0 ,$$
which is $1+\alpha J$ at $\omega=0$ and at no other weight,
$\eta=g(1-\tilde\beta)/D_x$ being strictly positive throughout the
proper region. Second, $\Lambda=J(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)/(1-\omega)$ of
(12), which is $\alpha J(1+\Upsilon)$ only at $\omega=0$. Third — and
this is the one that decides the argument — the weight *inside*
$\varepsilon_\Lambda$. Since $\Lambda$ is proportional to
$J(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)$,
$$\varepsilon_\Lambda \;=\; \varepsilon_J \;+\; \tilde\upsilon\,\big(\varepsilon_r+\varepsilon_T\big),
\qquad \tilde\upsilon \;\equiv\; \frac{\alpha\Upsilon}{\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon}
\;\le\; \frac{\Upsilon}{1+\Upsilon} \;=\; \upsilon,$$ with equality
exactly at $\omega=0$, where $\zeta=\alpha$. So $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$ is
the right object at the baseline weight and not above it, and the whole
$(\varepsilon_r+\varepsilon_T)$ block — the term the criterion subtracts
— is *down-weighted* as $\omega$ rises.

The quantities the argument is otherwise built from carry no $\omega$,
and each is checked rather than asserted: $\mathcal{M}$ and
$g\mathcal{M}'$, $J$, $\eta$, $D_x$, $\Omega$, $T$, $\mu$, $\mu_K$,
$\varepsilon_J$, $\varepsilon_\Omega$, $\varepsilon_\mathcal{M}$ and
$g\mathcal{H}'$ — thirteen derivatives in $\omega$, each identically
zero. That $\mathcal{M}$ is untouched is (B.7)’s own point and is what
makes the extension a substitution rather than a re-derivation.

The third channel also explains a direction the computation could report
and not account for. At all $61{,}440$ proper cells of Computation 8’s
grid the minimum of $N$ *rises* in $\omega$ — from $1.048$ to $1.053$ to
$1.063$ at $\omega=0$, $0.25$ and $0.50$ — and $\tilde\upsilon$ falling
in $\omega$ is why. In the proof it shows up as the residual:
$(\mathcal{Q}+\omega)$ below, in place of the baseline’s $1+\alpha J$.

*Proof.* Write $\Upsilon=rT$, $\upsilon=\Upsilon/(1+\Upsilon)$ and
$\tilde\upsilon=\alpha\Upsilon/(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)$, both in $(0,1)$,
so that
$\varepsilon_\Lambda=\varepsilon_J+\tilde\upsilon(\varepsilon_r+\varepsilon_T)$
with
$\varepsilon_r=(1-\sigma)+\zeta J\varepsilon_J/\mathcal{Q}-\varepsilon_\mathcal{M}$
and $\varepsilon_T=(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega/T$. At $\omega=0$
these reduce to $\tilde\upsilon=\upsilon$ and
$\varepsilon_\Lambda=\varepsilon_J+\varepsilon_{rT+1}$.

**Step 1: what $\varepsilon_\mathcal{M}$ contains.** Differentiate
(B.7$''$), using $g\eta'=-(1-\alpha)J\varepsilon_J$ from (B.7$'$) and
$gT'=(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega$, and write
$\Pi=(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+\alpha\eta T$ for its bracket:
$$g\mathcal{M}' \;=\; (\sigma-1)\tilde\beta\big[1-\gamma\Pi\big]
   \;-\; \gamma\tilde\beta(1-\alpha)\Big[(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega(1+\alpha J)
   \;+\; \alpha JT\varepsilon_J\Big].$$ The factor $(1-\alpha)$ comes
out because the $(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)$ term contributes
$-(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega$ and the $T$ channel
$+\alpha\eta(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega$, and
$1-\alpha\eta=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$ by (B.7$'$). Since
$(1-\theta)(1-\Omega)+T=1$ identically,
$\Pi=1-T(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)\le1$, so $1-\gamma\Pi>1-\gamma>0$ and the
first term is non-negative. Dropping it and dividing by $\mathcal{M}>0$,
with $\gamma\tilde\beta(1-\alpha)/\mathcal{M}=r/\mathcal{Q}$ from
$r\mathcal{M}=\tilde\beta(1-\alpha)\gamma\mathcal{Q}$,
$$-\varepsilon_\mathcal{M} \;\le\; \frac{r(1+\alpha J)}{\mathcal{Q}}(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega
   \;+\; \frac{r\,\alpha JT\varepsilon_J}{\mathcal{Q}}.$$ This is where
the $1/(1-\alpha)$ inside $c$ goes: it cancels against $r$’s numerator,
so no bound below carries it and $\alpha\to1$ is not a singular
direction.

**Step 2: the collection.** Substituting that into $\varepsilon_r$ and
weighting by $\tilde\upsilon$, the two $\varepsilon_J$ terms carry
$\tilde\upsilon(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)=\alpha\Upsilon$ — an identity by
construction, and the exact counterpart of
$\upsilon(1+\Upsilon)=\Upsilon$ at the baseline weight — while the two
$\varepsilon_\Omega$ terms collect under
$w_\Omega=\tilde\upsilon\big[r(1+\alpha J)/\mathcal{Q}+1/T\big]$, which
is $r$ at $\omega=0$. This leaves
$$c\,\tilde\upsilon\big(\varepsilon_r+\varepsilon_T\big) \;\le\; -\,c\,\tilde\upsilon(\sigma-1)
   \;+\; \frac{c\,\alpha\Upsilon J\varepsilon_J}{\mathcal{Q}}
   \;+\; c\,w_\Omega(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega .$$ Both
collections are exact identities; the only inequality is Step 1’s
dropped term.

**Step 3: assembly.** With Proposition 10’s decomposition of $N$ and
$g\mathcal{H}'=1+c\mu_K+\gamma(1-\theta)\mu$, the two $c\mu_K$ terms
cancelling as before,
$$N-1 \;\ge\; \underbrace{c(\sigma-1)(1+\tilde\upsilon)}_{>0}
   \;+\; \underbrace{c\,\frac{gD_x'}{D_x}}_{>0}
   \;+\; \underbrace{\Lambda}_{>0} \;+\; A \;+\; B,$$
$$A=cJ\Big[\frac{(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)\,\mu_K}{1-\omega}-\frac{\alpha\Upsilon\,\varepsilon_J}{\mathcal{Q}}\Big],
\qquad
B=\gamma(1-\theta)\mu\big[1+\Lambda\big]-c\,w_\Omega(1-\theta)\Omega\varepsilon_\Omega,$$
each slice of $g\mathcal{H}'$ spent exactly once: the leading $1$ on the
free term, the $c\mu_K$ slice on $A$, the $\gamma(1-\theta)\mu$ slice on
$B$ alongside $N$’s own such term. At $\omega=0$,
$\Lambda=\alpha J(1+\Upsilon)$ and $\mathcal{Q}=1+\alpha J$, and these
are the baseline displays. Here $A$ and $B$ name the two residual slices
of this decomposition and are local to Steps 3–5; they are not
Assumption 1’s productivity $A$ or Assumption 2’s reproduction scale
$B$, neither of which appears in this proof.

**Step 4: the capital channel.**
$\varepsilon_J=-(\sigma-1)+\mu_K-gD_x'/D_x<\mu_K$ because both
subtracted terms are positive — $\sigma-1>0$ by Assumption 3, and
$gD_x'/D_x>0$ because
$gD_x'=g(1-\tilde\beta\alpha)+(\sigma-1)\tilde\beta(\alpha u+1-\delta)>0$
and $D_x>0$ by Proposition 4(iii) — and $\mu_K>0$. Since
$\mathcal{Q}\ge1-\omega$ and $\zeta>0$ — and, from the same identity,
$\mathcal{Q}\ge J\zeta>0$, which is the bound that does not degenerate
as $\omega\to1^{-}$ —
$$\alpha\Upsilon(1-\omega) \;<\; (1-\omega)(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon) \;\le\; \mathcal{Q}\,(\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\frac{\alpha\Upsilon}{\mathcal{Q}} \;\le\; \frac{\zeta+\alpha\Upsilon}{1-\omega},$$
so $A>0$ whatever the sign of $\varepsilon_J$. The pole stands on both
sides of that comparison and it stays strict, so no bound on
$\varepsilon_J$ is required — which is exactly what replacing
$\varepsilon_J$ by $\mu_K$ destroys.

**Step 5: the wedge channel.** First, $\varepsilon_\Omega\le\mu$, and it
is worth deriving rather than citing (22), whose closed form is stated
at a zero of the split margin and is in fact an identity in $g$:
differentiating $\Omega$ directly,
$$\mu-\varepsilon_\Omega \;=\; \frac{\sigma\,g^{\sigma}}{g^{\sigma}-\beta(1-\delta_G)} \;>\;0,$$
the denominator being positive exactly when $\tilde\beta(1-\delta_G)<g$,
which is Proposition 3(i)’s condition and is what makes $\Omega$ a
proper fraction in the first place. So the bound holds unconditionally
on the proper region, at every instrument and not only at a zero of
$\Psi$. Hence $B\ge(1-\theta)\mu\,\Gamma$ with
$\Gamma=\gamma(1+\Lambda)-c\Omega w_\Omega$. Since $\zeta\ge\alpha$
gives $\tilde\upsilon\le\upsilon$ and the bracket
$\big[r(1+\alpha J)/\mathcal{Q}+1/T\big]$ is positive, replacing
$\tilde\upsilon$ by $\upsilon$ only lowers $\Gamma$; write
$\Gamma^{\mathrm{o}}$ for that replacement. Multiplying by
$\mathcal{M}>0$, with $c(1-\alpha)=\gamma\alpha$ and $\Upsilon=rT$,
$$\Gamma^{\mathrm{o}}\mathcal{M}
 \;=\; \frac{\gamma\mathcal{Q}}{1-\omega}\Big[\mathcal{M}+\gamma\tilde\beta\alpha(1-\alpha)JT\Big]
   \;-\; \gamma^{2}\alpha\tilde\beta\Omega\,\mathcal{Q}
   \;-\; \gamma^{2}\alpha\tilde\beta\Omega\,\upsilon\omega\eta .$$ Now
$\mathcal{M}\ge\tilde\beta\gamma\alpha\eta T$, one of its three strictly
positive terms by (B.7$''$), and $\eta T+(1-\alpha)JT=T$ by (B.7$'$) —
so the first bracket is at least $\tilde\beta\gamma\alpha T$ **as an
equality in $\eta$**, no slack being spent on it. Using
$\mathcal{Q}\ge1-\omega$ once more, with $T-\Omega=\theta(1-\Omega)$ and
$T-\upsilon\eta\Omega>T-\Omega$,
$$\Gamma\mathcal{M} \;\ge\; \Gamma^{\mathrm{o}}\mathcal{M}
 \;\ge\; \gamma^{2}\alpha\tilde\beta\big[\mathcal{Q}(T-\Omega)+\omega(T-\upsilon\eta\Omega)\big]
 \;\ge\; \gamma^{2}\alpha\tilde\beta\,\theta(1-\Omega)\,(\mathcal{Q}+\omega),$$
and therefore $$B \;\ge\; (1-\theta)\mu\,
   \frac{\gamma^{2}\alpha\tilde\beta\,\theta(1-\Omega)\,(\mathcal{Q}+\omega)}{\mathcal{M}} \;>\;0 .$$
Every summand in Step 3 is strictly positive, which is (28). At
$\omega=0$ the residual is $(1+\alpha J)$ and every display above is the
baseline one. $\blacksquare$

Like Theorem 5, Theorem 6 is a statement about an explicit scalar map
and does not invoke Theorem 4; the translation from single-peakedness of
that map to single-peakedness of the capitalist’s *true* discounted
payoff is Theorem 4’s, and carries Theorem 4’s hypotheses — the declared
start equal to the induced rest point, and local asymptotic stability by
Theorem 2(iii). Under those, (18) makes the translation immediate, and
with it settles the curvature question in the only instrument the
capitalist holds: his set is one-dimensional, so single-peakedness in
$i$ exhausts the curvature side of his clause. It does not exhaust the
clause. Theorem 4 translates only where the declared start equals the
rest point the *evaluated* share induces, and a deviation is evaluated
from a start held fixed at the incumbent’s rest point; the two coincide
at the incumbent share alone. Carrying the criterion off that diagonal
is a separate step, and Section 8.6 takes it under an explicit
condition. The worker’s clause needs that same step and one more,
because his deviation set is the two-dimensional box
$[0,\bar\tau]\times[0,1]$ — single-crossing in each coordinate
separately does not exclude a saddle, and it is the *joint* condition in
$(\tau,\phi)$, not Proposition 11’s tax criterion on its own, that his
clause requires. Proposition 11 is a component of that condition and not
a substitute for it. Section 8.7 supplies the joint condition: in the
product coordinates $(\tau,\varpi)$ with $\varpi=\phi\tau$ the three
coefficients the worker reaches through are affine on a convex set —
which is what the $(\tau,\phi)$ box, convex already, does not deliver —
and Proposition 15 reduces the requirement to the single scalar
inequality $(\ast\ast)$ — strictly harder than its restriction to the
split direction, as joint concavity must be — for which Proposition
15$'$ gives a sufficient condition in the primitives alone. The saddle
this paragraph raises is a property of the $(\tau,\phi)$ coordinates and
not an obstruction to the route; Proposition 15(ii) says so of
Proposition 13(v) explicitly.

Three things about the proof are worth separating from it. The first is
that it never signs $\varepsilon_{rT+1}$, and never bounds it:
Computation 8’s refutation stands, and is not in the way. What the two
collections of Step 2 buy is that the quantity needing a bound is
$\varepsilon_J$ against $\mu_K$ and $\varepsilon_\Omega$ against $\mu$ —
each pole against *itself*, rather than either against a threshold. The
crude majorant failed because it broke that pairing, and Computation 8’s
$-731$ measures the cost of breaking it.

The second is where properness does the work. It enters four times, and
each time as $\tilde\beta<1$: through $\mathcal{M}>0$, through
$\Omega<1$, through $D_x>0$, and through $\eta\in(0,1)$. The residual
$\gamma\alpha\tilde\beta\,\theta(1-\Omega)$ vanishes as
$\tilde\beta\to1$ and as $\theta\to0$, and those are the two directions
in which the theorem is tight. Only the first is also a direction in
which $\mathcal{M}$ approaches zero: Lemma B.2 identifies
$\tilde\beta\to1$ alone, and (B.7$''$) bounds
$\mathcal{M}>1-\tilde\beta>0$, so at fixed $\tilde\beta<1$ it stays away
from zero as $\theta\to0$.

The third is that (B.7$'$) is used twice and differently: once as
$1-\alpha\eta=(1-\alpha)(1+\alpha J)$, to cancel $c$’s $1/(1-\alpha)$,
and once as $\eta T+(1-\alpha)JT=T$, to turn $\mathcal{M}$ plus a piece
of $r$ into $\gamma\alpha\tilde\beta T$ exactly. That identity is the
accounting statement that the capitalist’s two claims on the capital
envelope — his valuation of the workforce and his valuation of his own
dynasty — exhaust it. The criterion holds because that accounting is
exact, not because any quantity in it happens to be small.

What Theorem 6 does *not* reach is the tax. Proposition 11’s inequality
remains a criterion, and Proposition 12 says why the two are not the
same problem: the rate reaches one stock flow and the tax reaches both,
so the tax is tight at *both* poles where the rate is tight at one, and
the collection of Step 2 has no counterpart when three channels rather
than one carry the instrument. The worker’s joint condition in
$(\tau,\phi)$ is untouched by any of this.

## 8.6 Off the diagonal

Theorem 4 holds at a start equal to the rest point the evaluated share
induces. Definition 1 holds the start fixed at the incumbent’s. The two
coincide at the incumbent share and nowhere else, so Theorems 5 and 6
buy the curvature of a scalar map and not yet the curvature of the
object Definition 1 grades. That is the whole of what separates a
stationary profile from an equilibrium in the two instruments whose
criteria are proved.

The obstacle looks like a value-function problem and is not one. Under
constant shares the deviator’s payoff is an explicit sum in the object
he values. Write $W_{j,t}$ for that object — the worker’s population
$N_{W,t}$, the capitalist’s aggregate $P_t$ of (O) — so that
$$V_j \;=\; \frac{1}{1-\sigma}\,
   \sum_{t\ge0}\beta^{t}\exp\Big[(1-\sigma)\ln W_{j,t}\Big]. \tag{29}$$
For the worker $\ln N_{W,t}=\ln N_{W,0}+\sum_{u<t}\ell_{W,u}$ with
$\ln N_{W,0}$ a constant, so this is the cumulated log growth of the
previous sections with a fixed offset; for the capitalist $\ln P_t$
carries date zero along with the rest, because his rate moves his
date-zero consumption. Writing (29) in $\ln W_{j,t}$ rather than in
cumulated growth costs nothing and covers both. The start enters (29)
only by fixing which path $W_{j,t}$ is read off. Nothing else about it
is used, which makes the next statement start-blind. Theorem 7
formalises the start-blind convexity that Appendix C.8 left as a checked
conjecture and isolates the cost of its hypothesis.

**Theorem 7 (the fixed-start payoff inherits cumulated curvature).**
*Let Assumptions 1–3 hold. Fix a start $(x_0,\nu_0,z_0)$, a set $s$ of
deviating instruments, and the remaining shares. Let $I$ be a **convex**
set of values of $s$ — an interval when one instrument is varied, and
any convex region when several are — on which the profile is proper, and
let $W_{j,t}(s)$ be as above. If for every $t\ge0$ the log payoff object
$\ln W_{j,t}$ — for the worker, equivalently the cumulated log growth
$\sum_{u<t}\ell_{W,u}$ — is concave on $I$, then $V_j$ is concave on
$I$, strictly if any one term is strictly concave. Hence any critical
point of $V_j$ interior to $I$ is its global maximum on $I$, and so is
any boundary point at which no feasible direction ascends. Concavity of
every $\ell_{j,u}$ separately is sufficient for the worker’s form of the
hypothesis and is not implied by it.*

*Proof.* No step below refers to the dimension of $s$, nor to which
object $W_{j}$ is; only the convexity of $I$ is used, and that only so
that “concave on $I$” means anything. Assumption 3 gives $\sigma>1$, so
$1-\sigma<0$ and $(1-\sigma)\ln W_{j,t}$ is convex on $I$ whenever
$\ln W_{j,t}$ is concave there. The exponential is convex and
increasing, so each summand of (29) is convex; a sum of convex functions
is convex, and properness makes the sum finite, a pointwise limit of
convex functions being convex. The prefactor $1/(1-\sigma)$ is strictly
negative because $\sigma>1$, so $V_j$ is concave. Strictness transfers
through the strictly increasing exponential. The last clause is that a
sum of concave functions is concave and that the converse fails.
$\blacksquare$

Writing (29) without a separated initial condition is what makes the
theorem cover both classes, and the reason is worth one line. The
dynastic version pulled $N_{j,0}^{1-\sigma}$ out in front as a constant,
which is legitimate only when the deviating instrument cannot move the
