displayed contraction then presents $e_t$ as a geometrically weighted
average of increments that are $O(1/s)$, whence $e_t=O(1/t)=O(R_t)$. Fed
back, the same line is $1/R_{t+1}-1/R_t=\nu+O(R_t)$ and
$1/R_t=\nu t+O(\ln t)$: the increments of $w$ are
$1/(\nu t)+O(\ln t/t^{2})$. The two readings are not interchangeable —
the second needs a rate for $e_t$ that only the first supplies.
*Fourth*, that error is summable, so
$w_t=\nu^{-1}\ln t+\text{const}+o(1)$ and the first line carries it to
$h_t=\gamma\theta\alpha(D\nu)^{-1}\ln t+\text{const}+o(1)$. The
coefficient is $\gamma\theta\alpha/[(1-\alpha)\gamma(1-\theta)]$;
$\gamma$ cancels, and what is left is $\mathcal{C}$. $\blacksquare$

Two things are worth separating from the statement. The proof supplies a
**transient** that no fit could: the polynomial law emerges only once
$t$ passes $x_0^{\nu}/(\nu\psi)\,e^{-(1-\alpha)\mathcal{B}/D}$, so an
economy starting with much capital and little provision spends a long
time reading a *lower* exponent than it will settle at — at one such
start $0.639$ against $\mathcal{C}=1$, a figure the same formula returns
to four places. A sweep over starts alone would have recorded a
start-dependence that is not there. And the earlier computation is left
standing, because it is what a reader can run: fitting the exponent over
five parameter vectors matched it to between $8\times10^{-5}$ and
$2\times10^{-2}$, while the increment of $\ln\Pi_t-t\ln(1-\delta)$ per
decade of $t$ — which a polynomial factor holds constant at
$\mathcal{C}\ln10$ and a geometric one does not — matched to
$5\times10^{-5}$ and separated the diagonal from its neighbours by six
orders of magnitude rather than by a threshold.

Nothing in the substitution used $\delta=\delta_G$, and carrying both
rates through it says something about the cases the diagonal is excluded
*from* — one of which Proposition 17 has to assume. With
$H_t=\ln\Pi_t-t\ln g_\varpi$ in place of $h_t$ the first line is
unchanged and the second gains one term:
$$H_{t+1}=(1-D)H_t+\gamma\theta\alpha\,w_t+\mathcal{B}_\varpi , \qquad
  \ln x_t=w_t-H_t+ct , \qquad
  c=\frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{D}\,\ln\frac{1-\delta}{1-\delta_G}=\ln\frac{1-\delta}{g_\varpi} , \tag{44}$$
where $\mathcal{B}_\varpi$ is $\mathcal{B}$ with $\ln g_\varpi$ in place
of $\ln(1-\delta)$, so that the two coincide on the diagonal. The second
form of $c$ is the one to read: it is the sentence above — capital per
worker rises at $(1-\delta)/g_W$ — with the limiting factor put in.
**The whole face is governed by the sign of $c$**, and the three cases
Proposition 15(i) reaches by three separate arguments are its three
signs; $c>0$ is $g_\varpi<1-\delta$, which is $(\varpi)$’s own existence
condition for the ray.

**Proposition 17$''$ (the ray is reached from every start, and at a rate
the primitives name).** *Let Proposition 17$'$’s hypotheses hold except
that $\delta<\delta_G$, both still in $(0,1)$. Then from every start
with $x_0,z_0>0$ the sequence $w_t$ converges, $\ln x_t-ct$ converges,
and $g_{W,t}\to g_\varpi$. In particular $x_t\to\infty$ geometrically at
rate $c$.*

*Proof.* The first line of (44) is the first line of (43), so
$e_{t+1}=(1-D)e_t-(\gamma\theta\alpha/D)(w_{t+1}-w_t)$ holds unchanged
and, the increments being positive and $1-D\in(0,1)$,
$e_t\le\max(e_0,0)$ at every date. The second line turns the increment
into $w_{t+1}-w_t=\ln\big(1+R_te^{(1-\alpha)e_t}e^{-(1-\alpha)ct}\big)$
with $R_t=\psi e^{(1-\alpha)\mathcal{B}_\varpi/D}e^{-\nu w_t}$. Since
$w$ increases, $R_t\le R_0$; since $e_t$ is bounded above,
$e^{(1-\alpha)e_t}\le\bar\varepsilon$; and $c>0$ precisely because
$\delta<\delta_G$. Hence
$w_{t+1}-w_t\le R_0\bar\varepsilon\,e^{-(1-\alpha)ct}$, a **geometric**
bound, so the increments are summable and $w_t\uparrow w_\infty<\infty$.
They therefore vanish, $e_t\to0$ with them,
$H_t\to(\mathcal{B}_\varpi+\gamma\theta\alpha w_\infty)/D$, and the
second line of (44) leaves $\ln x_t=ct+\text{const}+o(1)$.
$\blacksquare$

That the path reaches the ray, or that its public stock stays bounded
away from zero, was the one thing Proposition 17 assumed about a *path*
rather than about primitives; where $\delta<\delta_G$ it is discharged
here, rate included, and where $\delta>\delta_G$ by Proposition 17$''''$
below. What the two propositions share is the reason they differ in
difficulty. Off the diagonal the forcing that drives $w$ decays
geometrically and the argument is three lines. On the diagonal $c=0$,
the same forcing decays only like $1/t$, and everything laborious about
(41) — the Riccati step, the summable error, the transient — is what
that single degradation costs.

Both propositions describe the face once the economy is on it. How the
rest of the box *approaches* it is a separate question, and it turns out
to be governed by the same constant.

**Proposition 17$'''$ (how the rest points approach the face).** *Let
Assumptions 1–4 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$, $\delta,\delta_G\in(0,1)$,
$i\in(0,1)$ and $\tau\in(0,\bar\tau]$, and let $\phi\downarrow0$, so
that $\varpi=\phi\tau\downarrow0$. Write
$(x^\ast,\nu^\ast,z^\ast)(\varpi)$ for the interior rest point Theorem 2
supplies, $g(\varpi)$ for its common factor, and
$\varepsilon=g-(1-\delta)$. Then $g(\varpi)$ falls to
$\max\{1-\delta,\,1-\delta_G\}$, and*

*(i) where $\delta\le\delta_G$: $x^\ast\to\infty$ and $z^\ast\to0$,
with* $$z^\ast \;\asymp\; \varepsilon^{\,\mathcal{C}} , \tag{45}$$
*$\mathcal{C}$ the exponent of* (41)*. Each of the three quantities is
in fact a power of $\varpi$ times a convergent factor, with*
$$\varepsilon \sim L_\varepsilon\,\varpi^{\,p_\varepsilon}, \qquad
  x^\ast \sim L_x\,\varpi^{\,-p_\varepsilon/(1-\alpha)}, \qquad
  z^\ast \sim L_z'\,\varpi^{\,\mathcal{C}p_\varepsilon},$$ *for
constants $L_\bullet\in(0,\infty)$, where*
$$p_\varepsilon \;=\; \frac{(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)}{\alpha} \ \ \text{off the diagonal}, \qquad
  p_\varepsilon \;=\; \frac{(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)}{\alpha\theta+1-\theta} \ \ \text{on it} ,$$
*so that off the diagonal $x^\ast\asymp\varpi^{-(1-\theta)/\alpha}$ and
$z^\ast\asymp\varpi^{\theta}$, while on it
$x^\ast\asymp\varpi^{-(1-\theta)/(\alpha\theta+1-\theta)}$ and
$z^\ast\asymp\varpi^{\alpha\theta/(\alpha\theta+1-\theta)}$ — the
bracket being $(\varpi)$’s own. In particular
$z^\ast/\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}$ converges to a strictly positive
finite limit, which is more than* (45) *asserts;*

*(ii) where $\delta>\delta_G$: $x^\ast$ and $z^\ast$ converge to finite
strictly positive limits.*

*In either case both classes’ discounted payoffs at the induced rest
point converge to finite limits whenever
$\beta<g_{\min}^{\,\max\{\sigma_W,\sigma_C\}-1}$, where
$g_{\min}=\max\{1-\delta,\,1-\delta_G\}$. Where $\beta$ exceeds that
bound, every sufficiently small $\varpi$ is improper — and a family that
is admissible at some $\varpi>0$ therefore leaves the admissible set at
a strictly positive $\varpi$.* ⛔ *The scope clause is not decorative:
“leaves the admissible set” presupposes having been in it, and nothing
here establishes that any member is admissible. A family improper at
every $\varpi>0$ satisfies the hypothesis vacuously and exits nowhere.*

*Proof.* Substituting Theorem 2(ii)’s $x(g)$ and $z(g)$ into
$\ln g=\ln g_W$ and collecting the two $\ln x$ terms gives that
theorem’s own $H$,
$$H(g)=\ln g+\frac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln(g-1+\delta)+\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)
  =\gamma(1-\theta)\ln\varpi+\text{const},$$ the constant depending on
$\varpi$ only through $\kappa_W$ and so only at $O(\varpi)$. $H$ is
strictly increasing on $g>g_{\min}:=\max\{1-\delta,1-\delta_G\}$ and
falls to $-\infty$ at that endpoint, both established there, so as
$\varpi\downarrow0$ the right-hand side falls without bound and $g$
falls to $g_{\min}$.

Which logarithm diverges at the endpoint decides the rest. Where
$\delta<\delta_G$ the endpoint is $1-\delta$ and only the second
diverges, so
$\tfrac{\gamma\alpha}{1-\alpha}\ln\varepsilon=\gamma(1-\theta)\ln\varpi+O(1)$;
where $\delta=\delta_G$ the second and third are one logarithm and their
coefficients add to $\gamma\big[\alpha\theta+1-\theta\big]/(1-\alpha)$,
the bracket being $(\varpi)$’s own. Either way $\varepsilon\to0$, so
$x^\ast=\big[i(1-\tau)\alpha A/\varepsilon\big]^{1/(1-\alpha)}\to\infty$,
and the other inversion,
$\ln z^\ast=\ln\varpi+\alpha\ln x^\ast+\ln(\alpha A)-\ln(g-1+\delta_G)$,
sends $z^\ast\to0$. Writing $p_\bullet$ for the exponent of $\varpi$ in
each quantity, $p_x=-p_\varepsilon/(1-\alpha)$ and
$p_z=1+\alpha p_x-p_{\varepsilon_G}$, with $p_{\varepsilon_G}=0$ off the
diagonal and $p_{\varepsilon_G}=p_\varepsilon$ on it. Both give
$$\frac{p_z}{p_\varepsilon}=\frac{\alpha\theta}{(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)}=\mathcal{C} ,$$
the bracket cancelling on the diagonal and the $\alpha$ off it, which is
$(45)$. Matching the divergent coefficient against $\gamma(1-\theta)$
gives $p_\varepsilon$ itself and not only the ratio: off the diagonal
the divergent coefficient is $\gamma\alpha/(1-\alpha)$, on it the second
and third add to $\gamma(\alpha\theta+1-\theta)/(1-\alpha)$, and
dividing gives the two values displayed in the statement. **The ratio
$(45)$ is the same on both branches while the exponents themselves are
not**, which is why the ratio alone could not show them.

That the powers carry *constants* rather than only orders is the step
$(45)$ leaves on the table. Every term of $H$ other than the diverging
logarithm converges: $\ln g\to\ln g_{\min}$; off the diagonal
$\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(g-1+\delta_G)\to\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(\delta_G-\delta)$,
finite and strictly so; and the constant moves only at $O(\varpi)$. So
the residual after subtracting the divergent term converges rather than
merely staying bounded, and exponentiating a convergent logarithm gives
an asymptotic equivalence with a strictly positive finite constant. The
two inversions then carry that property to $x^\ast$ and $z^\ast$, and
$z^\ast/\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}$ inherits a limit. ⚠ **Bounded is not
convergent, and only the second delivers the constant** — the
distinction is where a reader could stop one step short.

Where $\delta>\delta_G$ the endpoint is $1-\delta_G$ and only the third
logarithm diverges; its coefficient is already the coefficient of
$\ln\varpi$, so $g-1+\delta_G\asymp\varpi$. Then
$g-1+\delta\to\delta-\delta_G>0$, which pins $x^\ast$ at a finite
positive limit, and the ratio $\varpi/(g-1+\delta_G)$ pins $z^\ast$ at
one. For the last sentence, $N_{W,t}=N_{W,0}g^{t}$ at the induced rest
point and, $P$ being homogeneous of degree one in $(K,N_W)$, $g_P=g$
there as well, so (A5)’s two clauses read $\beta g^{1-\sigma_W}<1$ and
$\beta g^{1-\sigma_C}<1$ and Assumption 3’s sums are geometric with
those ratios. Both $\sigma_j$ exceed one and $g$ falls to $g_{\min}$, so
both ratios rise, to $\beta g_{\min}^{1-\sigma_W}$ and
$\beta g_{\min}^{1-\sigma_C}$; since $\delta,\delta_G\in(0,1)$ put
$g_{\min}<1$ strictly, the **larger** curvature carries the smaller
bound and therefore binds. Both are below one exactly on the stated
range and one is above it beyond, where continuity puts the crossing at
a strictly positive $\varpi$. That the crossing is a single point, so
that the family leaves the admissible set once rather than re-entering
it further out, is Proposition 6(iv): each clause of (A5) reads
$g>\beta^{1/(\sigma_j-1)}$ by Proposition 6(iii), each such set of
splits is an interval because $g$ is strictly single-peaked in $\varpi$,
and an intersection of two intervals is an interval. Its lower endpoint
is the exit. $\blacksquare$

In words, the rates say how a withdrawn social wage is absorbed, and the
answer is that it is not absorbed evenly. Provision per worker falls as
$\varpi^{\theta}$ off the diagonal — **more slowly than the instrument
that funds it**, since $\theta<1$ — while capital per worker rises
without bound as $\varpi^{-(1-\theta)/\alpha}$. The economy does not
simply lose the public stock; it converts it, and the conversion rate is
fixed by the two elasticities and by nothing else, with no depreciation
rate and no reproduction scale anywhere in either exponent. On the
diagonal the same withdrawal is gentler on the worker: $\theta$ is
replaced by $\alpha\theta/(\alpha\theta+1-\theta)$, which is strictly
smaller — the difference being
$\theta(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)/(\alpha\theta+1-\theta)$, and vanishing only
as $\alpha\to1$ or as $\theta$ approaches either end. **When the two
stocks depreciate at the same rate, provision survives the withdrawal
strictly better than when capital is the more durable of the two.**

The two cases say something about the economy and not merely about the
algebra. Withdraw the social wage and the rest point does not slide
gently onto the face. Where the public stock decays at least as fast as
capital it runs away — capital per worker without bound, provision per
worker to zero, and the two locked to one another by $\mathcal{C}$, the
very exponent the face carries in time, with the gap $\varepsilon$
standing where $1/t$ stood. Where capital decays faster it does not run
away at all: it settles at a finite configuration carrying a strictly
positive public stock, even though at $\varpi=0$ no interior rest point
exists and the stock is bound for zero. The limit of the rest points is
then not the rest point of the limit, and nothing decides which of the
two happens but the order of the two depreciation rates.

**A second limit fails to commute, and this one sits inside case (i).**
Read as neighbouring cases the two branches invite the error that the
speed of approach off the diagonal tends to the speed on it as $\delta$
rises to $\delta_G$. It does not: $p_\varepsilon$ carries neither
depreciation rate, so it is *constant* at $(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)/\alpha$
on the whole of $\delta<\delta_G$ and drops at the diagonal. Writing
$p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{off}}$ and $p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{diag}}$ for
the proposition’s two values, what survives the drop is the reciprocal,
and it survives exactly:
$$\frac{1}{p_\varepsilon^{\,\mathrm{diag}}}\;-\;\frac{1}{p_\varepsilon^{\,\mathrm{off}}}\;=\;1
\qquad\text{at every }(\alpha,\theta)\in(0,1)^{2} . \tag{46}$$ The
reason is the one Theorem 2$'$(v) already gives for its own bound. Each
$p_\varepsilon$ is $\gamma(1-\theta)$ over the coefficient of the
diverging logarithm; the public-stock term contributes exactly
$\gamma(1-\theta)$ to $(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)$; and on the
diagonal that term joins the divergence instead of staying finite. The
gain in the reciprocal is therefore $\gamma(1-\theta)$ divided by itself
— free of $\gamma$, of both depreciation rates, of the reproduction
scale and of every instrument. At Computation 1’s primitives the two
exponents are $0.812$ and $0.448$, so the discontinuity is not a
rounding matter. The second of them has in fact already been computed
once, under Theorem 2$'$(v): driving $i\to0$ at the knife-edge sends
$(g-1+\delta_G)\mathcal{H}'(g)$ to
$\gamma[1-\theta+\alpha\theta]/(1-\alpha)$ and the share to
$(1-\alpha)(1-\theta)/(1-\theta+\alpha\theta)$, which is
$p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{diag}}$. Two different limits, in two different
sections, reach the same number, and for the same reason: both collapse
the two logarithms into one.

**What jumps is the limit and not the economy.** Differentiating
$\mathcal{H}(g)=\mathcal{P}$ along the locus $\varpi\mapsto g(\varpi)$
gives, at every $\varpi>0$ and not only in the limit,
$$\frac{\mathrm{d}\ln(g-1+\delta_G)}{\mathrm{d}\ln\varpi}
   =\frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{(g-1+\delta_G)\,\mathcal{H}'(g)},
\qquad
 \frac{\mathrm{d}\ln(g-1+\delta)}{\mathrm{d}\ln\varpi}
   =\frac{\gamma(1-\theta)}{(g-1+\delta)\,\mathcal{H}'(g)},$$ up to the
$O(\varpi)$ that $\kappa_W$ carries. The first is Theorem 2$'$(v)’s
growth-side share, and lies in $[0,1)$ for the reason given there. Both
are continuous in every primitive, and this proposition’s three cases
are their three limits: $(0,\,p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{off}})$ below the
diagonal,
$(p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{diag}},\,p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{diag}})$ on it,
and $(1,0)$ above it — the last being case (ii), where the stock’s gap
closes at the rate of the instrument and capital’s does not close at
all.

**So the exponent should not be read off a cell near the diagonal.**
Freezing $\ln g$ at its endpoint, the step the proof already takes,
reduces the locus to the single relation
$$\varepsilon^{\,1/p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{off}}}\big(\varepsilon+\delta_G-\delta\big)
   \;=\;\text{const}\cdot\varpi ,$$ whose two regimes —
$\varepsilon\ll\delta_G-\delta$ and $\varepsilon\gg\delta_G-\delta$ —
carry the proposition’s two exponents. They meet where
$\varepsilon\asymp\delta_G-\delta$, that is at
$$\varpi_\times\;\asymp\;\big(\delta_G-\delta\big)^{\,1/p_\varepsilon^{\mathrm{diag}}} ,$$
so the off-diagonal exponent governs only below a scale that vanishes as
the diagonal is approached, while on $\varpi_\times\ll\varpi\ll1$ — a
window that *opens* as $\delta_G\downarrow\delta$, because its lower end
falls and its upper end does not move — what is visible is the diagonal
exponent. Nothing in Computation 1 is touched: its nearest case-(i) cell
has $\delta_G-\delta=0.12$, where $\varpi_\times$ lies above $\bar\tau$
and the whole instrument box is off-diagonal at all three of its
equilibria, and the crossover enters the box only below a
$\delta_G-\delta$ of about $0.05$ to $0.06$, depending on which of the
three. The distinction is owed to a reader who takes the two branches
for neighbours, not to the computations.

It also settles one half of what the comparison in Proposition 17 needs.
Below the proposition’s joint bound the incumbent’s own payoffs stay
finite along the whole approach and both clauses of (A5) hold at every
member of the family, so an admissible start exists at every $\varpi>0$,
however small. The other half it does not settle: off a rest point $g_P$
and $g_W$ genuinely differ, so properness on the *deviation* path is a
separate condition and is not established here.

One statement about the face itself remains, the counterpart of
Proposition 17$''$ where capital is the less durable of the two stocks,
and it is the easy one: there the path does not run away, it settles,
and from every start.

**Proposition 17$''''$ (the stationary stock is reached from every
start).** *Let Assumptions 1–2 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$, fix
$\tau\in[0,\bar\tau]$ and $i>0$ with $\varpi=0$, and let
$\delta_G<\delta$ — so $\delta_G<1$, while $\delta=1$ is allowed. Write
$\iota=i(1-\tau)>0$. Then the face map — the constant-share transition
at $\varpi=0$ — has exactly one rest point in the open orthant
$x,z\in(0,\infty)$,*
$$x^{\circ}\;=\;\Big(\frac{\iota\alpha A}{\delta-\delta_G}\Big)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},\qquad
  g_W(x^{\circ},z^{\circ})\;=\;1-\delta_G , \tag{47}$$ *the second
equation pinning $z^{\circ}$ because $\theta<1$; and from every start
with $x_0,z_0>0$ the path converges to it: $x_t\to x^{\circ}$,
$z_t\to z^{\circ}$ and $g_{W,t}\to1-\delta_G$. In particular
$\inf_t z_t>0$.*

*Proof.* On the face $\kappa_W$ is constant, so Assumption 2 gives
$g_W=B(\kappa_WA)^{\gamma\theta}x^{\gamma\theta\alpha}z^{\gamma(1-\theta)}$,
the two exponents summing to Lemma 3’s
$D=\gamma[\alpha\theta+1-\theta]\in(0,1)$; the capital law of $(\star)$
divided by $N_{W,t+1}$ gives $x_{t+1}=x_th(x_t)/g_{W,t}$ with
$h(x)=(1-\delta)+\iota\alpha Ax^{\alpha-1}$, and the public law, having
no source, gives $z_{t+1}=(1-\delta_G)z_t/g_{W,t}$. Write $u_t=\ln x_t$
and $m_t=\ln g_{W,t}-\ln(1-\delta_G)$. Since $\gamma(1-\theta)>0$,
$(x,z)\mapsto(u,m)$ is a homeomorphism of the open orthant onto the
plane, and in these coordinates the two laws read
$$u_{t+1}=u_t+N(u_t)-m_t,\qquad m_{t+1}=(1-D)\,m_t+\gamma\theta\alpha\,N(u_t),\qquad
  N(u):=\ln h(e^{u})-\ln(1-\delta_G),$$ the second because
$\ln g_{W,t+1}=\ln g_{W,t}+\gamma\theta\alpha\ln h(x_t)+\gamma(1-\theta)\ln(1-\delta_G)-D\ln g_{W,t}$.

Three facts about $N$ carry the argument. It is continuous and strictly
decreasing, because $h$ is; it rises to $+\infty$ as $u\to-\infty$ and
falls, as $u\to+\infty$, to $\ln(1-\delta)-\ln(1-\delta_G)=:-L$, which
is negative because $\delta>\delta_G$ — and is $-\infty$ at $\delta=1$;
so it has exactly one zero, at $h(x)=1-\delta_G$, which is $x^{\circ}$
of (47). A rest point of the pair has $m=N(u)$ from the first law and
$Dm=\gamma\theta\alpha N(u)$ from the second, hence
$\gamma(1-\theta)N(u)=0$: it is $(u^{\circ},0)$ with
$u^{\circ}=\ln x^{\circ}$, unique, and $m=0$ there is $g_W=1-\delta_G$,
Proposition 2(iii)’s (N$''$) once more.

Order the plane by $(u_1,m_1)\preceq(u_2,m_2)$ when $u_1\le u_2$ and
$m_1\ge m_2$. The transition $T$ preserves it. In the first law
$u\mapsto u+N(u)$ is increasing, its derivative
$1+xh'(x)/h(x)=1-(1-\alpha)\,\iota\alpha Ax^{\alpha-1}/h(x)$ being at
least $\alpha$, and $-m$ rises with the order; in the second $1-D>0$ is
Lemma 3’s $D<1$ — Assumption 2’s $\gamma<1$ in another dress —
$\gamma\theta\alpha>0$, and $N$ falls, so $m_{t+1}$ respects the order
as well. On the curve $m=N(u)$ the transition is explicit,
$(v,N(v))\mapsto(v,(1-\gamma(1-\theta))N(v))$: the first coordinate
stands still and the second is drawn toward zero. So for
$v\le u^{\circ}$, where $N(v)\ge0$, the point $(v,N(v))$ lies below its
own image and below the rest point in the order,
$(v,N(v))\preceq T(v,N(v))$ and $(v,N(v))\preceq(u^{\circ},0)$; for
$v\ge u^{\circ}$ both relations reverse. An order-preserving map carries
$\mathsf{p}\preceq T(\mathsf{p})$ to
$T(\mathsf{p})\preceq T^{2}(\mathsf{p})$ and
$\mathsf{p}\preceq(u^{\circ},0)$ to $T(\mathsf{p})\preceq(u^{\circ},0)$,
so the orbit of $(v,N(v))$ with $v\le u^{\circ}$ is monotone in the
order and trapped between its start and the rest point — $u$
nondecreasing and bounded by $u^{\circ}$, $m$ nonincreasing and bounded
by $0$ — hence convergent, and by continuity of $T$ its limit is a rest
point, the only one there is. The orbit of $(v,N(v))$ with
$v\ge u^{\circ}$ converges to the same point from the other side.

Now take any start $(u_0,m_0)$ with $m_0>-L$. Since $N$ is continuous
and strictly decreasing between the limits named, there is a
$v_1\le\min\{u_0,u^{\circ}\}$ with $N(v_1)\ge m_0$ and a
$v_2\ge\max\{u_0,u^{\circ}\}$ with $N(v_2)\le m_0$, so that
$(v_1,N(v_1))\preceq(u_0,m_0)\preceq(v_2,N(v_2))$; order preservation
keeps $T^{t}(v_1,N(v_1))\preceq(u_t,m_t)\preceq T^{t}(v_2,N(v_2))$ at
every date, both bounds converge to $(u^{\circ},0)$, and $(u_t,m_t)$ is
squeezed to it. What remains is a start with $m_0\le-L$ — a growth
factor no larger than $1-\delta$ — which arises only at $\delta<1$.
Since $N>-L$ everywhere, $m_{t+1}\ge(1-D)m_t-\gamma\theta\alpha L$; with
$d_t=-L-m_t$ this is
$d_{t+1}\le(1-D)d_t-\gamma(1-\theta)L\le d_t-\gamma(1-\theta)L$ while
$d_t\ge0$, and $\gamma(1-\theta)L>0$, so $d_t<0$ — the growth factor
exceeds $1-\delta$ — after finitely many dates, at most
$\lceil d_0/(\gamma(1-\theta)L)\rceil+1$, and the squeeze applies from
there. Convergence of $(u_t,m_t)$ is convergence of $(x_t,z_t)$ through
the homeomorphism, $m_t\to0$ is $g_{W,t}\to1-\delta_G$, and
$z_t\to z^{\circ}>0$ with every $z_t>0$ gives $\inf_tz_t>0$.
$\blacksquare$

The argument is the elementary one for a plane map that preserves an
order, and it uses no linearisation: Theorem 2(iii), which speaks of
interior rest points and so has nothing to say about this one, is never
called, and the rate of approach — which the Jacobian at $(u^{\circ},0)$
would give — is neither needed nor claimed. What the proposition adds to
Proposition 15(i) is the word *every*: the stationary positive stock per
worker was that part’s only rest point on this side of the diagonal, and
is now its only limit. With Proposition 17$''$ where $\delta<\delta_G$
and Proposition 17$'$ on the diagonal, the collapsed face’s path is a
theorem about primitives at every ordering of the two depreciation rates
below $\delta_G=1$ — Proposition 15(i)’s $z_1=0$ covering that corner —
and Proposition 17 carries no hypothesis about a path at all.

The economics of Proposition 17’s crossing is worth separating from its
proof. The collapsed face is the configuration in which the whole
receipt is handed back as cash and nothing is built, and it is not
obviously bad for the worker: he gets more cash, and where capital
outlives the public stock his consumption can grow fast enough to hold
the composite up while provision per worker falls to zero. What defeats
it is patience. The face carries the lowest growth factor the box
allows, so its payoff is the first to blow up as the discount factor
rises, while the funded profile’s growth factor is bounded away from
that floor by construction and its payoff stays finite over the same
range. A sufficiently patient worker is therefore not bought off with
cash — not because provision is worth more to him at the margin, but
because the path that abandons the stock is the one his own discounting
punishes first.

The rate that governs the crossing can be named, even where the crossing
point cannot. On the ray the marginal value of the public source at the
face is a series whose terms grow at
$\big(g_\varpi/(1-\delta_G)\big)^{1/\theta}$ against a discount weight
$\beta g_\varpi^{1-\sigma_W}$, so it diverges exactly at and above
$$\beta_\dagger \;=\; g_\varpi^{\,\sigma_W-1}
  \left(\frac{1-\delta_G}{1-\delta}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{\alpha\theta+1-\theta}} ,$$
a fixed fraction of the ceiling $g_\varpi^{\sigma_W-1}$ whose size
carries no preference parameter at all. An infinite marginal value at a
boundary does not by itself order two payoffs, and that step is not
taken here, so $\beta_\dagger$ is reported as the rate the crossing runs
on rather than as $\beta^{\sharp}$. Below it the ranking is a comparison
of finite numbers that can go either way: at
$(\delta,\delta_G,\theta,\sigma)=(0.10,0.70,0.60,2.5)$ with
$\beta=0.054$, and again at $(0.04,0.35,0.75,3.0)$ with $\beta=0.168$, a
search over $\varpi$ returns the face itself. Those are computed cells,
and what they establish is not a region but the fact that the
restriction to an interval is real rather than an artefact of the
argument.

The joint condition is *not* proved. It requires the date-wise
sufficient hypothesis of Theorem 7, with the same two free dates and
alternating recursion. Its tail criterion is $(\ast\ast)$ rather than
$(\ast)$: it is strictly harder, false at the same cell, and false on
about twice as much of the swept grid. The remaining issue is the
intermediate dates, as for the split. The route remains open in the
product coordinates; it was closed only in $(\tau,\phi)$.

There is one face of the assumption set on which the hypothesis costs
nothing, and Proposition 13(iv) has already half-named it. Assumption 1
admits $\delta=\delta_G=1$, and there the tail criterion is vacuous
because $\mathcal{G}''=0$. That is a statement about the limit. The
stronger one is about every date, it holds jointly rather than in the
split alone, and it does not follow from $\mathcal{G}''=0$: recursion
(30) carries inherited curvature forward with its **sign reversed**, so
nonnegative coefficients have to be established and not read off. At
full depreciation they can be written down.

**Proposition 16 (at full depreciation the hypothesis is a theorem).**
*Let Assumptions 1–3 and 5 hold with $\theta\in(0,1)$ and
$\delta=\delta_G=1$, fix a start with $x_0,z_0>0$ and a rate $i>0$, and
let the worker’s pair range over $T$. With
$m=\gamma[\theta\alpha+(1-\theta)]$ and $\chi=\alpha-m$, at every date*
$$\ell_t \;=\; \mathrm{const}_t \;+\; \mathsf{w}^{\kappa}_t\ln\kappa
  \;+\; \mathsf{w}^{\iota}_t\ln\iota \;+\; \mathsf{w}^{\varpi}_t\ln\varpi , \tag{39}$$
*the constants carrying the start and no instrument, and, for $t\ge1$,*
$$\mathsf{w}^{\kappa}_t = \frac{\gamma\theta\big(1-\alpha+m\chi^{t}\big)}{1-\alpha+m}, \quad
  \mathsf{w}^{\iota}_t = \alpha\Big[\gamma\theta
     + \frac{m(1-\gamma\theta)\big(1-\chi^{t-1}\big)}{1-\alpha+m}\Big], \quad
  \mathsf{w}^{\varpi}_t = \frac{\gamma(1-\theta)\big(1-\alpha+m\chi^{t-1}\big)}{1-\alpha+m},
  \tag{40}$$ *while $\mathsf{w}^{\kappa}_0=\gamma\theta$ and
$\mathsf{w}^{\iota}_0=\mathsf{w}^{\varpi}_0=0$. All three weights are
nonnegative at every date and strictly positive from $t=1$. Hence every
$\ell_t$ is concave on $T$, strictly so for $t\ge1$; $\ln N_{W,t}$ is
concave on $T$ at every $t$; and Corollary 4’s hypothesis holds with no
side condition. Moreover the path converges to an interior rest point
from any such start, so Assumption 4 is not a further restriction here;
and the set on which the profile is proper is itself **convex**, so it
is a legitimate domain for Theorem 7, which asks for one.*

*Proof.* One point of bookkeeping first, since the hypothesis list is
shorter than Proposition 13’s. The only thing borrowed from that
proposition is (30), and (30) is Assumption 2’s $\ln g_W$ with $(\star)$
substituted into it: it is an identity along a path, uses no rest point
and no linearisation, and Assumption 4 does not enter it. Assumption 4
enters only through Definition 1 in Corollary 5, and at full
depreciation its condition (A) reads $g>0$, which Assumption 2 gives.

At $\delta=\delta_G=1$ the two stock numerators lose their survival
terms and become monomials in the state,
$\ln P_u=\ln\iota+\ln(\alpha A)+\alpha\ln x_u$ and
$\ln R_u=\ln\varpi+\ln(\alpha A)+\alpha\ln x_u$. The state then reduces
from three logs to two, and this is where $\theta<1$ earns its place
rather than inheriting it: Assumption 2 makes
$\ell_u=\ln B+\gamma\theta\ln(\kappa A)+\gamma\theta\alpha\ln x_u+\gamma(1-\theta)\ln z_u$,
so
$$\ln z_u \;=\; \frac{\ell_u-\ln B-\gamma\theta\ln(\kappa A)-\gamma\theta\alpha\ln x_u}
                     {\gamma(1-\theta)}$$ recovers the third log from
the first two, and the change of variable is a bijection precisely when
$\gamma(1-\theta)\neq0$. At $\theta=1$ the public stock leaves
reproduction, the coefficient is zero and the reduction is unavailable —
which is the no-social-wage corner of Section 4 and not a case this
proposition speaks to. Substituting into (30), and using that constant
shares make the intercept date-invariant,
$$\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{u+1}\\ \ln x_{u+1}\end{pmatrix} =
  \mathcal{R}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{u}\\ \ln x_{u}\end{pmatrix} + \mathsf{d}, \qquad
  \mathcal{R}=\begin{pmatrix}-m & \alpha m\\ -1 & \alpha\end{pmatrix}
   =\begin{pmatrix}m\\ 1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-1 & \alpha\end{pmatrix},$$
where $\mathsf{d}$’s first entry is
$\mathrm{const}+\gamma\theta\ln\kappa+\gamma\theta\alpha\ln\iota
+\gamma(1-\theta)\ln\varpi$ and its second is $\mathrm{const}+\ln\iota$.
$\mathcal{R}$ is **rank one**: writing $\mathcal{R}=vw^{\top}$ with
$v=(m,1)^{\top}$ and $w^{\top}=(-1,\alpha)$ gives
$\mathcal{R}^{2}=v(w^{\top}v)w^{\top}=\chi\mathcal{R}$ with the scalar
$\chi=w^{\top}v=\alpha-m$, so $\mathcal{R}^{k}=\chi^{k-1}\mathcal{R}$
for every $k\ge1$ by induction, and
$$\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{t}\\ \ln x_{t}\end{pmatrix}
  = \mathcal{R}^{t}\begin{pmatrix}\ell_{0}\\ \ln x_{0}\end{pmatrix}
    + \big(\mathrm{I}+\Sigma_t\mathcal{R}\big)\mathsf{d} ,
  \qquad \Sigma_t=\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\chi^{k-1}=\frac{1-\chi^{t-1}}{1-\chi},$$
which is (39) with the weights (40) once the entries are collected;
$\ell_0=\mathrm{const}
+\gamma\theta\ln\kappa$ supplies $\mathsf{w}^{\kappa}_0=\gamma\theta$
and $\mathsf{w}^{\iota}_0=\mathsf{w}^{\varpi}_0=0$.

For the signs, $1-\chi=1-\alpha+m>0$, so $\Sigma_t\ge0$ whenever
$\chi^{t-1}\le1$, which $|\chi|<1$ gives; and both remaining questions
reduce to a single inequality, since
$$1-m\Sigma_t=\frac{1-\alpha+m\chi^{t-1}}{1-\alpha+m},
\qquad \frac{\mathsf{w}^{\kappa}_t}{\gamma\theta}=\frac{1-\alpha+m\chi^{t}}{1-\alpha+m} .$$
So it suffices that $1-\alpha+m\chi^{k}>0$ at every integer $k\ge0$. If
$\chi\ge0$ this is immediate. If $\chi<0$ then $m>\alpha$ and
$|\chi|=m-\alpha$, and $m<1$ gives $m(m-\alpha)<m-\alpha<1-\alpha$, so
$1-\alpha+m\chi^{k}\ge1-\alpha-m(m-\alpha)>0$. Then
$\mathsf{w}^{\iota}_t\ge\alpha\gamma\theta>0$ for $t\ge1$. The three
drivers $\kappa=(1-\alpha)+\alpha(\tau-\varpi)$, $\iota=i(1-\tau)$ and
$\varpi$ are affine and strictly positive on the interior of $T$, so
(39) is a nonnegative combination of logarithms of positive affine
functions and Lemma 2 applies; the gradients $(\alpha,-\alpha)$,
$(-i,0)$ and $(0,1)$ span $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ and all three weights are
strictly positive from $t=1$, so the concavity is strict there. A sum of
concave functions is concave.

For the last two clauses, $|\chi|<1$ makes $(\ell_t,\ln x_t)$ converge
geometrically, so $\ell_t\to\ell_\infty$ with
$\mathsf{w}^{\kappa}_\infty=\gamma\theta(1-\alpha)/(1-\alpha+m)$,
$\mathsf{w}^{\varpi}_\infty=\gamma(1-\theta)(1-\alpha)/(1-\alpha+m)$ and
$\mathsf{w}^{\iota}_\infty=\alpha[\gamma\theta+m(1-\gamma\theta)/(1-\chi)]$,
all strictly positive. The class ratio then converges too, and for a
reason independent of the instruments: substituting Assumption 2 into
$\nu'=\nu g_C/g_W$ gives $\ln\nu_{t+1}=(1-\gamma)\ln\nu_t+\Theta_t$,
which is Theorem 2(i)’s own recursion with its forcing term $\Theta$
evaluated along the path; $\Theta_t$ is convergent and the map is a
contraction of modulus $1-\gamma<1$. So $x_t,z_t,\nu_t$ all converge to
finite strictly positive limits, the rest point is interior in the sense
of Assumption 4 — whose condition (A) is $g>0$ here — and $g_C$ and
$g_W$ share their limit, so Assumption 5’s two conditions coincide.
Series (29) therefore converges exactly when
$\beta e^{(1-\sigma)\ell_\infty}<1$, that is when
$\ell_\infty>\ln\beta/(\sigma-1)$; and $\ell_\infty$ is a strictly
positive combination of the same three logarithms, hence strictly
concave by Lemma 2, so the proper set is a superlevel set of a strictly
concave function and is convex. $\blacksquare$

**Corollary 5 (one unconditional closure).** *Under Proposition 16’s
hypotheses, a stationary profile evaluated from the rest point it
induces is the worker’s global best reply over his whole instrument box,
and his clause of Definition 1 closes with no hypothesis beyond
Assumptions 1–3 and 5. The best reply is unique, $\widetilde V_W$ being
strictly concave on $T$.*

*Proof.* Proposition 16 supplies Theorem 7’s hypothesis, and the rest is
Corollary 4’s argument unchanged, corner included. Strictness passes
through the strictly increasing exponential in Theorem 7’s proof.
$\blacksquare$

Three things bound what that buys, and none of them is a formality. The
face is a **face**: $\delta=\delta_G=1$ is one corner of the
depreciation rectangle Assumption 1 allows, and the cells of Computation
1 are not on it. Nothing extends by continuity, and the obstruction is
structural rather than technical — with the survival terms restored,
$\ln P_u$ is the logarithm of a sum of two log-affine terms, which is
*convex* in $(\ln x_u,\ln\iota)$ rather than affine, and (30) carries it
with a positive coefficient. That is the term (32) reports, read a date
at a time instead of in the limit, and it is why Computation 9’s
counterexample lives at interior depreciation and could not live here.
Second, the closure is the **worker’s** clause alone. The capitalist’s
rate raises the stock that funds his consumption while lowering the
consumption itself, so his date-wise object carries $\ln(1-i)$ and
$\ln\iota$ with weights this recursion does not sign, and Proposition 16
does not reach him. Third, admissibility is not free at full
depreciation: both stocks are rebuilt from nothing each period, so
Assumption 5 constrains $B$, which Assumption 2 leaves open.

**Computation 11 (the pair, measured).** *At the ten profiles of
Computation 9, with the start each incumbent induces, the per-date
Hessian of $\ell_u$ in $(\tau,\varpi)$ has a largest eigenvalue never
exceeding $1.8\times10^{-7}$ of the matrix norm, at every date $u\le399$
and every cell. That residual is reported rather than rounded to zero,
and it is identified rather than excused: it falls at $u=0$, the one
date where Proposition 15(ii)–(iii) proves the determinant is exactly
zero and a difference quotient must therefore return zero plus error,
and tripling the differencing step multiplies it by $8.97$ to $9.04$ —
the $O(h^{2})$ signature of truncation and not of a violation. The same
nine-point stencil in $(\tau,\phi)$ returns a largest eigenvalue between
$0.70$ and $0.94$ of the matrix norm at all ten: a genuine saddle, six
orders of magnitude away, which is the control that makes the first
number mean something. The cumulated Hessian of $\ln N_{W,t}$ — Theorem
7’s actual hypothesis — is negative semidefinite on the same range, and
the Hessian of $\widetilde V_W$ is negative definite at every one of the
$121$ admissible points of an $11\times11$ grid inside $T$ at each of
three cells, whose endpoints Appendix C.9 states. At the rest point of
each of the ten, criterion $(\ast\ast)$ takes values in $[0.119,0.530]$
against its threshold of $1$ and $(\ast)$ values in $[0.010,0.363]$,
with $(\ast\ast)\ge(\ast)$ at every one — which is a check on the
arithmetic and not a finding, Proposition 15(v) having proved that
ordering.*

*Unlike the elasticity of Computation 10, the tail criterion can be
swept where that computation could not, because $(\ast)$ and
$(\ast\ast)$ are closed-form at a rest point. Two-thirds of the gap
closes by derivation rather than by sweeping: neither $\mathcal{H}$ nor
$\mathcal{P}$ nor $\mathcal{G}'$ nor $\mathcal{G}''$ contains $\beta$ or
$\sigma$, so neither criterion depends on them at all except through the
admissibility gate of Proposition 6(iii). On a grid of $54{,}000$
profiles in the remaining six primitives and the three shares,
$38{,}208$ admissible after that gate ($15{,}792$ excluded improper,
none for Assumption 4), $(\ast\ast)$ **fails at $3{,}851$** and $(\ast)$
at $1{,}955$ — the joint criterion breaking on just under twice as many
cells, and on about a tenth of the admissible grid rather than in a
corner of it: the breaches occur at every one of the five $\gamma$
values and all four $\delta_G$ values swept, with worst values of $6.21$
against $2.76$. That is a refutation and stands as one. The
complementary counts establish nothing about the cells not tested. The
ordering $(\ast\ast)\ge(\ast)$ holds at all $38{,}208$, which is a check
on the implementation and not evidence, Proposition 15(v) having proved
it.*

*That tenth is not spread evenly, and where it sits is the more useful
statement. Across the twelve $(\delta,\delta_G)$ blocks the $(\ast\ast)$
failure rate runs from $0$ to $34.8\%$, and it concentrates in two
disjoint ones — $\delta=0.03$ with $\delta_G\ge0.20$, at $28.4\%$ and
$34.8\%$, and $\delta=0.15$ with $\delta_G=0.02$, at $18.4\%$ — durable
private capital against a fast-decaying public stock, or the reverse.
The other nine blocks breach at $2.3\%$ or less. It falls from $17.8\%$
at $\alpha=0.20$ to $4.1\%$ at $\alpha=0.45$, and from $21.8\%$ at
$\theta=0.3$ to $1.6\%$ at $\theta=0.8$; in $\gamma$ it barely moves at
all, $8.4\%$ to $11.5\%$. Six of the twelve blocks show no breach, but
two of those six carry only $104$ and $5$ admissible cells after the
properness gate and establish nothing. The criterion is nowhere near
vacuous on this grid: $|\mathcal{G}''|$ has median $213$ and minimum
$15.1$, the full-depreciation corner being absent from it by
construction.*

*The measure is also not a constant of the model, which the derivation
above does not say and could not. Because $\beta$ and $\sigma$ enter the
properness gate and nothing else, they cannot move a cell’s value but do
move which cells are counted. Over $\sigma\in\{1.5,2,3\}$ and
$\beta\in\{0.94,0.96,0.98\}$ the admissible count runs from $24{,}006$
to $51{,}351$ and the $(\ast\ast)$ failure rate from $2.1\%$ to
$12.8\%$, the $(\ast)$ rate from $0.01\%$ to $6.9\%$. The worst value
moves too — $6.21$ to $1.47$ — not because any cell changed but because
a tighter gate deletes the cell that attains it. So the tenth reported
above is the $(\sigma,\beta)=(2,0.96)$ figure and should be read as
one.*

*The other criterion behaves differently on the same grid, and the
contrast is the point. Taking $\tau$ to the zero of $\Xi$ and $g$ to the
rest point that zero induces — so that the growth factor is pinned by
the primitives rather than swept freely as in Computation 6 — the
down-crossing criterion (27) is refuted nowhere: it holds at all
$12{,}015$ of the $18{,}000$ cells that close, reaching at most $0.505$
of its threshold — a different grid from the $0.804$ above and not
comparable to it, $g$ being pinned here and free there. That count is
worth what its census says and no more. A third of those cells,
$4{,}001$, sit in the quadrant where $\varepsilon^{\tau}_g$ and the
bracket are both negative and the corner result does not transport, so
the zero failure rate is not the vacuous kind — though all but five of
those cells sit at $\delta=0.03$, so the coverage of that quadrant is
itself narrow. And $(\delta,\delta_G)=(0.15,0.34)$ closes at no cell and
$(0.15,0.20)$ at forty, both emptied by properness, and (27) is untested
there rather than verified. None of it touches the $\sigma=12$ cell
above, where the criterion does fail and continues to.*

*The two clauses of Definition 1 are now closed by two different
hypotheses, and since a mutual best reply needs both to hold **at the
same profile**, it is worth saying where they do. The capitalist’s
clause is closed by concavity of $\ln P_t$ in his rate; the worker’s by
joint concavity of $\ln N_{W,t}$ on $T$ — sufficient routes both, not
the conditions themselves, and Computation 13 measures what the worker’s
route gives away. These are distinct objects in distinct instruments,
neither implying the other, and each is verified — the capitalist’s by
Computation 12, on an interval of rates at the self-consistent cell, the
worker’s by the paragraph above at ten profiles over eight cells. Those
ten sit at $\tau\in\{0.34,0.45\}$ and the capitalist’s half at
$\tau=0.50$, so as reported the two share a cell and not a profile, and
the stencil was run again at the self-consistent profiles themselves.
Computation 12 reports two of them, at $\omega=0$ and at $\omega=0.50$,
and both are covered. Definition 1 fixes the start as well as the
profile, and the start each induces is the one Computation 12 evaluates
from, the two kernels agreeing on it to machine precision. There, at
$\phi=1$ exactly — the face $\varpi=\tau$ of $T$, where the stencil must
be one-sided — the cumulated Hessian of $\ln N_{W,t}$ is negative
semidefinite at every date $u\le399$. Its one zero eigenvalue falls at
$u=0$, where Proposition 15(ii)–(iii) proves the determinant vanishes
identically, and from $u=1$ the matrix is strictly definite, worst
eigenvalue $-3.0\times10^{-3}$ at $\omega=0$ and $-4.2\times10^{-3}$ at
$\omega=0.50$. The Hessian of $\widetilde V_W$ is negative definite
there and at all $121$ points of the same lattice inside $T$ from each
start. So Definition 1 is met there conditionally on a conjunction, not
on either half, and the conjunction holds at those two profiles and is
untested elsewhere. The two halves were not verified equally well, and
the weaker was the capitalist’s: Theorem 7 asks for concavity on an
interval of his rate, and Computation 9 checks the second difference at
the incumbent rate alone, which is concavity at a point. Computation 12
closes that asymmetry on the same terms the worker’s half already met.*

*A curvature certificate is a sufficient route to Definition 1 and not
the condition itself, which compares payoffs. Compared directly — the
worker’s discounted payoff over the whole instrument box and the
capitalist’s over the whole rate set, both from the start the profile
induces and with the other class’s instrument held — the incumbent is
the maximiser at both profiles. The split rests at the corner at every
admissible point sampled; each instrument’s payoff changes the sign of
its derivative exactly once, from positive to negative; and the tax and
the rate return their reported values to within the reporting lattice,
the rate agreeing besides with the closed form (12) to three decimals at
both weights by a route that shares no step with it. Two properties are
worth reporting beside the curvature. The maximiser is stable in the
horizon, identical at eight hundred dates and at twice and half that, so
it is not an artefact of truncation. And it moves by more than twenty
lattice atoms when the start is changed, which is the concept behaving
as declared rather than a fixed point being rediscovered. The region
where $(\ast\ast)$ fails is where a competing maximum would have to
live, and it carries none: sampled at a spacing an order finer than that
region is wide, its best payoff falls short of the incumbent’s by three
orders of magnitude more than the best point anywhere else in the box
does. Like the grids above this can refute and did not; it does not
establish, and nothing is claimed beyond the profiles and points
tested.*

**Computation 12 (the capitalist on an interval).** *Theorem 7’s
hypothesis for the capitalist is concavity in his rate of the cumulated
log of the object he values, and under (O) that object is $\ln P_t$
rather than $\ln N_{C,t}$ — a different function, so the question is
asked again here rather than inherited. On a $41$-point grid of the rate
spanning $[0.10,0.90]$ at the self-consistent cell, the second
difference of $\ln P_t$ in $i$ is strictly negative at every date
$t\le399$ and at every point of that grid, worst value $-1.29$; and
$V_C$ itself is strictly concave there, worst second difference
$-1.8\times10^{3}$. At $\omega=0.50$ the same holds, worst values
$-0.646$ and $-3.8\times10^{2}$. So the conjunction of Section 8.7’s two
hypotheses is verified on a grid on **both** sides at the reported cell,
and remains untested elsewhere.*

*One feature of that grid is worth separating from the verdict, because
it is a consequence of the objective rather than of the cell. All $41$
rates are admissible, where under a dynastic capitalist between $28$ and
$41$ were, the remainder improper. The reason is Assumption 5: at the
rest point each rate induces, his payoff object grows at the worker’s
factor, so his properness clause is the worker’s own read at his
exponent, and the range of rates leaving both payoffs convergent widens
accordingly. The change of objective has removed a source of
inadmissibility from the model rather than relabelled one. This is a
statement about the rest points, not about arbitrary paths: away from
one, $g_P$ and $g_W$ separate and the clause has independent content.*

*Proposition 16 is algebra and is not sampled, but its closed form is
checkable against a path built from the primitive laws, and that check
is worth reporting because it can fail. Over $18$ full-depreciation
cells spanning $\theta\in\{0.30,0.60,0.85\}$,
$\gamma\in\{0.40,0.50,0.70\}$ and two values of $B$, (39)–(40) reproduce
an independently simulated $\ell_t$ path to a worst relative
$1.1\times10^{-14}$ over $200$ dates. The **control** is the same
comparison off the face: at $\delta=\delta_G\in\{0.99,0.90,0.50,0.08\}$
the same closed form misses the simulated path by at least
$9.4\times10^{-2}$, so the first number is a verification and not an
identity restated. Eleven of the eighteen cells are admissible in the
sense of Assumption 5, which is what keeps Proposition 16 from being
vacuous; the seven that are not are improper and reported as such. The
per-date joint Hessian of $\ell_u$ in $(\tau,\varpi)$ is negative
semidefinite at those cells with a largest eigenvalue at most
$1.6\times10^{-7}$ of the matrix norm — the same $O(h^{2})$ truncation
signature Computation 11 identifies, at the same date and for the same
proved reason.*

*The proper set’s convexity is proved and not sampled, but the object it
is proved about — the closed form for $\ell_\infty$ — is checkable
against the simulator’s own admissibility verdict, which shares no step
with it. Over a $24\times24$ grid on $T$ at four values of $B$, the
closed-form gate $\ell_\infty>\ln\beta/(\sigma-1)$ and the simulated
path’s properness agree at $2{,}304$ of $2{,}304$ points, with no case
in which the capitalist’s condition bound where the worker’s did not —
which is what Proposition 16’s convergence argument predicts, the two
growth factors sharing a limit. Four thousand random chords per cell
stay inside the proper set, a check on the implementation rather than
evidence, the convexity having been proved.*

*At the counterexample cell of Computation 9 both criteria fail,
$(\ast\ast)=3.353$ against $(\ast)=2.806$, and everything breaches
earlier for the pair than for the split, as the ordering of the two
criteria requires: the date-wise condition at $u=68$ against $u=87$, and
the cumulated hypothesis at $t=504$ against $t=1190$. That last
comparison is the one worth stating plainly. The split’s counterexample
carries a discount weight of $8.0\times10^{-22}$ and the pair’s carries
$1.2\times10^{-9}$ — still negligible, but thirteen orders of magnitude
less so, and the honest reading is that the joint hypothesis is the more
exposed of the two wherever $(\ast)$ fails at all. Ten profiles and one
grid can refute joint concavity and did not; they do not establish it,
and nothing here is claimed beyond the profiles and dates tested.*

**Computation 13 (the hypothesis and the conclusion, separated at the
same points).** *Theorem 7’s per-date hypothesis is a sufficient route
to the concavity Corollary 4 uses, not the object itself, and the wedge
between the two is measurable, because the worker’s Hessian splits
exactly. On the chart $(\tau,\varpi)$, with weights
$w_t=\beta^{t}N_{W,t}^{1-\sigma}$ and per-date gradients
$a_t=\nabla\ln N_{W,t}$,*
$$\nabla^{2}\widetilde V_W \;=\; \mathsf{M}\;-\;(\sigma-1)\,\mathsf{G}, \qquad
  \mathsf{M}=\sum_t w_t\,\nabla^{2}\ln N_{W,t}, \qquad
  \mathsf{G}=\sum_t w_t\,a_t a_t^{\top}\;\succeq\;0,$$ *so the
aggregator term only helps: $\mathsf{M}$ negative semidefinite — the
cumulated form of the per-date hypothesis — implies the conclusion and
is not implied by it, and where $\mathsf{G}$ is positive definite the
conclusion is exactly the generalized-eigenvalue statement
$\lambda_{\max}(\mathsf{M},\mathsf{G})\le\sigma-1$. This is the split
Section 8.6 reads at a zero of $\Psi^{\rm fix}$ along the one direction
the crossing moves — there in its own chart $(\ln\kappa_W,\varpi)$, the
decomposition being chart-generic — now unrestricted. Measured at
$\sigma=2$, $\beta=0.96$, from the start induced by the incumbent
$(\tau,\phi,i)=(0.50,0.90,0.41)$ at each cell, on a grid crossing
$\gamma\in\{0.5,0.8,0.95,0.99\}$, $\alpha\in\{0.33,0.60,0.88\}$,
$\theta\in\{0.20,0.60,0.90\}$, the depreciation pairs $(0.08,0.05)$ and
$(0.005,0.002)$, five taxes and four splits — $1{,}246$ points evaluated
after the properness and tail gates — $\mathsf{M}$ is indefinite at $78$
of them and $\nabla^{2}\widetilde V_W$ at none: every failure of the
sufficient route lands inside the window $(0,\sigma-1]$, where the
subtracted Gram term absorbs it, with
$\min\big[(\sigma-1)-\lambda_{\max}(\mathsf{M},\mathsf{G})\big]=0.668$
over the grid and the worst point at the near-permanent pair,
$\gamma=0.99$, $\alpha=0.88$, $\theta=0.2$,
$(\delta,\delta_G)=(0.005,0.002)$. A sample refutes and cannot
establish: the census licenses no universal. What it does license is
pointed — on this grid, a failure of the per-date route says nothing
about the worker’s objective, whose curvature question it does not
answer. The pencil does.*

One exponent carries all three of the burdens above, and it is worth
saying so once. Take the worker’s $\sigma_W$, which is the one the
pencil is about. It fixes the *sign* of his felicity, which is what
makes an improper path pay $-\infty$ rather than $+\infty$, so that (A5)
binds by dominance rather than by fiat; below one he would deviate
towards the improper side, and what fails there is the equilibrium
concept rather than the arithmetic of convergence (Section 2). Its
complement $1-\sigma_W$ is the homogeneity degree carrying his state
reduction, and that reduction changes form exactly where the degree
vanishes (Section 2). And $\sigma-1$ is the threshold in the pencil
above, $\sigma$ being that same exponent at the common value Assumption
3 names. The three are one number, and it is not an elasticity read off
consumption data: his payoff object is the size of his dynasty rather
than his consumption, so no such elasticity transfers to $\sigma_W$ at
all. The capitalist’s $\sigma_C$ is not insulated in the same way, and
Section 2 says how far the transfer reaches there; the pencil, whose
$\mathsf{M}$ and $\mathsf{G}$ are built from $\ln N_{W,t}$, does not
involve it.

Read that way the pencil condition is not a regularity condition. The
Gram term is subtracted with weight $\sigma-1$, so the exponent that
keeps the equilibrium concept intact against improper paths is also what
absorbs the curvature the per-date route fails to sign. What supplies
that curvature is durability: where both stocks depreciate fully, every
date’s log growth is a nonnegative combination of logarithms of affine
coefficients and the payoff is concave outright, and it is the survival
terms that turn each such logarithm into a log-sum-exp. So
$\lambda_{\max}(\mathsf{M},\mathsf{G})\le\sigma-1$ asks that the
curvature of the worker’s felicity keep pace with the durability of the
stocks he funds — a condition on the economy rather than on the
analysis. The census above fails to refute it and, being a sample,
establishes nothing.

# 9. Related Literature

The asymmetry of objectives has a lineage of its own, and it is worth
naming because it is older than this paper’s use of it. Putting the
owned stock into the owner’s felicity, rather than valuing it only for
the consumption it later buys, is the wealth-effects specification of
Kurz (1968), revived as the “spirit of capitalism” by Zou (1994) and
taken up by Bakshi and Chen (1996) to price equities. Its usual
motivation is a preference for wealth as such. Here the motivation is
different and narrower: it is what a *class* objective has to look like
if reinvestment is to remain a decision, and the model itself supplies
the boundary. Proposition 5(i) shows the specification cannot be pushed
to its limit — an owner who values only the stock has no reinvestment
margin at all — so the interior of the family is not one choice among
many but the whole of the admissible set. That is a statement this
literature does not make, because in a representative-agent growth model
the consumption term is never in question.

Against the Lancaster (1973) lineage itself, the departure is one
primitive. In Lancaster and in Pohjola (1983) each class maximises its
own consumption stream and nothing else — no capital or wealth term
enters either payoff — and Hoel (1978) extends that analysis to the
whole Pareto set; that shared form does real work there, because it is
what makes the two-class game a game between comparable players. Section
4 records what it costs once reproduction is the objective — the
consumption ratio is identically one and no instrument reaches
distribution — and Section 6 records what replacing it costs, which at
the baseline weight is almost nothing numerically and one primitive
conceptually.

The lineage did not stop at open-loop solutions, and nothing here should
be read as supplying what it already has. Pohjola’s Stackelberg
characterisation was corrected by de Zeeuw (1992), who found a single
open-loop solution in each leadership case and left the objective values
standing. Başar, Haurie and Ricci (1985) replaced commitment to a whole
path with stagewise leadership, and showed that capitalist leadership
weakly Pareto-dominates feedback Nash — no worse for either class,
better only inside the interior of the workers’ reaction function —
while worker leadership collapses back to Nash. Haurie and Pohjola
(1987) took the game to an infinite horizon, characterised feedback Nash
and Pareto solutions, and used the Nash pair as a threat to support an
efficient equilibrium that is subgame perfect. Shimomura (1991) is the
dedicated feedback treatment. Coloma (1999) runs accumulation and income
distribution as the outcome of a dynamic game, and Leong and Huang
(2010) add uncertainty. Infinite horizons and feedback strategies have
therefore been worked in this literature for four decades, and the
departure claimed here is neither of them: it is the reproduction
object, carried in discrete time on a Nash equilibrium of the
constant-share restriction — a narrower strategy class than Markov
feedback, and one this paper does not show is without loss against
state-contingent rules.

This paper extends the size-not-condition benchmark rather than
overturning it. That result is the $\theta=1,\ \phi=0$ corner of this
model (Section 4), and it keeps binding on the reproduction metric even
once a contested social wage is present, since the composite still
equalises. What the social wage changes is not the welfare boundary but
the reach of the instruments: they now move the private-consumption
distribution and the incidence of reproduction funding. The discipline
of the exercise is that the extension must nest the impossibility
exactly, which it does. Lemma 1 states the relation the other way round,
and that direction is the more useful one. It also forecloses a claim it
is tempting to make: holding the reproduction map common does not escape
the compensating-differential verdict on class-specific technologies.
Expanded in private consumption the worker’s map is class-specific, that
verdict applies, and the wedge is not a condition advantage. What the
verdict leaves open is the case where the class-specific factor is not a
technology at all but a stock the contested instrument accumulates, and
that is the whole of the difference here.

The equalisation itself is not new, and no claim is made on it. In the
dynastic models with endogenous fertility that begin with Barro and
Becker (1989), a parent who can add descendants rather than enrich the
ones he has works the extensive margin, and the transfer to each child
comes out independent of wealth; Alvarez (1999) carries the result into
a stochastic setting, and Córdoba, Liu and Ripoll (2016) put the
consequence plainly, that there is no inequality after the original
generation. The phenomenon is theirs. What differs is the primitive that
produces it. There fertility and transfers are both chosen and
equalisation falls out of a quantity–quality optimisation; here
fertility is not a margin at all but a technology that fuses consumption
and dynasty growth, and the ratio is held by the requirement that two
classes grow at one rate in a stationary state. The two results are
cousins rather than nested. This model does not generalise Barro–Becker,
and what it exhibits is asymptotic and holds only at a stationary $\nu$.

Nearer in setting, and outside that family altogether, is Brezis (2001):
two classes, a proletariat and a business elite, both with endogenous
and class-differentiated fertility, in a model built explicitly on that
class structure. She makes the move against altruistic fertility that
this setup also needs — on her framing children are a necessity for
survival rather than a consumption good — so worker fertility there is
constraint-determined, the minimum number the household’s budget will
carry, and it falls as the wage rises. It is the nearest precedent this
paper has found for treating reproduction as a technology rather than a
preference. It cannot contain the result here, and the reason is a
single assumption: the elite population is taken to be negligible, so
total population is worker population and there is no class ratio left
to have a law of motion. She assumes the size of the non-labouring class
away; here that size is the object.

The public-capital growth literature reached the transition first. Once
a public capital stock accumulates out of tax revenue alongside private
capital the model has two state variables and therefore a transition,
and Futagami, Morita and Shibata (1993) showed in exactly that setting
that the welfare-maximising tax is smaller than the growth-maximising
one. The transition factor $\Omega$ is this paper’s version of that
wedge, and the direction of the over-prediction is theirs. What is this
paper’s own is where the public input sits and what the wedge is asked
to do. The stock enters reproduction, not production, so what it moves
is the class composition and the incidence of reproduction funding
rather than the marginal product of capital. The economy is two-class
rather than representative-agent, which is what allows a fiscal split to
be contested at all, and is why the interesting question here is whether
the capitalist funds the social wage. The two-class setting is not
itself a departure: Tavani and Zamparelli (2016) give the public sector
a productive and redistributive role in a two-class growth model. What
separates this one from theirs is what separates it from Futagami,
Morita and Shibata — there the public stock is a production input and
the model is closed by a growth rule, where here it enters reproduction
and each class holds an instrument it chooses against the other. And
$\Omega$ is below one exactly when the worker’s problem is proper, and
rises with the stock’s depreciation — both facts about this model’s own
structure. That the placement is not a relabelling can be checked rather
than asserted: in a minimal comparison, a stock placed in production has
its output elasticity enter the worker’s own fiscal rule, where a
reproduction map’s outer elasticity provably cannot. Barro (1990) is the
flow antecedent and sits further off: there the public input is an
argument of the production function, and a representative agent has no
class composition for the dilution channel of Section 6 to work through.
A unique and stable transition is in any case not something this paper
discovers: Futagami, Morita and Shibata establish one for their
two-stock model. What differs here is the object — a two-class dynamic
game in discrete time rather than a representative-agent growth model,
with margins that hold identically across the admissible parameter space
rather than at a computed point.

The cross-valuation structure is not this paper’s either. Proposition 5
turns on a marginal value one player places on a state belonging to the
other, and that structure is standard: an asymmetric two-player
differential game generically carries own and cross co-states, and Huato
(2005) already runs a modified Keynes–Ramsey rule in which appropriated
output is valued at the cross shadow price and retained output at the
own one. Grossman and Kim (1996) likewise set a structurally asymmetric
pair of dynasties accumulating against each other. What this model adds
is the object the cross-price is taken on: not a wealth stock that can
be seized but an endogenously reproducing population, which is what lets
a reproduction elasticity enter one class’s accumulation rule while
cancelling from the other’s fiscal rules — a channel that requires both
a population that grows out of consumption and a class that does not own
it.

Huato (2005) studies a two-player production-and-appropriation game in
which intertemporal exchange between the players, and hence any
interest-rate or credit channel, is excluded by assumption. The present
framework is class-structural rather than endowment-symmetric and does
not turn on appropriation, but it is compatible with that programme and,
in the credit-augmented extension the design contemplates, reinstates
precisely the channel Huato sets aside. Huato (2005, p. 29) leaves his
own dynamics open between a “self-correcting mechanism … that prevents
initial disparities from exploding” and nonlinearities that “build up
initial small disparities … to the point of creating conditions that
induce distributional conflict.” Put to this chassis the dichotomy
resolves on the self-correcting side, and the correcting mechanism is
not the concavity of an appropriation function but the endogenous
reproduction rate standing in the denominator of every transition. His
is a continuous-time appropriation game and this a discrete-time fiscal
one, so what is settled is the dichotomy on this chassis, not his
conjecture.

**So what.** The mechanisms named above — a preference for wealth as
such, a consumption stream each class maximises, a quantity–quality
trade-off over children, the curvature of an appropriation function —
are all things somebody chooses. The corresponding object here is a
technology, and no one optimises over it. That is what the equalisation
and the displacement following it are entitled to claim: they hold
whatever either class maximises, so no respecification of an objective
touches them. Where the comparable mechanism is a taste — for wealth,
for consumption, for children — that same respecification is precisely
what would dispose of the result. The same choice bounds the claim, and
the bound is not small. What a technology buys in robustness it gives up
in behavioural content: the results that do turn on what the classes
want — the reinvestment margin, and the best responses in the tax and
the split — carry the constant-share restriction with them, and this
paper does not show that restriction is without loss against
state-contingent rules.

# 10. Conclusion

The equalisation boundary is arithmetic, and this paper does not dispute
it. A stationary class ratio means equal dynasty growth; a common,
strictly increasing reproduction map turns equal growth into equal
reproduction; and no instrument appears anywhere in that chain. The
question asked here was whether granting the boundary leaves the
contested instruments inert, as it does at the no-social-wage corner,
where the private gap is identically one whatever the policy.

It does not. Adding one object the boundary’s setup excludes — public
reproduction infrastructure, funded by the tax that is already the
worker’s instrument, entering the worker’s reproduction and not the
capitalist’s — leaves the boundary binding on the reproduction metric
while giving the instruments a distributional object they could not
previously touch. Lemma 1 locates why: what a stationary ratio equalises
is a reproduction index, and the no-social-wage corner is where that
index is private consumption itself. Where the index is a composite, the
private per-capita gap is a closed form in the stationary quantities,
policy-dependent through the public stock, with a constant negative
elasticity along the stationarity locus (Theorem 1). That is the
Speenhamland shape — a public supplement co-moving against the private
claim — with its welfare sign left open, because the social wage here is
capital-funded. Which class holds the larger private claim is a
different question from that displacement. Theorem 1 orders the two
claims by the public stock alone, and the ordering and the displacement
are independent: each holds under composites where the other fails.

A second margin sits alongside that one, and it is not a per-capita
object. At a stationary ratio Lemma 1 equalises the class *condition*;
the relative *size* of the two classes it leaves alone. Theorem 2(i)
makes $\ln\nu$ a contraction whose $\Theta$ is free of $\nu$, so the
stationary ratio is $\ln\nu^\ast=\Theta^\ast/\gamma$ — a closed form in
the instruments and the primitives. Theorem 2$'$ makes that form
explicit, and the capital stock cancels out of it, leaving the
instruments and the growth factor alone; the no-social-wage ratio
Proposition 2(ii) states on the unfunded face turns out to hold across
the whole split range once the worker’s index is private consumption. At
that corner the growth factor drops out too, and with it the whole
technology: relative class size there is a function of the three
instruments and the capital share and of nothing else — not
productivity, not either depreciation rate, and no feature of the
reproduction technology at all, its functional form included, since that
half of the argument is Lemma 1’s and Lemma 1 holds for an arbitrary
common increasing map. All three instruments are signed there. Public
provision destroys both properties at once, returning the technology to
the ratio and reversing signs rather than shading them. Read off it,
reinvestment lowers the capitalist class’s relative size at every rate
where the worker reproduces on private consumption alone, and the
public-input weight is the only channel that can reverse the sign.
Whether it does is settled rather than surveyed: where provision carries
weight, the direction reverses exactly when the public stock depreciates
no faster than capital, or, where it depreciates faster, when the
reproduction technology’s scale clears a threshold this paper gives in
closed form. So the wedge that separates reproduction from private
consumption in Theorem 1 is the wedge that unsettles the direction here.
What equal reproduction forecloses is a difference in condition, not a
difference in number.

Labour is paid its marginal product before the state acts on the
remainder. That one fact, with the two stocks’ own survival rates, puts
a floor under the growth of the workforce that no configuration of tax,
split and reinvestment can breach — at every date, from any interior
start, and along instrument paths that need not be constant, need not be
optimal, and need not be anyone’s best reply (Lemma 3). The floor is a
weighted geometric mean of the two survival factors, carrying the
production and reproduction elasticities and no instrument at all; where
capital outlives the public stock it is not merely a bound but exactly
attained, and attained by the configuration that funds nothing. Two
things follow. The worker’s convergence condition stops being a
restriction on what may be played and becomes a restriction on
parameters — below one number, built from that floor and the curvature
of his felicity, his side of the model is proper whatever anyone does,
and the bound is nearly attained rather than loose (Corollary 6). And
the owner has no counterpart, his own consumption share having infimum
zero on the same box: the class whose reproduction the technology
secures is not the class that owns it. Read beside the marginal
valuation of Section 6, that is the same conclusion reached twice from
opposite directions — once through the owner’s optimisation, and once
without consulting it at all.

That yields the model’s sharpest statement about what fixes those
conditions, and it is a result here rather than a premise. What carries
it is not the reproduction technology: that is one strictly increasing
map, common to both classes, exactly as Assumption 2 states it. What
carries it is the composite the map is applied to — one class can reach
that composite through the public stock and the other cannot. On that
margin, and by that route, the conditions on which class continuation
rests are not fixed by biology or by preference: they are a contested
outcome, built by instruments the two classes set against each other.
Proposition 2(iii) shows how little it takes to end the worker’s line —
the worker’s own split at zero, with the stock fully depreciating, and
no move by the capitalist required.

The dependence is mutual. The public stock ties the worker’s
reproduction to capital income, and the capitalist’s own interior
reinvestment rate rises with the price he puts on the workforce he must
reproduce, the rest of his rate rule held fixed — a closed form in the
primitives, entering his rate directly, and arising because labour is an
input his production needs rather than because he values worker numbers
as such. That the dependence is a partial and not a comparative static
in the primitives is the paper’s own finding: what the rule contains is
the price weighted by the reproduction elasticity, and Section 6 reports
a sweep along which the price falls while the rate rises.

That the social wage is capital-funded is the model’s structure and not
a calibration, and Proposition 2(iii) is what gives it teeth. The public
stock’s law of motion has one source term, a fraction of the capital
share; the wage bill funds none of it. Where $\theta<1$ the stock so
funded is not an amenity. Unfunded, the worker’s dynasty contracts at
exactly the stock’s depreciation rate, and it does so at every tax rate;
where the stock fully depreciates, the dynasty is extinguished in one
step and the payoff is $-\infty$. So the instrument that reaches the
stock on which worker reproduction depends is funded out of the income
of the class the worker contests. Within this instrument set the two are
not separable. At the split’s corner his private claim is exactly the
competitive wage bill, and the whole of his redistributive tax has
become public provision with none of it private income. Nor is the
constraint imposed on him from outside. Proposition 4(iii)’s closed form
puts the entire gap between his optimal tax and full expropriation in
the capital wedge — the public wedge read on the other stock — and the
instrument ceiling $\bar\tau$ does no work at the reported cells. The
scope of that reading is the model’s instrument set: one tax base, one
route to the stock. That an economy affords no other route is not shown
here and is not asserted, and neither is the corner itself a general
fact — Proposition 3(iii) gives it under an explicit inequality between
the tax and the transition wedge, and the sweep returns it at nine of
the ten distinct proper cells and not at the tenth.

Within the constant-share class, the stable long-run configuration is
not a numerical accident. The class ratio follows a first-order process,
the interior rest point is unique, and the local stability bound holds
across the admissible parameter space. At the no-social-wage corner the
statement is sharper still: the reduced system is triangular, the class
ratio and the capital ratio converge from every start, and the public
stock settles behind them exactly when the corner’s growth factor clears
the stock’s depreciation floor — no condition at all where the stock
depreciates at least as fast as capital, and a closed-form floor on net
reinvestment where it does not. The same outer elasticity that keeps
this long-run state stable also supports the fiscal asymmetry. An
interior stationary profile exists under an explicit primitive
condition, with each instrument satisfying its own stationary decision
rule. A second uniqueness sits above that one and is a different object:
not the rest point in the state, but the balanced-growth factor the
profile composes to. Where the two transition wedges are jointly signed
it is unique as well — under one further sign condition in general, and
under the wedge condition alone on an explicit band of the capital
share, the public-input weight and the curvature. Where that holds, the
start-dependence the constant-share class carries is a dependence in the
shares and not in the rate they generate.

The reported equilibrium levels, fiscal corner, rule comparisons, and
leader-follower exercises are computations. They establish what holds at
their reported cells and rule families, not a general financing
relation. What they establish there is worth stating rather than only
bounding: the state-contingent deviation that pays against a fixed
opponent is extinguished by the opponent’s reply, while the widenings
that do beat the constant-share equilibrium require commitment, are not
the deviating class’s own best commitment, and are not equilibria of the
game. The paper’s formal results are stronger at the stationary level
than the computations, but they do not certify a general state-dependent
equilibrium or exclude cycles far from the long-run state.

A stationary profile is not yet a mutual best reply from an arbitrary
declared start. At the rest point it induces, the stationary decision
margins are the full-payoff derivatives and the fiscal split and rate
each satisfy a single-crossing criterion. Moving a deviation off that
rest point requires curvature along the transition path. For the
capitalist, the remaining fixed-start curvature condition holds at
reported cells and fails elsewhere inside the assumptions. For the
worker, the tax condition likewise holds at reported cells and fails
elsewhere inside the assumptions; separate single-crossing in tax and
split does not rule out a saddle, so the joint condition closes when
both stocks fully depreciate and remains open otherwise. The durability
of the funded stocks is the residual formal obstacle. The capitalist’s
valuation of the workforce, by contrast, is signed by two independent
derivations.

The capitalist’s objective makes this a model of contested accumulation
rather than a symmetric dynastic game. Valuing both retained consumption
and owned capital preserves a trade-off, and so does valuing consumption
alone — the baseline weight, where the cost of reinvestment is largest.
Only valuing capital alone removes it: reinvestment has no cost in the
objective and moves to its corner. The interior objective family is
therefore not a calibration choice but the domain on which accumulation
remains a decision.

The natural extensions are organised in Section 11: a broader
reproduction composite, multiple funding sources, intertemporal
exchange, and public influence over accumulation all change the objects
whose incidence the present model isolates.

What the argument is for is a distinction that ordinary distributional
comparisons can miss. A stationary class ratio can coexist with a
private-consumption gap because public provision enters the worker’s
reproduction input. The gap is neither evidence that the worker is
better off nor evidence that the capitalist is worse off: those are
welfare questions outside the model. What is established is narrower and
sharper. When the public stock matters for worker reproduction, the
source of its finance and the private claim it displaces cannot be read
as independent policy objects.

# 11. Future Research: Financing, Provision, and Accumulation

Two stylized facts give the next theory its direction. First, public
social protection combines cash and in-kind benefits; in 2023, cash
benefits were below 60% of social-protection benefits in Finland,
Denmark, and Sweden (Eurostat 2025). Second, China combines improved
access to services with a rebalancing process toward consumption that
remains gradual (World Bank 2026). Neither fact makes either setting an
instance of this game. Together they identify the missing theoretical
margins. The first also carries a caution about measurement rather than
a result: a budget line reporting cash benefits and benefits in kind as
one total, or leaving the base that financed the in-kind half
unidentified, cannot be read against Theorem 1, whose displacement runs
through a stock built from capital income — and at a stationary positive
stock per worker the same receipt paid out as cash leaves worker
reproduction exactly where no tax leaves it (Proposition 2(iii)).

The multi-source finance extension is a Nordic-motivated theory problem.
Replace the single capital-income tax base with several revenue sources
and allow the public stock to coexist with a cash transfer. The question
is whether the private-consumption gap survives when the public stock is
financed partly from labour income, consumption taxes, or social
contributions, and whether the incidence theorem can identify the
contribution of each source separately. The present model provides the
benchmark precisely because it holds those margins fixed.

The public-private accumulation extension is a China-motivated theory
problem. To represent a mixed setting in which public authority affects
both provision and accumulation, the successor model must add a public
or state-directed capital margin rather than treating reinvestment as
wholly private. The question then is not whether the present result
applies to China. It is whether public influence over accumulation
changes the separation between the public stock that sustains
reproduction and the private claim it displaces.

Three further extensions already follow from the present derivations.
The fiscal asymmetry survives an arbitrary increasing outer reproduction
map and an arbitrary composite; a successor should therefore replace
Cobb–Douglas only to study how state-dependent input shares alter the
closed form, not to test whether the mechanism survives. And an
intertemporal-exchange extension should introduce credit or an
interest-rate margin explicitly, because a claim on accumulated capital
changes the worker’s fiscal choice rather than merely adding a financial
label to it.

The third is a separation the closed forms make by what they omit.
Productivity and the capital share are not the same event for the
state’s problem. A rise in $A$ raises the growth factor and moves
neither the split that maximises it nor the rate in the floor beneath
it: $\varpi^{\circ}$ carries $\alpha$, $\theta$ and $\tau$ and nothing
else (Proposition 6(iv)), and $g_\varpi$ is the two survival factors
under exponents in $\alpha$ and $\theta$ alone. Productivity reaches
Lemma 3’s bound only through the level constant $\underline{C}$, which
is capped at one and stops moving once it arrives there. A change in the
capital share reaches both margins. The peak falls at
$-(1-\theta)/\alpha^{2}$, and $g_\varpi$ moves with the weight its
exponents place on capital’s own survival factor — up where capital
wears out more slowly than the public stock, down where it wears out
faster; the comparison of $\delta$ against $\delta_G$ that Theorem 2$'$
already uses for reinvestment returns here on a different margin. So it
is the division of the product between the factors, not the size of the
product, that changes what a growth-maximising state should do with the
receipt and how fast the workforce is guaranteed to grow. What
Assumption 1 cannot supply is the passage between two such economies:
factor shares are constant there, so moving $\alpha$ compares two
technologies rather than tracing a change within one. A successor that
wants the passage needs shares that move, and the pair to carry across
is $\varpi^{\circ}$ and $g_\varpi$ — the state’s growth margin, and the
floor beneath it.

Every extension named here should preserve the paper’s central
discipline. The relevant objects must be kept separate: in-kind
provision, private household claims, funding sources, and accumulation
responses. Neither aggregate public spending nor household consumption
alone identifies their incidence. The task for future theory is to
derive the conditions under which those margins collapse into one
another, or remain distinct.

*A companion Online Technical Appendix carries the proof of the state
reduction, the complete derivation of the envelope conditions with the
two sign lemmas, the computational procedure with its grids, horizons
and admissibility masks, the full sweep tables, the state-contingent
rule censuses behind Computation 2, the robustness comparisons, and the
replication statement.*
